🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete OTCA exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's an observability training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on your 6-cluster lab. Every section is designed to build observability muscle memory through progressive difficulty: theory → diagnostics → hands-on instrumentation.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational knowledge across all 4 OTCA domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Fundamentals of Observability (Q1-Q36), OTel API & SDK (Q37-Q128), OTel Collector (Q129-Q180), Maintaining & Debugging Pipelines (Q181-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper insights. Target: 85%+ correct across all 4 domains.

✅ Daily Study Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 10-15 MCQs from Part 1 + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Re-attempt questions you got wrong. Write down why each distractor is incorrect — this builds exam intuition.
  • Evening (30 min): Review ASCII diagrams and key concepts. Focus on Collector pipeline patterns and SDK configuration.
  • Weekend (2 hours): Full review of the week's topics. Take a timed run of 50 random MCQs. Track your score trend.
  • Day before exam: Review your notes on weak areas. Focus on Domain 2 (API/SDK — 46%) and Domain 3 (Collector — 26%).
  • Exam day morning: Quick review of OTel environment variables, Collector config YAML syntax, and SDK setup patterns. Stay calm — you've got this!

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why are my traces broken across services?" — No context propagation
  • ❌ "My metrics show zeros but the app is running" — Wrong instrument type
  • ❌ "The Collector is crashing and I don't know why" — No health_check or logging exporter
  • ❌ "Logs aren't correlating with traces" — Missing TraceId/SpanId injection
  • ❌ "The p99 latency spiked but I can't find the trace" — No exemplars configured
  • ❌ "Sampling is dropping ALL my interesting traces" — Wrong sampling strategy
  • ❌ "I can't switch between backends without rewriting code" — Vendor lock-in

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Instrument any app with OTel SDK for traces, metrics, and logs confidently
  • ✅ Configure context propagation (W3C TraceContext, Baggage) across services
  • ✅ Design Collector pipelines: Receivers → Processors → Exporters
  • ✅ Debug broken traces using Jaeger/Grafana waterfall analysis
  • ✅ Choose the right metric instrument (Counter/Histogram/Gauge/UpDownCounter)
  • ✅ Set up tail sampling, batch processing, and memory limits for production
  • ✅ Export to ANY backend — instrument once with OTel, switch backends freely

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: The OTCA is a multiple-choice exam (90 min). Read every option before selecting — distractors are designed to be close.
  • ✅ DO: Focus on the API/SDK (46%) and Collector (26%) domains — they make up 72% of your score.
  • ✅ DO: Know semantic conventions cold — http.method, db.system, rpc.service are guaranteed to appear.
  • ✅ DO: Understand the Collector pipeline: Receivers → Processors → Exporters. You'll see YAML config fragments in questions.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 90 seconds on any single question. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Confuse Counter with UpDownCounter, or Histogram with Gauge. Instrument type questions are common traps.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget that OTel is vendor-neutral. Questions testing "which backend does OTel require?" — the answer is NONE.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any question blank. The OTCA has no penalty for wrong answers — ALWAYS guess.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

OTCA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple-choice (online, proctored) | Cost: $250 | Certification Validity: 2 years | Prerequisites: None

4 Official Domains: Fundamentals of Observability (18%), OTel API & SDK (46%), OTel Collector (26%), Maintaining & Debugging Pipelines (10%). The API/SDK and Collector domains make up 72% of your score — focus your study time accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first → Answer easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → The OTCA is multiple-choice — always guess if unsure (no penalty). Focus your study on the heaviest domains: API/SDK (46%) and Collector (26%).

Your 6-cluster lab is documented below for reference — use ksw to switch between clusters when practicing OTel Collector deployment and configuration.

📚 Pre-Learning: OpenTelemetry & Observability FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Observability Mental Model

F.1 What Is Observability & Why Does It Exist? — The "I don't know what's happening in production" problem. Monitoring vs Observability. The Three Pillars: Logs, Metrics, Traces. Real-world analogies and why distributed systems NEED observability.

F.2 OpenTelemetry Architecture — The Big Picture — What IS OpenTelemetry? CNCF incubating project (OpenTracing + OpenCensus merger). Core components: API, SDK, Collector. How a request flows through OTel: App → API → SDK → Exporter → Collector → Backend (Jaeger/Prometheus/Grafana). Signals: Traces, Metrics, Logs, Baggage.

F.3 The Three Signals Deep Dive — Traces (span, trace ID, parent-child, waterfall), Metrics (Counter, Histogram, Gauge, UpDownCounter, temporality), Logs (LogRecord, severity, TraceId/SpanId correlation). How signals complement each other and why you need ALL three.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Django job board (web + API + PostgreSQL + Redis). Where observability matters at each tier. Complete file structure with OTel instrumentation. Dockerfile with multi-stage build + OTel auto-instrumentation agent.

F.5 OpenTelemetry vs Other Observability Tools — OTel vs Jaeger, Prometheus, Fluentd, Datadog, New Relic. The "vendor-neutral" advantage: instrument ONCE, export ANYWHERE. Comparison matrix and CNCF landscape positioning.

If you're new to observability: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs, troubleshooting issues, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete OTCA MCQ Bank — Organized by the 4 official Linux Foundation OTCA exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost.

🔭 Category 1: Fundamentals of Observability (18%)Q1-Q36

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding Observability & OpenTelemetry

This category builds your mental model of what observability IS and why distributed systems like anihpj/jobpost need it. Think of observability like a car's dashboard — gauges (metrics) tell you speed and fuel level, the check-engine light (alerts) tells you something is wrong, and diagnostic logs (traces/logs) help you find the root cause. OpenTelemetry is the CNCF standard that provides this dashboard for your applications — vendor-neutral, community-driven, and the foundation of modern observability.

Q1-Q36 36 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Telemetry DataQ1-Q10

Fundamentals Q1 of 200
What is telemetry data in the context of OpenTelemetry and observability?
A) Data sent by a monitoring tool to a cloud dashboard for visualization only
B) Binary log files stored on disk that record every system call made by an application
C) Signals (traces, metrics, and logs) emitted by a system about its behavior and performance, collected to understand internal state
D) A proprietary format used exclusively by OpenTelemetry to communicate between microservices
✅ Correct Answer: C

Telemetry data refers to the signals emitted by a system about its behavior. In OpenTelemetry, these signals are traces (the journey of a request through your system), metrics (measurements over time like request count or latency), and logs (timestamped records of events). Just like a car emits speed, RPM, and fuel level data to its dashboard, your application emits telemetry data that helps you understand what's happening inside.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Three Telemetry Signals — OpenTelemetry's Foundation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TELEMETRY DATA (Signals)                  │
  ├─────────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────────────┤
  │     TRACES      │     METRICS     │          LOGS           │
  │  "Where & How   │  "How Much &   │  "What Happened at      │
  │   Long?"        │   How Fast?"   │   This Moment?"         │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │  Request Flow:  │  Counters:      │  Timestamped Events:    │
  │  Web → API → DB │  requests/sec   │  [14:32:01] ERROR       │
  │                 │                 │  DB connection failed   │
  │  Spans form a   │  Histograms:    │                         │
  │  waterfall      │  p99 latency    │  Carries TraceId &      │
  │  showing each   │  distribution   │  SpanId for correlation │
  │  service hop    │                 │                         │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │  Example: User clicks "Apply for Job" on anihpj/jobpost    │
  │  Trace: Web (200ms) → API (150ms) → DB Query (45ms) → Done│
  │  Metric: job.applications counter +1, latency 395ms        │
  │  Log:    [INFO] Application submitted for job #1234        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  💡 Telemetry = The "nervous system" of your distributed application

For anihpj/jobpost, when a user submits a job application, all three signals work together: a trace follows the request through Web → API → PostgreSQL → Redis, metrics count how many applications were submitted and measure the p99 latency, and logs record the exact timestamp and details of each step. This is why OpenTelemetry exists — to standardize how all this telemetry data is collected, processed, and exported.

Fundamentals Q2 of 200
What are the Three Pillars of Observability and what question does each answer?
A) Alerts (what broke?), Dashboards (how does it look?), Reports (who is responsible?)
B) Logs (what happened?), Metrics (how much/how fast?), Traces (where and how long did it take?)
C) CPU (how busy?), Memory (how full?), Disk (how much space?)
D) Ingestion (how data enters?), Storage (where data lives?), Query (how data is accessed?)
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Three Pillars of Observability are Logs, Metrics, and Traces. Each answers a fundamentally different question about your system: Logs answer "WHAT happened?" — they are immutable, timestamped records of discrete events (e.g., "User 42 submitted job application #1234"). Metrics answer "HOW MUCH / HOW FAST?" — they are numerical measurements aggregated over time (e.g., "150 job applications per minute, p99 latency of 395ms"). Traces answer "WHERE did it go and HOW LONG did each step take?" — they show the complete journey of a single request through all your services (e.g., Web → API → DB → Redis → Response).

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Three Pillars — Each Answers A Different Question
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    THE THREE PILLARS OF OBSERVABILITY             │
  │                                                                  │
  │   🔴 LOGS                🔵 METRICS             🟢 TRACES        │
  │   "What happened?"       "How much/fast?"       "Where & how      │
  │                                                  long?"          │
  │   ─────────────────      ─────────────────      ─────────────────│
  │   Timestamped events     Aggregated numbers     Request journeys │
  │                                                                  │
  │   Example:               Example:               Example:         │
  │   [14:32:01] ERROR       job.applications: 150  Web     200ms    │
  │   DB connection failed   http.latency.p99:395ms   │               │
  │                          db.pool.active: 8        ▼               │
  │   Like:                  Like:                  API     150ms     │
  │   A security camera      Your car's speedometer   │               │
  │   recording every event  showing current speed    ▼               │
  │                                                   DB      45ms    │
  │   Immutable record       Real-time measurement   │               │
  │   of WHAT occurred       of HOW MUCH             ▼               │
  │                                                   Redis    5ms    │
  │   Best for:              Best for:              │               │
  │   Debugging specific     Identifying trends     ▼               │
  │   errors after they      and anomalies across   Response  ← Done │
  │   happen                 your entire system                     │
  │                                                   Best for:      │
  │                                                   Finding WHERE  │
  │                                                   latency lives  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  💡 You need ALL THREE — logs tell you WHAT broke, metrics tell you HOW OFTEN,
     and traces tell you EXACTLY WHERE in the request path the problem lives.

In the anihpj/jobpost application, when a user complains "my job application didn't go through", you use all three: logs show the error message at the exact timestamp, metrics reveal that error rate spiked 500% in the last 5 minutes affecting 200 users, and a trace pinpoints that the bottleneck was a slow PostgreSQL query taking 3.2 seconds instead of the usual 45ms. Without all three, you'd be debugging blind.

Fundamentals Q3 of 200
What is the key difference between Monitoring and Observability?
A) Monitoring answers known questions about known failures; Observability lets you explore unknown-unknowns and ask NEW questions without deploying new code
B) Monitoring uses dashboards; Observability uses command-line tools only
C) Monitoring is for small applications; Observability is only needed for microservices with 50+ services
D) They are the same thing — Observability is just a newer marketing term for Monitoring
✅ Correct Answer: A

Monitoring is about tracking KNOWN failure modes — you set up dashboards and alerts for things you've seen before (CPU > 90%, disk full, 500 errors). It answers "is this KNOWN thing happening right now?" Observability goes further — it lets you ask NEW questions about your system that you DIDN'T anticipate when you wrote the code. When a novel failure occurs (a "unknown-unknown"), observability gives you the telemetry data to diagnose it without deploying new instrumentation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Monitoring vs Observability — Known-Knowns vs Unknown-Unknowns
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              MONITORING                     OBSERVABILITY        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔴 Known-Knowns                      🟢 Unknown-Unknowns       │
  │  "I know CPU spiking is bad"          "Something is wrong but    │
  │                                        I don't know what yet"   │
  │  ─────────────────────                ─────────────────────     │
  │  Pre-defined dashboards               Ad-hoc exploration         │
  │  Threshold-based alerts               Correlation across signals │
  │  "Alert me IF cpu > 90%"              "Show me WHY p99 spiked"  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Example:                             Example:                   │
  │  Alert: "API returning 500s"          Customer: "Job search is   │
  │  → You know to check the DB           suddenly slow"             │
  │                                       → No alert fired           │
  │  Reactive to KNOWN patterns           → Query traces: discover   │
  │                                        that a new Redis key      │
  │  Works well for:                      pattern causes cache misses│
  │  Stable, predictable systems          → Fix the root cause       │
  │                                       → You discovered something │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────  you NEVER monitored for   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Monitoring = "Did this KNOWN thing happen?"                  │
  │     Observability = "WHAT is happening and WHY?"                 │
  │     You need BOTH — monitoring catches known issues fast,        │
  │     observability lets you debug the ones you never expected.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: your monitoring alerts you that the API error rate jumped to 15% (a KNOWN pattern you set an alert for). But observability is what lets you trace a specific failed request, discover that it ONLY affects employers searching with a specific salary range, and find that a recent database index change caused the regression. Without observability, you'd know something is wrong but never find the root cause.

Fundamentals Q4 of 200
Why do distributed systems (like microservices) fundamentally NEED telemetry data more than monoliths?
A) Distributed systems use more CPU, so they need metrics to track resource usage
B) Monoliths are inherently more stable so they don't produce any errors worth tracking
C) In a monolith, a single stack trace shows the full request path; in a distributed system, a request hops across multiple services, and without telemetry you cannot see the end-to-end journey or identify which service caused a failure
D) Telemetry is only needed for compliance and auditing, which distributed systems require more of
✅ Correct Answer: C

In a monolithic application, when something breaks, you get a single stack trace that shows the entire failure path — all the code runs in ONE process. In a distributed system, a single user request may touch 5, 10, or 50 different services, each running on different machines with different logs. Without telemetry (specifically distributed tracing), you have NO way to follow that request end-to-end. You'd have to manually correlate timestamps across dozens of log files — which is practically impossible at scale.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Monolith vs Distributed — Why Telemetry Becomes Essential
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MONOLITH (Single Process)          DISTRIBUTED (Microservices) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                     ┌─────────┐                │
  │  User Request ──────────────────►   │  Web    │ ────────────── │
  │         │                           └────┬────┘                │
  │         ▼                                │                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌─────▼──────┐              │
  │  │   SINGLE PROCESS     │          │  API       │              │
  │  │                      │          └─────┬──────┘              │
  │  │  Function A()  ←     │                │                     │
  │  │  Function B()  ←     │      ┌─────────┼─────────┐           │
  │  │  Function C()  ←     │      │         │         │           │
  │  │  DB Query      ←     │  ┌───▼──┐  ┌───▼──┐  ┌───▼──┐       │
  │  │                      │  │  DB  │  │ Redis│  │Queue │       │
  │  │  ONE stack trace     │  └──────┘  └──────┘  └──────┘       │
  │  │  shows everything    │                                      │
  │  └──────────────────────┘  Without a TRACE, you have NO IDEA   │
  │                            which service caused the slowdown!   │
  │  Debug: Open the log,      Debug: Correlate 5+ log files,      │
  │  find the stack trace,     find the trace ID, follow spans     │
  │  fix the line of code      across EVERY service hop            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ❌ Telemetry is "nice      ✅ Telemetry is ESSENTIAL —         │
  │     to have"                  you CANNOT debug without it       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  💡 Microservices = More failure modes × More services × Network = Telemetry is REQUIRED

For anihpj/jobpost: a job application request touches Web (Django template) → API (Django REST) → PostgreSQL (save application) → Redis (update cache) → Celery (send email). If the request is slow, is it the Web tier rendering the template? The API serializing JSON? A slow DB query? Redis eviction? Without distributed tracing, you'd spend hours checking each service individually. With a trace, you see the waterfall instantly: "Ah, the DB query took 3.2 seconds — that's the bottleneck."

Fundamentals Q5 of 200
Why does OpenTelemetry emphasize STRUCTURED telemetry data over unstructured formats?
A) Structured data compresses better, reducing storage costs by 80%
B) Unstructured data violates GDPR and other privacy regulations
C) Structured data is required by the OTLP protocol specification
D) Structure enables correlation — when logs carry TraceId and metrics share attribute names, you can seamlessly navigate from a metric spike → to the trace → to the exact log line, connecting all three signals into one debugging workflow
✅ Correct Answer: D

Structure is what makes the three pillars work TOGETHER instead of in isolation. When telemetry is structured — logs have a TraceId field, metrics use consistent attribute names like http.method, traces follow semantic conventions — you can correlate across signals seamlessly. An unstructured log that says "Error connecting to database" is useful. A structured log that says {"severity":"ERROR","message":"DB connection failed","TraceId":"abc123","SpanId":"def456","db.system":"postgresql"} lets you jump directly from that log line to the full distributed trace showing exactly which query failed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Unstructured vs Structured — Why Correlation Changes Everything
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UNSTRUCTURED (Legacy)                STRUCTURED (OpenTelemetry)│
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Log: "Error: timeout connecting     Log: {"timestamp":"14:32", │
  │  to database at 14:32:01"            "severity":"ERROR",        │
  │                                       "message":"DB timeout",   │
  │  Metric: app.errors = 47             "TraceId":"abc123",        │
  │                                       "SpanId":"def456",        │
  │  Trace: (can't find it — no          "db.system":"postgresql"}  │
  │  connection between the log                                   │
  │  and any trace)                       Metric: http.errors{      │
  │                                        method="GET",            │
  │  🔴 CANNOT connect them              route="/api/jobs",        │
  │     — three isolated islands          status="500"} = 47        │
  │                                                                 │
  │                                       Trace: TraceId=abc123     │
  │                                       └─ Span: DB Query (3.2s) │
  │                                          └─ Event: "timeout"   │
  │                                                                 │
  │                                       🟢 ONE click navigates    │
  │                                       Metric → Trace → Log      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  DEBUG FLOW WITH STRUCTURED TELEMETRY:                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  1. 🔵 Grafana dashboard: p99 latency spiked at 14:32           │
  │  2. 🟢 Click the spike → exemplar links to TraceId "abc123"     │
  │  3. 🟣 Jaeger trace waterfall: DB query span took 3.2 seconds!  │
  │  4. 🔴 Click the span → see correlated log: "timeout connecting │
  │     to PostgreSQL at 14:32:01"                                   │
  │  5. ✅ Root cause found in SECONDS, not hours                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  💡 Structure = Correlation = Speed. Without it, you're searching blind.

For anihpj/jobpost, this means every Django log line automatically includes the current TraceId and SpanId. When an employer reports "job search is broken," you don't grep through raw text logs — you find the trace in Jaeger, see the slow span, and click through to the exact structured log line that says "PostgreSQL query timeout: SELECT * FROM jobs WHERE salary > 100000". That's the power of structured telemetry.

Fundamentals Q6 of 200
In the anihpj/jobpost application, a user submits a job application. Which sequence of telemetry signals correctly traces this request through the system?
A) Metrics counter increments → Log written → Trace created (must happen in this order)
B) Web span (Django template render) → API span (REST endpoint) → DB span (PostgreSQL INSERT) → Redis span (cache update) → Celery span (email task) — all linked by one Trace ID
C) Each service writes its own independent trace, and you manually search by timestamp to correlate them
D) Only the API tier needs instrumentation since it's the entry point; the database and cache are automatically traced
✅ Correct Answer: B

When a user submits a job application on anihpj/jobpost, the request creates a single distributed trace — one Trace ID links ALL spans across every service. The trace follows the exact request path: Web tier (Django renders the form and accepts the POST) → API tier (Django REST Framework validates the data and processes the application) → PostgreSQL (INSERTs the application record) → Redis (invalidates the job listing cache so the new application count is reflected) → Celery (enqueues a task to send a confirmation email to the applicant). Every one of these operations becomes a SPAN, and all spans share the SAME Trace ID.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — A Complete Trace for "Submit Job Application"
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE ID: a1b2c3d4e5f6 (Shared by ALL spans below)             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  User clicks "Apply for Job" on anihpj/jobpost                  │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ▼                                                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Span 1: WEB — Django Template Render  [200ms]            │   │
  │  │   http.method=POST  http.route=/jobs/42/apply            │   │
  │  │   http.status_code=200                                    │   │
  │  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                           │                                      │
  │                           ▼                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Span 2: API — Django REST Endpoint  [150ms]               │   │
  │  │   http.method=POST  http.route=/api/jobs/42/applications  │   │
  │  │   job.id=42  user.id=789                                  │   │
  │  └──────┬───────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────────┘   │
  │         │               │               │                        │
  │         ▼               ▼               ▼                        │
  │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Span 3: DB   │ │ Span 4: Redis│ │ Span 5: Celery       │     │
  │  │ PostgreSQL   │ │ Cache Update │ │ Email Task Enqueue   │     │
  │  │ INSERT INTO  │ │ DEL job:42:  │ │ task=send_confirmation│    │
  │  │ applications │ │ applications │ │ email applicant #789  │     │
  │  │ [45ms]       │ │ [5ms]        │ │ [10ms]               │     │
  │  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │

  │                                                                  │
  │  📝 LOGS correlated (all carry TraceId):                         │
  │     [INFO] POST /jobs/42/apply — 200 OK                          │
  │     [INFO] Application #5678 created for job #42, user #789     │
  │     [DEBUG] Cache key job:42:applications invalidated            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  💡 One Trace ID = Complete visibility across 5 services in ONE view

This is the power of OpenTelemetry on anihpj/jobpost: with proper instrumentation, a single user action creates ONE unified trace. The platform team can immediately see that the total request took 395ms, with the Web tier consuming 200ms (rendering the template) and the DB query taking only 45ms. If the latency ever spikes, the trace waterfall instantly reveals WHICH span is the bottleneck — no manual log correlation needed.

Fundamentals Q7 of 200
What is "cardinality" in telemetry, and why do high-cardinality dimensions matter for debugging but pose a challenge for metrics systems?
A) Cardinality is the number of unique values for an attribute — high cardinality (e.g., user ID) gives precise debugging power but can overwhelm metrics storage; low cardinality (e.g., HTTP method) is safe for metrics but less specific
B) Cardinality refers to the number of services in your system — more services means higher cardinality which always improves performance
C) Cardinality is a measure of trace duration — high cardinality traces are slower and should be sampled more aggressively
D) Cardinality only applies to logs; metrics and traces do not have cardinality concerns
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cardinality refers to the number of UNIQUE values an attribute can take. Low cardinality attributes like http.method (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE — only ~5 values) or http.status_code (~20 common values) are safe and efficient for metrics. High cardinality attributes like user.id (millions of unique values), trace.id, or session.id give incredible debugging precision — you can filter to EXACTLY one user's requests — but they create a separate time series for every unique value, which can explode your metrics storage and crash Prometheus.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Low vs High Cardinality — The Trade-Off
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LOW CARDINALITY (Safe for Metrics)   HIGH CARDINALITY (Use     │
  │                                        Sparingly on Metrics)    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  http.method = {GET, POST, PUT,       user.id = {1, 2, 3, ...,  │
  │                DELETE} (4 values)            5,000,000 values}   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  http.status_code = {200, 201,        trace.id = {unique for     │
  │     301, 400, 401, 403, 404, 500}     EVERY request}            │
  │     (~20 values)                                                 │
  │                                      session.id = {millions}     │
  │  cloud.region = {us-east-1,                                        │
  │     eu-west-1, ap-south-1}          email.address = {unique      │
  │     (~20 values)                     per user}                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Safe on metrics dashboards       ⚠️  Use on SPANS & LOGS    │
  │  ✅ Create few time series           ⚠️  NEVER as metric attrs   │
  │  ✅ Aggregate efficiently            ✅  Perfect for trace search │
  │                                       ✅  "Show me user #789's    │
  │  Example:                                slow requests"          │
  │  http.requests{method="GET",                                        │
  │    status="200"} = 15,000            ──────────────────────────  │
  │  → 1 time series                     💡 RULE: High cardinality   │
  │                                         on spans/logs = GOOD     │
  │                                         High cardinality on      │
  │                                         metrics = BAD (explodes) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: adding user.id as a metric attribute would create a separate time series for every job seeker — with 500,000 users, that's 500,000 time series for a single metric. Instead, put user.id as a SPAN attribute on traces — it's searchable in Jaeger ("show me all traces for user #789") but doesn't blow up your metrics. Use low-cardinality attributes like job.category (tech, healthcare, finance — ~20 values) on metrics for dashboards.

Fundamentals Q8 of 200
What are the main cost factors of telemetry, and how does sampling help manage them?
A) Telemetry is free — OpenTelemetry is open source, so there is no cost to consider
B) The only cost is the Collector's CPU usage; storage and network are negligible
C) Telemetry costs come from data volume (network bandwidth), storage (retention of traces/metrics/logs), and processing (Collector CPU/memory); sampling reduces costs by keeping only a statistically representative subset of traces while preserving key signals like errors
D) Sampling increases costs because you need extra infrastructure to run the sampling algorithms
✅ Correct Answer: C

Telemetry is NOT free — while OpenTelemetry itself is open source, the infrastructure to transport, process, store, and query telemetry data has real costs. The three main cost drivers are: Network bandwidth (sending every span/metric/log from your app to the Collector to the backend), Storage (keeping traces for 7-30 days, metrics for months, logs potentially for years), and Processing (Collector CPU/memory for batching, transforming, and exporting data). Sampling is the primary cost-control mechanism — by keeping only a representative subset of traces (e.g., 10% of normal requests but 100% of errors), you dramatically reduce volume while preserving debugging capability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Cost Equation — Volume × Retention × Cardinality
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TELEMETRY COST FACTORS                        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                  │
  │  📡 VOLUME (How much data?)          💾 RETENTION (How long?)    │
  │  ─────────────────────               ─────────────────────       │
  │  • 1,000 requests/sec × 5 spans     • Traces: 7-30 days          │
  │    each = 5,000 spans/sec            • Metrics: 1-13 months       │
  │  • 5,000 spans/sec × 500 bytes      • Logs: 30 days - years      │
  │    = 2.5 MB/sec = 216 GB/day                                     │
  │                                      💰 COST = Volume × Retention│
  │  ⚙️ PROCESSING (CPU/Memory)                                     │
  │  ─────────────────────                                           │
  │  • Batch processor: memory for                                   │
  │    buffering                                                     │
  │  • Transform processor: CPU for                                  │
  │    OTTL operations                                               │
  │  • Tail sampling: memory to hold                                 │
  │    spans until trace completes                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SAMPLING STRATEGIES TO CONTROL COST:                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔴 Head Sampling (at SDK):          🟢 Tail Sampling (at        │
  │     • Decide immediately               Collector):               │
  │     • "Keep 10% of all traces"         • Decide AFTER trace ends │
  │     • Simple, low overhead             • "Keep traces with ERROR │
  │     • ❌ May drop important traces       status OR duration > 1s"│
  │                                        • Smarter, higher overhead│
  │                                        • ✅ Never drops errors   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Best practice: Head-sample 10% + Tail-sample ALL errors      │
  │     = 90% cost reduction + 0% missed errors                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost at scale: with 10,000 job seekers per hour, each generating ~5 spans (Web → API → DB → Redis → Celery), that's 50,000 spans/hour. Over 30 days, that's 36 million spans. With head sampling at 10%, you store 3.6 million spans — manageable. But you configure tail sampling to ALWAYS keep traces where http.status_code=500 or span.duration > 1 second, so you never miss the important ones. Result: 90% cost reduction with zero loss of debugging power for failures.

Fundamentals Q9 of 200
What are the two main telemetry collection models, and how does OpenTelemetry support both?
A) Synchronous (real-time) and Asynchronous (batch) — OTel only supports synchronous
B) Push model (SDK actively sends data to the Collector via OTLP) and Pull model (Prometheus scrapes /metrics endpoints exposed by the SDK or Collector) — OTel supports both, making it compatible with Prometheus and other pull-based systems
C) Agent-based (requires a local daemon) and Agentless (direct to cloud) — OTel only supports agent-based
D) Streaming (continuous data flow) and Polling (periodic checks) — OTel only supports streaming via gRPC
✅ Correct Answer: B

OpenTelemetry supports BOTH collection models. The Push model is the default OTel approach: the SDK in your application actively sends telemetry data to a Collector or backend via OTLP (gRPC or HTTP). This is how traces and logs typically flow — the app pushes spans to the Collector as they complete. The Pull model (popularized by Prometheus) works differently: the SDK or Collector exposes a /metrics HTTP endpoint, and Prometheus periodically scrapes it. OTel supports this via the Prometheus Exporter in the SDK and the Prometheus Receiver in the Collector — making OTel fully compatible with existing Prometheus setups.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Push vs Pull — How Telemetry Reaches Your Backend
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUSH MODEL (OTel Default)          PULL MODEL (Prometheus)     │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────┐    OTLP/gRPC     ┌───────────┐                        │
  │  │ App │ ───────────────► │ Collector │ ───► Jaeger/Prom/...   │
  │  │ SDK │   "Here's a      │           │                        │
  │  └─────┘    span!"        └───────────┘                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  • App decides WHEN to     ┌──────────┐    HTTP GET /metrics    │
  │    send data               │Prometheus│ ◄───────────────────    │
  │  • Best for: traces, logs  └──────────┘                         │
  │  • Low latency — data              │                             │
  │    arrives immediately             │ "Give me your metrics!"     │
  │                              ┌─────▼──────┐                     │
  │                              │ Collector  │                     │
  │                              │ /metrics   │                     │
  │                              └────────────┘                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │                              • Prometheus decides WHEN to scrape │
  │                              • Best for: metrics (Prometheus)    │
  │                              • Pull interval: typically 15-60s   │
  │                                                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  HOW OTEL SUPPORTS BOTH:                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Push: SDK → OTLP Exporter → Collector (gRPC :4317 / HTTP :4318)│
  │  Pull: SDK → Prometheus Exporter → exposes :9464/metrics         │
  │        OR Collector → Prometheus Receiver → scrapes your apps    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 OTel is the universal adapter — push traces, pull metrics,  │
  │     stream logs. All three models in ONE standard.               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django application uses the PUSH model for traces — every HTTP request span is immediately pushed to the OTel Collector via OTLP/gRPC on port 4317. For metrics, the team already has Prometheus running, so the Collector exposes a /metrics endpoint that Prometheus scrapes every 30 seconds. This hybrid approach gives the best of both worlds: real-time traces for debugging individual requests, and Prometheus-compatible metrics that integrate with existing dashboards and alerting rules.

Fundamentals Q10 of 200
What is OTLP (OpenTelemetry Protocol) and why is it central to the OpenTelemetry ecosystem?
A) OTLP is a replacement for HTTP — it's a new network protocol that OpenTelemetry invented for faster data transfer
B) OTLP is the query language used to search traces in Jaeger, similar to SQL for databases
C) OTLP is only used between the Collector and backends; applications use different protocols to send data to the Collector
D) OTLP is the universal telemetry wire protocol that defines how traces, metrics, and logs are serialized and transmitted — it supports both gRPC (port 4317) and HTTP (port 4318) and is used at every hop: SDK → Collector, Collector → Collector, Collector → Backend
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTLP (OpenTelemetry Protocol) is the standard wire protocol that defines exactly how telemetry data — traces, metrics, and logs — is serialized into a binary format (Protocol Buffers) or JSON and transmitted over the network. It's the "universal language" of OpenTelemetry: every OTel SDK speaks OTLP, every Collector understands OTLP, and increasingly every backend (Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana, Datadog, etc.) accepts OTLP natively. It operates over two transports: gRPC (high-performance binary, default port 4317) and HTTP/1.1 (firewall-friendly, default port 4318).

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTLP — The Universal Telemetry Protocol at Every Hop
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     OTLP — ONE PROTOCOL, EVERY HOP               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐    OTLP/gRPC     ┌───────────┐    OTLP/gRPC       │
  │  │ Python   │ ───────────────► │ Collector │ ───────────────►   │
  │  │ SDK      │   :4317          │ (Agent)   │   :4317            │
  │  └──────────┘                  └─────┬─────┘                    │
  │                                      │                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐    OTLP/HTTP          │    OTLP/HTTP              │
  │  │ Node.js  │ ───────────────►      └──────────────►  Jaeger    │
  │  │ SDK      │   :4318                              ►  Prometheus│
  │  └──────────┘                                      ►  Grafana   │
  │                                                    ►  Datadog   │
  │  ┌──────────┐    OTLP/gRPC                         ►  Any OTLP  │
  │  │ Java     │ ───────────────►                      ►  backend  │
  │  │ Agent    │   :4317                                             │
  │  └──────────┘                                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  OTLP SPECIFICS:                                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  📦 Format:    Protocol Buffers (binary) or JSON                 │
  │  🚀 Transport: gRPC (port 4317) — streaming, high throughput     │
  │                HTTP/1.1 (port 4318) — firewalls, load balancers  │
  │  📊 Signals:   Traces, Metrics, Logs — ALL in one protocol       │
  │  🔄 Flow:      Request/Response or Server Streaming              │
  │  🔐 Security:  TLS/mTLS, Bearer token auth, OAuth2               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Key insight: OTLP means instrument ONCE in any language,     │
  │     send to any Collector, export to any backend. No lock-in.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django application's OTel SDK sends traces and metrics to the local Collector agent via OTLP/gRPC on localhost:4317. That Collector agent forwards to the central Gateway Collector via OTLP/gRPC on otel-gateway.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:4317. The Gateway exports traces to Jaeger and metrics to Prometheus — both via OTLP. Every hop uses the SAME protocol with the SAME data format. If the team ever decides to switch from Jaeger to Grafana Tempo, they change ONE line in the Collector config — the applications don't need any code changes. That's the power of OTLP.

1.2 Semantic ConventionsQ11-Q18

Fundamentals Q11 of 200
What are semantic conventions in OpenTelemetry?
A) A set of rules that define how telemetry data must be encrypted before transmission
B) A standardized naming scheme for telemetry attributes — they define that http.method always means the HTTP method, db.system always means the database type, ensuring consistency across all services and languages
C) A programming language syntax for writing OpenTelemetry Collector configuration files
D) A legal agreement between OpenTelemetry users and the CNCF governing how data can be shared
✅ Correct Answer: B

Semantic conventions are the "shared vocabulary" of OpenTelemetry — a standardized set of attribute names and values that every OTel SDK, instrumentation library, and backend agrees on. When you see http.method in a trace, you KNOW it means the HTTP method (GET, POST, etc.) regardless of whether the span came from a Python Django app, a Java Spring service, or a Node.js Express API. Without semantic conventions, every team invents their own attribute names — one service uses http_verb, another uses request.method, a third uses method — making cross-service debugging a nightmare.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Without vs With Semantic Conventions — The Tower of Babel Problem
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ WITHOUT Semantic Conventions (Chaos)                         │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                           │
  │  Python Service:   span.set_attribute("http_verb", "GET")       │
  │  Java Service:     span.set_attribute("request.method", "GET")  │
  │  Node.js Service:  span.set_attribute("method", "GET")          │
  │  Go Service:       span.set_attribute("HTTP_METHOD", "GET")     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔴 Querying "all GET requests" requires 4 different filters!    │
  │                                                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ✅ WITH Semantic Conventions (Harmony)                          │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                           │
  │  Python Service:   span.set_attribute("http.method", "GET")     │
  │  Java Service:     span.set_attribute("http.method", "GET")     │
  │  Node.js Service:  span.set_attribute("http.method", "GET")     │
  │  Go Service:       span.set_attribute("http.method", "GET")     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🟢 ONE query finds ALL GET requests across ALL services!        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, semantic conventions mean that the Web tier (Python/Django), API tier (Python/DRF), and any future services (maybe a Go recommendation engine) all use http.method, db.system, and messaging.system the same way. The observability team can write ONE dashboard query and ONE alert rule that works across the ENTIRE platform.

Fundamentals Q12 of 200
Why do semantic conventions matter in a multi-language, multi-service environment?
A) They ensure that http.method means the same thing whether it comes from a Python, Java, Go, or Node.js service — enabling unified queries, dashboards, and alerts across the entire platform without per-service customization
B) They enforce strict typing so that all telemetry data is automatically validated for correctness
C) They replace the need for API documentation since attribute names are self-documenting
D) They are primarily for aesthetic consistency — the attribute names look cleaner when they follow a standard
✅ Correct Answer: A

In a modern platform, you typically have services written in multiple languages — Python for the API, Go for high-performance data processing, Node.js for the frontend BFF, Java for legacy integration. Without semantic conventions, each language's OTel SDK would produce differently-named attributes. Semantic conventions solve this by defining a language-agnostic naming standard: http.method means the same thing whether emitted by opentelemetry-instrumentation-django (Python) or opentelemetry-javaagent (Java). This means your Grafana dashboards, Prometheus alerts, and Jaeger trace queries work identically across ALL services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
One Convention, Many Languages — Unified Observability
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SEMANTIC CONVENTIONS: Write Once, Query Everywhere              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐       │
  │  │ Python   │  │  Java    │  │  Go      │  │ Node.js  │       │
  │  │ Django   │  │ Spring   │  │  Gin     │  │ Express  │       │
  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘       │
  │       │             │             │             │               │
  │       ▼             ▼             ▼             ▼               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │              ALL EMIT THE SAME ATTRIBUTES:               │   │
  │  │  http.method="GET"    db.system="postgresql"             │   │
  │  │  http.status_code=200  messaging.system="redis"          │   │
  │  │  http.route="/api/..." rpc.system="grpc"                 │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │       │                                                        │
  │       ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ONE QUERY: "Show http.method=GET AND http.status_code   │   │
  │  │             >= 500 across ALL services"                   │   │
  │  │  → Works instantly, no per-service translation needed!   │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django API and the React frontend BFF (Node.js) both tag spans with http.route=/api/jobs/{id}/apply. The ops team creates ONE Grafana dashboard panel filtering on this route — and it automatically shows data from BOTH services, even though they're written in completely different languages. That's the power of semantic conventions.

Fundamentals Q13 of 200
Which of the following correctly lists HTTP semantic convention attributes as defined by OpenTelemetry?
A) http.path, http.verb, http.response, http.headers — custom attribute names used by most web frameworks
B) request.url, request.method, response.code, response.time — generic names that work for any protocol
C) http.method, http.status_code, http.route, http.url, http.scheme, http.target — standardized OTel attributes for HTTP spans
D) web.request_method, web.response_status, web.url_path — the legacy OpenTracing naming convention
✅ Correct Answer: C

OpenTelemetry defines a comprehensive set of HTTP semantic convention attributes under the http.* namespace. The core attributes are: http.method (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE), http.status_code (200, 404, 500, etc.), http.route (the matched route template like /api/jobs/{id}), http.url (the full request URL), http.scheme (http or https), and http.target (the path and query string). For the server side, http.server.duration is the standard histogram metric measuring request latency. These are automatically populated by OTel HTTP instrumentation libraries for all major frameworks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HTTP Semantic Conventions — Client vs Server Spans
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SERVER SPAN (Incoming Request)                                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────                                   │
  │  http.method        = "POST"                                     │
  │  http.status_code   = 201                                        │
  │  http.route         = "/api/jobs/{id}/applications"              │
  │  http.scheme        = "https"                                    │
  │  http.target        = "/api/jobs/42/applications"                │
  │  http.server.duration = 150ms  ← Metric (Histogram)              │
  │  network.protocol.name = "http"                                  │
  │  network.transport  = "tcp"                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CLIENT SPAN (Outgoing Request)                                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────                                   │
  │  http.method        = "GET"                                      │
  │  http.status_code   = 200                                        │
  │  http.url           = "https://api.anihpj.com/jobs/42"           │
  │  http.client.duration = 45ms  ← Metric (Histogram)              │
  │  server.address     = "api.anihpj.com"                           │
  │  server.port        = 443                                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In anihpj/jobpost, the Django REST Framework auto-instrumentation automatically populates http.method, http.status_code, and http.route for every API endpoint. When the API calls PostgreSQL via psycopg2, the DB instrumentation automatically adds db.system="postgresql" and db.statement to the child span. All of this happens WITHOUT writing any manual instrumentation code — the semantic conventions are baked into the OTel instrumentation libraries.

Fundamentals Q14 of 200
Which attributes does OpenTelemetry define for database semantic conventions?
A) db.host, db.port, db.user, db.password — connection-level details for security auditing
B) db.system (postgresql/mysql/redis/mongodb), db.operation (SELECT/INSERT), db.name (database name), db.statement (the query text), db.collection.name — identifying WHAT database, WHAT operation, and WHERE
C) sql.query, sql.table, sql.duration — SQL-specific attributes that only work with relational databases
D) database.type, database.action, database.target — the legacy OpenTracing naming for databases
✅ Correct Answer: B

OTel database semantic conventions use the db.* namespace. Key attributes: db.system identifies the database type (postgresql, mysql, redis, mongodb, elasticsearch, cassandra, etc.), db.operation specifies the operation (SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, HMSET for Redis), db.name is the logical database name, db.statement captures the actual query text (can be sanitized for PII), and db.collection.name for NoSQL databases. The metric db.client.operation.duration measures query latency as a histogram.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Database Semantic Conventions — Multiple DB Types, One Standard
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RELATIONAL DATABASE (PostgreSQL)  CACHE (Redis)                 │
  │  ─────────────────────────────     ─────────────                 │
  │  db.system     = "postgresql"      db.system   = "redis"        │
  │  db.operation  = "SELECT"          db.operation = "HGET"        │
  │  db.name       = "jobpost"         db.statement = "HGET jobs:42"│
  │  db.statement  = "SELECT * FROM    db.index     = 0              │
  │     jobs WHERE salary > 100000"                                   │
  │  db.sql.table  = "jobs"                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  NOSQL (MongoDB)                    MESSAGE QUEUE (Kafka)        │
  │  ────────────────                   ─────────────────────        │
  │  db.system      = "mongodb"         messaging.system = "kafka"  │
  │  db.operation   = "find"            messaging.operation = "send"│
  │  db.name        = "jobpost"         messaging.destination =     │
  │  db.collection.name = "jobs"           "job.applications"       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Same db.system attribute, different values —                │
  │     one query pattern for ALL database types!                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the PostgreSQL instrumentation automatically tags every DB span with db.system="postgresql", db.name="jobpost", and db.statement with the query text. The Redis instrumentation tags cache operations with db.system="redis". The ops team can write a single alert: "Alert when db.client.operation.duration p99 > 100ms for db.system=postgresql" — and it automatically covers every DB query in the entire platform.

Fundamentals Q15 of 200
Which attributes does OpenTelemetry define for RPC (Remote Procedure Call) semantic conventions, including gRPC?
A) rpc.url, rpc.headers, rpc.body — HTTP-level attributes reused for RPC calls
B) grpc.service, grpc.method, grpc.status — gRPC-specific attributes that only work with gRPC
C) remote.call, remote.response, remote.error — generic names for any remote invocation
D) rpc.system (grpc/java_rmi/dotnet_wcf), rpc.service (package.ServiceName), rpc.method (MethodName), rpc.grpc.status_code — system-agnostic attributes that work across all RPC frameworks
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTel RPC semantic conventions use the rpc.* namespace to cover ALL RPC frameworks — not just gRPC. rpc.system identifies the RPC framework (grpc, java_rmi, dotnet_wcf, apache_dubbo), rpc.service is the fully qualified service name, rpc.method is the method being called, and rpc.grpc.status_code captures gRPC-specific status codes (OK=0, CANCELLED=1, etc.). The metric rpc.server.duration measures RPC latency.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RPC Semantic Conventions — One Standard for gRPC, Thrift, and Beyond
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  gRPC CALL EXAMPLE                                               │
  │  ─────────────────                                               │
  │  rpc.system           = "grpc"                                   │
  │  rpc.service          = "jobpost.ApplicationsService"            │
  │  rpc.method           = "SubmitApplication"                      │
  │  rpc.grpc.status_code = 0   (OK)                                 │
  │  network.transport    = "tcp"                                    │
  │  network.type         = "ipv4"                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SERVER METRICS:                   CLIENT METRICS:               │
  │  rpc.server.duration (Histogram)    rpc.client.duration (Hist.)  │
  │  rpc.server.request.size           rpc.client.request.size       │
  │  rpc.server.response.size          rpc.client.response.size      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Works identically for gRPC, Apache Thrift, Apache Dubbo,    │
  │     and any RPC framework — ONE standard, MANY frameworks.       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: if the team adds a Go-based recommendation microservice that communicates with the Django API via gRPC, the OTel gRPC instrumentation automatically populates rpc.system="grpc", rpc.service="jobpost.RecommendationService", and rpc.method="GetRecommendedJobs" on both the client and server spans. The platform team can trace gRPC calls with the same tools they use for HTTP — no special configuration needed.

Fundamentals Q16 of 200
Which attributes does OpenTelemetry define for messaging system semantic conventions?
A) messaging.system (kafka/rabbitmq/aws_sqs), messaging.operation (send/receive/process), messaging.destination (topic or queue name), messaging.message.id — identifying the messaging technology, the action, and the target
B) queue.name, queue.action, queue.message — queue-specific attributes that only work with message brokers
C) event.source, event.type, event.payload — event-driven architecture naming that applies to all async patterns
D) mq.host, mq.port, mq.channel — low-level connection details for the message broker
✅ Correct Answer: A

OTel messaging semantic conventions use the messaging.* namespace. messaging.system identifies the broker (kafka, rabbitmq, aws_sqs, azure_event_hubs, gcp_pubsub), messaging.operation specifies the action (send, receive, process, publish, subscribe), messaging.destination is the topic or queue name, and messaging.message.id uniquely identifies each message. The span kind changes based on context: PRODUCER for sending, CONSUMER for receiving.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Messaging Semantic Conventions — Async Tracing Across Queues
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRODUCER SPAN (Sending a Message)                               │
  │  ────────────────────────────────                                │
  │  messaging.system      = "rabbitmq"                              │
  │  messaging.operation   = "publish"                               │
  │  messaging.destination = "job.applications.exchange"             │
  │  messaging.message.id  = "msg-abc123"                            │
  │  Span Kind: PRODUCER                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CONSUMER SPAN (Receiving a Message)                             │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────                             │
  │  messaging.system      = "rabbitmq"                              │
  │  messaging.operation   = "process"                               │
  │  messaging.destination = "job.applications.queue"                │
  │  messaging.message.id  = "msg-abc123"  ← Same message!           │
  │  Span Kind: CONSUMER                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 The Trace ID links Producer → Broker → Consumer              │
  │     into ONE distributed trace, even across async boundaries.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In anihpj/jobpost: when the API publishes a "new application submitted" event to RabbitMQ, the OTel instrumentation creates a PRODUCER span with messaging.system="rabbitmq" and messaging.destination="job.applications". When the Celery worker receives that message, it creates a CONSUMER span — and because both spans share the same Trace ID via context propagation in the message headers, the full end-to-end trace includes the async hop through the message broker.

Fundamentals Q17 of 200
What are Resource semantic conventions and what key attributes do they define?
A) Resource conventions define CPU and memory limits for the OpenTelemetry Collector
B) Resource conventions specify how telemetry data is stored on disk — file paths, formats, and retention policies
C) Resource conventions describe the ENTITY producing telemetry — service.name identifies the service, service.namespace groups related services, service.instance.id uniquely identifies each instance, and attributes like k8s.pod.name, host.name, and cloud.region provide infrastructure context
D) Resource conventions are for billing purposes — they track how many spans each service produces for cost allocation
✅ Correct Answer: C

Resource is the entity that produces telemetry — it answers "WHO sent this data?" Every span, metric, and log record in OTel carries Resource attributes that identify the source. The most critical Resource attribute is service.name (the logical name of your service like "anihpj-api"). Other key Resource attributes include service.namespace (grouping, e.g., "production"), service.instance.id (the specific pod/VM), k8s.pod.name, k8s.namespace.name, host.name, cloud.provider, cloud.region, deployment.environment, and telemetry.sdk.language.
