  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  service.name        = "anihpj-api"                      │    │
  │  │  service.namespace   = "production"                      │    │
  │  │  service.instance.id = "anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde"          │    │
  │  │  service.version     = "2.3.1"                           │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  k8s.pod.name        = "anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde"          │    │
  │  │  k8s.namespace.name   = "anihpj"                         │    │
  │  │  k8s.node.name       = "anih"                            │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  host.name           = "anih"                            │    │
  │  │  os.type             = "linux"                           │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  deployment.environment = "production"                   │    │
  │  │  telemetry.sdk.language  = "python"                      │    │
  │  │  telemetry.sdk.version   = "1.28.0"                      │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Resource = Always attached. Every span, metric, and log      │
  │     automatically carries these attributes.                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when the API pod anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde emits a span, the Resource attributes automatically tell you it came from the "anihpj-api" service, running in the "production" namespace, on the "anih" node, using Python OTel SDK 1.28.0. If you need to check whether a bug is specific to one pod or affects all instances, service.instance.id gives you that granularity instantly.

Fundamentals Q18 of 200
How does anihpj/jobpost use semantic conventions to unify observability across its Django API, PostgreSQL database, and Redis cache?
A) Each component uses its own naming — Django uses http.*, PostgreSQL uses sql.*, Redis uses cache.* — and a translation layer maps them together
B) All components follow OTel semantic conventions: Django spans use http.method/http.route, PostgreSQL spans use db.system="postgresql"/db.statement, Redis spans use db.system="redis"/db.operation — enabling unified queries like "show all spans where db.system=postgresql AND duration > 100ms"
C) Semantic conventions are not needed for anihpj because it's a single Django application — conventions only matter for microservices
D) anihpj uses custom attribute names defined in a company-wide wiki that all developers manually reference
✅ Correct Answer: B

Even though anihpj/jobpost is primarily a Django application, it interacts with multiple technologies — each with its own OTel semantic conventions. The Django REST Framework instrumentation uses http.* conventions (http.method=POST, http.route=/api/jobs/{id}). The PostgreSQL (psycopg2) instrumentation uses db.* conventions (db.system="postgresql", db.statement="SELECT..."). The Redis instrumentation uses db.system="redis" and db.operation="HGET". Because all of these follow the OTel semantic conventions, the platform team can write unified queries: "show all spans in trace X", "alert when db.client.operation.duration > 100ms", "dashboard: http.server.duration by http.route".

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Semantic Conventions in Practice
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ONE TRACE, MULTIPLE CONVENTIONS — ALL UNIFIED                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Span 1: Django Web (http.*)                                     │
  │    http.method = "POST"                                          │
  │    http.route  = "/jobs/{id}/apply"                              │
  │    http.status_code = 200                                        │
  │         │                                                        │
  │    ┌────┴────────────────────────────┐                           │
  │    │                                 │                           │
  │    ▼                                 ▼                           │
  │  Span 2: PostgreSQL (db.*)         Span 3: Redis (db.*)         │
  │    db.system = "postgresql"          db.system = "redis"        │
  │    db.operation = "INSERT"           db.operation = "DEL"       │
  │    db.statement = "INSERT INTO       db.statement =              │
  │      applications..."                 "DEL job:42:applications" │
  │                                                                  │
  │  UNIFIED QUERIES THAT WORK ACROSS ALL SPANS:                     │
  │  ✅ "Trace ID = abc123" — shows ALL 3 spans                     │
  │  ✅ "db.system = postgresql" — shows ONLY PostgreSQL spans      │
  │  ✅ "http.status_code >= 500" — shows ONLY error responses      │
  │  ✅ "duration > 1s" — shows ALL slow spans regardless of type  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is why semantic conventions matter: the anihpj ops team doesn't need to know that Django uses http.* while PostgreSQL uses db.* — the conventions handle that. They write queries using the standard attribute names, and the OTel instrumentation libraries ensure every component emits data in the right format. One platform, one observability standard, zero per-service translation.

1.3 InstrumentationQ19-Q28

Fundamentals Q19 of 200
What is instrumentation in the context of OpenTelemetry?
A) The process of installing OpenTelemetry Collector on Kubernetes nodes
B) The act of adding code or agents to your application to emit telemetry data (traces, metrics, logs) — like installing sensors in a car to measure speed, temperature, and fuel consumption
C) Configuring network firewalls to allow OTLP traffic on ports 4317 and 4318
D) A musical instrument that plays when your application crashes — an audible alerting mechanism
✅ Correct Answer: B

Instrumentation is the process of making your application emit telemetry data. Without instrumentation, your application runs silently — you can't see what's happening inside. With instrumentation, your application produces spans for every request, metrics for every measurement, and logs for every event. Think of it like a car: without instruments, you don't know your speed, fuel level, or engine temperature. Instrumentation adds those gauges to your application. OpenTelemetry provides TWO approaches: manual (you write code using the OTel API/SDK) and automatic (agents inject instrumentation without code changes).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Instrumentation — Adding "Sensors" to Your Application
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT INSTRUMENTATION (Blind)                                 │
  │  ───────────────────────────────                                 │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                    │
  │  │ Your App │ ──── (silence) ──── ???                           │
  │  └──────────┘                                                    │
  │  🔴 You have NO IDEA what's happening inside                     │
  │                                                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  WITH INSTRUMENTATION (Observable)                               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────                               │
  │  ┌──────────┐    spans    ┌───────────┐    ┌──────────┐        │
  │  │ Your App │ ──────────► │ Collector │───►│ Jaeger   │        │
  │  │ + OTel   │   metrics   │           │    │ Prometheus│       │
  │  │ SDK      │ ──────────► │           │───►│ Grafana  │       │
  │  └──────────┘    logs     └───────────┘    └──────────┘        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🟢 You see EVERY request, EVERY metric, EVERY log —            │
  │     with full context and correlation                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: without instrumentation, if a user reports "my job application failed," you have no idea what happened. With OTel instrumentation, every HTTP request to the Django API automatically creates a span, every PostgreSQL query creates a child span, every Redis operation creates a child span — all linked by one Trace ID. You can see the FULL journey of that failed request in seconds.

Fundamentals Q20 of 200
When should you use manual instrumentation vs automatic instrumentation?
A) Always use manual — automatic instrumentation is unreliable and should never be used in production
B) Always use automatic — manual instrumentation is too complex and error-prone for any use case
C) Start with automatic instrumentation for standard coverage (HTTP, DB, gRPC, messaging), then add manual instrumentation for business-specific operations (custom spans for domain logic, custom metrics for business KPIs, custom attributes for user context) — they complement each other
D) Use manual for Python and automatic for Java — the choice depends entirely on the programming language
✅ Correct Answer: C

The best strategy is both. Automatic instrumentation gives you instant coverage of standard operations: HTTP requests, database queries, gRPC calls, message queue operations — all with ZERO code changes. This is your "baseline" observability. But automatic instrumentation only sees what the libraries expose — it can't know about your business logic. Manual instrumentation fills this gap: you add custom spans around critical business operations ("ProcessJobApplication", "CalculateSalaryMatch"), custom metrics for business KPIs ("qualified.applications.count"), and custom attributes for user context ("employer.plan=tier-3"). Use both together for complete visibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auto + Manual Instrumentation — The Complete Picture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🤖 AUTOMATIC (Zero Code)           ✍️ MANUAL (You Write)        │
  │  ─────────────────────               ─────────────────────       │
  │  ✅ HTTP requests/routes             ✅ Business logic spans      │
  │  ✅ Database queries (SQL/NoSQL)     ✅ Custom business metrics   │
  │  ✅ gRPC calls                       ✅ Domain-specific attributes│
  │  ✅ Message queue operations         ✅ SLA/SLO tracking          │
  │  ✅ Framework middleware              ✅ User context injection    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Covers: 80% of your telemetry       Covers: The 20% that        │
  │  with ZERO effort                    matters most to YOUR business│
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Auto = Foundation (broad coverage)                           │
  │     Manual = Precision (deep business insight)                   │
  │     Together = COMPLETE observability                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: auto-instrumentation covers every Django HTTP request, every PostgreSQL query, and every Redis operation automatically. Then the team adds manual instrumentation: a custom span around the "matchCandidateToJob" algorithm, a custom counter for "job.applications.by_category", and a custom attribute employer.subscription_tier on every span. The result: you can answer both "is the API healthy?" (auto) AND "which subscription tier generates the most applications?" (manual).

Fundamentals Q21 of 200
What are the steps in the OpenTelemetry instrumentation lifecycle?
A) Install OTel SDK → Configure TracerProvider/MeterProvider/LoggerProvider → Create spans/metrics/logs in your code → Set up Exporters (OTLP/Console) → Telemetry flows to Collector/Backend
B) Deploy Collector → Write YAML config → Start application → Telemetry appears automatically
C) Buy a commercial APM license → Install their proprietary agent → View dashboards
D) The lifecycle is fully automated — you just import opentelemetry and everything works without configuration
✅ Correct Answer: A

The instrumentation lifecycle follows a clear 4-step pattern: 1) Install the OTel SDK packages (pip install opentelemetry-api opentelemetry-sdk opentelemetry-exporter-otlp), 2) Configure the providers — set up TracerProvider (for traces), MeterProvider (for metrics), LoggerProvider (for logs) with appropriate exporters and resource attributes, 3) Create telemetry — get a Tracer and start spans, get a Meter and record metrics, emit logs via the Logger, and 4) Export — the configured exporters send telemetry to the Collector (via OTLP) or directly to backends.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Instrumentation Lifecycle — 4 Steps to Observability
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: INSTALL                   STEP 2: CONFIGURE             │
  │  ─────────────                     ────────────────               │
  │  pip install opentelemetry-api     from opentelemetry.sdk._trace│
  │  pip install opentelemetry-sdk        import TracerProvider      │
  │  pip install opentelemetry-        provider = TracerProvider(    │
  │     exporter-otlp                     resource=resource)         │
  │                                    provider.add_span_processor(  │
  │                                       BatchSpanProcessor(        │
  │  STEP 3: CREATE                       OTLPSpanExporter()))       │
  │  ─────────────                     trace.set_tracer_provider(    │
  │  tracer = trace.get_tracer(           provider)                  │
  │     "anihpj-api")                                                │
  │  span = tracer.start_span(                                       │
  │     "process_application")          STEP 4: EXPORT               │
  │  span.set_attribute(               ─────────────                 │
  │     "job.id", job_id)              SDK ──OTLP/gRPC──► Collector │
  │  # ... do work ...                 Collector ──► Jaeger/Prom/... │
  │  span.end()                                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django application's settings.py initializes the OTel SDK at startup (Step 2), configuring the OTLP exporter to send data to otel-collector.anihpj.svc:4317. The Django auto-instrumentation (Step 1) and custom business spans (Step 3) produce telemetry throughout the request lifecycle. The BatchSpanProcessor (Step 4) efficiently exports spans in batches to the Collector — completing the full lifecycle.

Fundamentals Q23 of 200
What CANNOT be captured by automatic instrumentation?
A) HTTP request timing
B) Database query text
C) Business logic operations (matchCandidateToJob algorithm), custom business metrics (qualified.applications), domain-specific attributes (employer.subscription_tier), and internal function calls
D) gRPC calls — auto-instrumentation does not support gRPC
✅ Correct Answer: C

Automatic instrumentation traces at framework/library boundaries — it sees HTTP requests entering and DB queries executing, but it cannot see INSIDE your business logic. The "calculateSalaryMatchScore" function, "filterByEmployerPreferences" logic, and "rankCandidatesByRelevance" algorithm are invisible without manual instrumentation. You need manual spans around these operations, custom metrics for business KPIs, and domain-specific attributes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auto vs Manual — The Coverage Gap
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AUTO COVERS:                       MANUAL NEEDED FOR:           │
  │  ✅ Django view receives request    ✅ matchCandidateToJob()     │
  │  ✅ PostgreSQL executes query       ✅ calculateSalaryScore()    │
  │  ✅ Redis returns cached data       ✅ filterByPreferences()     │
  │  ✅ Django returns HTTP response    ✅ rankCandidates()          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Auto = Framework boundaries. Manual = Business logic inside. │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: auto-instrumentation shows the /api/jobs/search endpoint took 500ms. Manual instrumentation reveals the "matchCandidateToJob" algorithm took 350ms — specifically, "calculateSalaryCompatibility" consumed 200ms due to a new salary range filter.

Fundamentals Q24 of 200
What is zero-code instrumentation and what are its limitations?
A) Zero-code means no packages needed — OTel works through the Kubernetes API
B) Zero-code provides full business observability without developer involvement
C) The Collector auto-discovers and instruments applications without configuration
D) Agents or Operators inject OTel at runtime without source code changes — great for framework coverage but cannot instrument business logic or custom metrics
✅ Correct Answer: D

Zero-code instrumentation uses agents (Java javaagent, Python opentelemetry-instrument, Node.js --require flag) or the Kubernetes Operator to inject OTel at runtime. It gives instant coverage of HTTP, DB, gRPC, and messaging — perfect for ops teams. However, it can't see business logic, custom metrics, or domain-specific operations — those require developer-written manual spans.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero-Code Approaches
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🐍 Python:  opentelemetry-instrument python manage.py runserver│
  │  ☕ Java:    java -javaagent:opentelemetry-javaagent.jar -jar ..│
  │  ☸️  K8s:     OTel Operator + pod annotation                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ No source changes  ✅ Ops can deploy  ✅ Instant coverage   │
  │  ❌ No business spans  ❌ No custom metrics  ❌ No domain attrs │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the OTel Operator auto-instruments Django pods via annotation. HTTP, DB, and Redis are traced instantly. But to answer "which job category converts best?", developers add manual spans.

Fundamentals Q25 of 200
What is the difference between library instrumentation and agent instrumentation?
A) Libraries are for production; agents are only for development
B) Library instrumentation is added via package manager (pip install/npm install) and compiled into the app; agent instrumentation is injected at runtime via JVM agent, wrapper script, or sidecar without modifying application code
C) There is no difference — the terms are interchangeable
D) Libraries work only with Python; agents work only with Java
✅ Correct Answer: B

Library instrumentation is added as a dependency (pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django) and compiled into your application. Agent instrumentation is injected at runtime — the Java agent intercepts class loading to add tracing, the Python wrapper script monkey-patches libraries, and the Kubernetes Operator injects a sidecar or init container. Libraries give you more control; agents give you zero-code deployment.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Library vs Agent — Two Paths to Instrumentation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LIBRARY (Compile-time)              AGENT (Runtime)             │
  │  ─────────────────────               ─────────────               │
  │  pip install otel-instrumentation-   java -javaagent:otel-       │
  │    django                              javaagent.jar             │
  │  # Import in settings.py             # No code changes           │
  │  OTEL_DJANGO_INSTRUMENT              opentelemetry-instrument    │
  │                                      python app.py               │
  │  ✅ Fine-grained control             ✅ Zero code changes        │
  │  ✅ Part of your build               ✅ Ops team can deploy      │
  │  ❌ Requires code changes            ❌ Less control             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the dev team adds library instrumentation for Django and PostgreSQL in requirements.txt. The platform team uses the OTel Operator (agent approach) to auto-instrument any new microservices without waiting for developers to add libraries.

Fundamentals Q26 of 200
How does the OpenTelemetry Operator auto-instrument pods on Kubernetes?
A) It modifies the Kubernetes source code to add tracing to the API server
B) It requires every pod to include the OTel SDK in its container image
C) You add an annotation like instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true" to the pod — the Operator mutates the pod spec to inject an init container that copies the OTel agent into a shared volume, and configures environment variables for auto-instrumentation
D) It deploys a DaemonSet that intercepts all network traffic on every node
✅ Correct Answer: C

The OpenTelemetry Operator for Kubernetes uses a mutating webhook to inject auto-instrumentation into pods at creation time. You add a simple annotation like instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true" (or java, nodejs, dotnet, go). The Operator then modifies the pod spec: it adds an init container that copies the OTel auto-instrumentation agent into a shared volume, sets environment variables (OTEL_SERVICE_NAME, OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT, etc.), and configures the language-specific auto-instrumentation mechanism (PYTHONPATH for Python, JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS for Java). The application container then starts with OTel automatically active.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Operator — Annotation-Driven Auto-Instrumentation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEPLOYMENT YAML:                                                │
  │  ────────────────                                                │
  │  apiVersion: apps/v1                                             │
  │  kind: Deployment                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: anihpj-api                                              │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    template:                                                     │
  │      metadata:                                                   │
  │        annotations:                                              │
  │          instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true"  │
  │      spec:                                                       │
  │        containers:                                               │
  │        - name: anihpj-api                                        │
  │          image: anihpj/api:v2.3.1                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  OPERATOR ACTIONS AT POD CREATION:                               │
  │  1. Mutating webhook intercepts pod creation                     │
  │  2. Injects init container with OTel Python agent                │
  │  3. Sets OTEL_SERVICE_NAME, OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT env vars│
  │  4. Configures PYTHONPATH to include OTel libraries              │
  │  5. App starts → auto-instrumented with ZERO code changes        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the deployment YAML for the Django API includes the annotation instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true". When a new pod is created, the Operator automatically injects the OTel Python agent. The Django application starts with full auto-instrumentation — tracing every HTTP request, DB query, and Redis operation — without a single line of OTel code in the Django application itself.

Fundamentals Q27 of 200
How should you instrument anihpj/jobpost for complete observability?
A) Use Django auto-instrumentation for HTTP/DB/Redis coverage, then add manual spans for business operations (matchCandidateToJob, calculateSalaryScore), custom metrics for business KPIs, and custom attributes for user context (employer.subscription_tier) — combining auto and manual for complete visibility
B) Use only auto-instrumentation — it covers everything needed for a Django application
C) Write entirely manual instrumentation — auto-instrumentation is too unreliable for production
D) Deploy the Collector and it will automatically discover and instrument the Django app
✅ Correct Answer: A

The best approach for anihpj/jobpost is the hybrid strategy: auto-instrumentation covers Django HTTP requests, PostgreSQL queries, and Redis operations automatically. Then manual instrumentation adds business-specific spans (matchCandidateToJob, calculateSalaryMatchScore), custom metrics (job.applications.by_category counter, application.match_score histogram), and custom attributes (employer.subscription_tier, job.category, user.role). Together, you get both broad framework coverage and deep business insight. This is the recommended pattern for all OTel adoptions.

This hybrid strategy gives the anihpj team: (1) immediate alerting when the API is slow or returning errors (auto), (2) the ability to trace every request end-to-end (auto), and (3) business analytics like "employers on the Enterprise tier have a 3x higher application rate" (manual). No other approach provides all three layers of insight.

1.4 Analysis and OutcomesQ29-Q36

Fundamentals Q29 of 200
What is the RED Method and what three metrics does it track?
A) Rate (requests/sec), Errors (% failed), Duration (latency p50/p95/p99) — the three golden signals for any service
B) RAM, Environment, Disk — infrastructure metrics for server health
C) Requests, Events, Data — the three telemetry signal types
D) Receive, Enrich, Distribute — Collector pipeline stages
✅ Correct Answer: A

The RED Method tracks Rate (requests/second), Errors (percentage of failed requests), and Duration (latency distribution). In OTel: Rate from Counter http.server.request_count, Errors from filtering by http.status_code >= 500, Duration from Histogram http.server.duration. RED is the universal starting point for monitoring ANY service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RED Method — Three Golden Signals
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔴 RATE: 150 req/s           🔴 ERRORS: 2% failure rate        │
  │  🔴 DURATION: p50=45ms  p95=120ms  p99=395ms                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 RED works for EVERY service — REST API, gRPC, GraphQL, MQ   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the RED dashboard shows 150 req/s, 2% error rate, p99=395ms. When p99 spiked to 3.2s, the RED dashboard flagged it immediately, and the on-call engineer traced to the slow DB query.

Fundamentals Q30 of 200
How does the USE Method differ from the RED Method?
A) USE is for user-facing services; RED is for backend infrastructure
B) They are the same thing — different vendors use different names
C) USE (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) focuses on RESOURCES — "how busy are my servers?" — while RED focuses on SERVICE BEHAVIOR — "how is my API performing?" They complement each other
D) USE is legacy; RED replaces it entirely
✅ Correct Answer: C

USE monitors RESOURCES (CPU utilization, memory saturation, disk errors). RED monitors SERVICES (request rate, error %, latency). USE identifies the CAUSE (PostgreSQL CPU at 95%), RED shows the IMPACT (API p99 jumped from 45ms to 3.2s). Both are essential — USE for infrastructure health, RED for application health.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RED + USE = Complete Picture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  USE: PostgreSQL CPU 95%  ──►  RED: API p99 3.2s, errors 15%   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 USE identifies CAUSE, RED shows IMPACT — together = RCA     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: USE monitors the PostgreSQL pod; RED monitors the API. When DB CPU hits 95%, RED shows API latency spike — the complete story in two dashboards.

Fundamentals Q31 of 200
What is an SLI (Service Level Indicator)?
A) A legal contract specifying financial penalties for downtime
B) A quantitative measure of service behavior — e.g., "p99 latency of /api/jobs over 5 minutes" or "% of requests returning non-5xx"
C) A dashboard widget showing all services' current status
D) A license indicator tracking active user count
✅ Correct Answer: B

An SLI is a precise, measurable metric: specific (exact endpoint/metric), measurable (from telemetry), and user-relevant. Examples: "% of /api/* requests returning 2xx/3xx", "p99 latency of /api/search", "job applications saved vs submitted ratio". SLIs are the raw measurements — they answer "what is happening right now?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLI → SLO → Error Budget Chain
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLI:  "99.7% of /api/* requests succeed" ← Raw measurement     │
  │  SLO:  "99.9% success over 30 days"       ← Target/goal         │
  │  Error Budget: 0.1% = 43 min/month downtime allowed             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, primary SLI: "% of /api/* requests returning non-5xx, measured over 5-minute windows." Calculated from OTel http.server.request_count metric.

Fundamentals Q32 of 200
What is an SLO (Service Level Objective)?
A) A target for an SLI — e.g., "99.9% success rate over 30 days" — defining the acceptable performance threshold and creating an error budget for the team
B) A financial penalty for exceeding API rate limits
C) Identical to an SLI — the terms are interchangeable
D) A legal document about user data handling
✅ Correct Answer: A

An SLO is the GOAL for an SLI over a time window: "99.9% success rate over 30 days." It creates an error budget (0.1% = ~43 min/month allowed downtime). While error budget remains, the team can ship features. When exhausted, freeze features and focus on reliability. SLOs replace subjective debates with objective data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Error Budget — The SLO's Power
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ERROR BUDGET > 0:  ✅ Ship features  ✅ Normal operations       │
  │  ERROR BUDGET = 0:  🔴 Freeze features 🔴 All hands on reliability│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: SLO is "99.9% availability over 30 days." Error budget had 12 min remaining last month — close but healthy. SLO-based alerts fire when burn rate predicts exhaustion within 3 days.

Fundamentals Q33 of 200
What is an SLA (Service Level Agreement)?
A) Another name for SLO — SLA and SLO mean the same thing
B) A dashboard that shows service levels for all APIs
C) A business contract between provider and customer specifying the promised service level (e.g., "99.5% uptime") and the financial consequences (refunds, credits, penalties) if it's not met
D) A Service Level Algorithm that automatically adjusts server capacity
✅ Correct Answer: C

An SLA is a BUSINESS contract — it's what you promise customers, with financial consequences if broken. SLOs should be stricter than SLAs: if your SLA promises 99.5% uptime, set your internal SLO at 99.9% so you catch problems before customers notice. SLI = measurement, SLO = internal goal, SLA = external promise with consequences.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLI → SLO → SLA — The Reliability Hierarchy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLI: "99.7% success"  ← What we MEASURE (internal, real-time)  │
  │  SLO: "99.9% over 30d" ← What we AIM FOR (internal target)      │
  │  SLA: "99.5% uptime"   ← What we PROMISE (external, with $$$)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 SLO should be stricter than SLA — catch issues internally    │
  │     before they breach the customer contract.                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Enterprise customer SLA promises 99.5% uptime. The internal SLO targets 99.9%. The actual SLI currently shows 99.7% — within SLA but below SLO, triggering an internal reliability review before customers are impacted.

Fundamentals Q34 of 200
How do you use telemetry for root cause analysis when something fails?
A) Check each service's logs individually until you find the error
B) Start from the symptom (RED dashboard shows latency spike) → click exemplar to find a representative trace → open trace waterfall to identify the slow span → click span to see correlated logs → identify root cause (e.g., missing DB index) → fix and verify
C) Restart all services and see if the problem goes away
D) Roll back the last deployment without investigating — it's faster
✅ Correct Answer: B

Root cause analysis with telemetry follows a systematic path: Metric → Exemplar → Trace → Span → Log → Root Cause. Start at the dashboard showing the symptom (e.g., p99 latency spiked). Click the spike to see exemplar traces. Open one trace to see the waterfall — which span is slow? Click that span to see its attributes and correlated logs. The log reveals the root cause (e.g., "missing index on jobs.salary column"). This entire flow takes SECONDS with structured OTel data vs HOURS of manual log searching.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RCA Flow with OTel — Seconds, Not Hours
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔵 Grafana: p99 latency spiked at 14:32                        │
  │     │ Click spike → exemplar                                     │
  │     ▼                                                            │
  │  🟢 Jaeger: Trace abc123 — DB query span took 3.2 seconds!      │
  │     │ Click span → correlated log                                │
  │     ▼                                                            │
  │  🔴 Log: "Seq Scan on jobs table — 2.3M rows — missing index"   │
  │     │                                                            │
  │     ▼                                                            │
  │  ✅ Root cause: Missing index on jobs.salary column              │
  │     Fix: CREATE INDEX ON jobs(salary) → Latency back to 45ms    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when job applications failed at 3:14 PM, the team followed this exact flow. RED dashboard showed error rate spike → clicked exemplar → trace showed DB timeout → correlated log revealed a deadlock on the applications table. Fix identified and deployed in 8 minutes.

Fundamentals Q35 of 200
What is SLO-based alerting and why is it better than threshold-based alerting?
A) SLO-based alerting uses AI to predict failures before they happen
B) There is no difference — they're the same approach
C) SLO-based alerting only alerts when the service is completely down
D) SLO-based alerting monitors the error budget burn rate — it alerts when you're consuming your error budget too fast (e.g., "will exhaust in 3 days at current rate"), which is more meaningful than threshold alerts ("CPU > 90%") that may fire for normal, non-critical behavior
✅ Correct Answer: D

SLO-based alerting is a paradigm shift: instead of alerting on individual metric thresholds (which generate noise), you alert on the error budget burn rate. If your 30-day error budget is burning so fast it'll be exhausted in 3 days, that's a real problem. If CPU spiked to 90% for 30 seconds but didn't impact your SLO, that's NOT worth waking someone up. SLO-based alerting dramatically reduces alert fatigue while catching issues that actually matter to users.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Threshold vs SLO-Based Alerting
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ THRESHOLD-BASED                     ✅ SLO-BASED             │
  │  "CPU > 90% → ALERT!"                   "Error budget will be   │
  │                                          exhausted in 3 days"   │
  │  Problem: CPU spikes are normal          → ALERT!               │
  │  during batch processing —                                        │
  │  this is a FALSE ALARM                  Problem: This means the  │
  │                                          service is DEGRADING    │
  │  🔴 High alert fatigue                  🟢 Low alert fatigue    │
  │  🔴 Many false positives                🟢 High signal quality  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the old threshold alerts ("API error rate > 5%") fired 3-4 times per week — most were transient spikes. After switching to SLO-based alerting ("30-day error budget will exhaust in 24 hours at current burn rate"), alerts dropped to once per month, and every alert represented a REAL user-impacting issue worth investigating.

🟡 Intermediate: The Heart of the OTCA Exam — 46% of Your Score

This category covers the data model, all three signals in depth, SDK configuration and pipelines, context propagation, and auto-instrumentation agents. Every concept maps directly to real-world instrumentation tasks.

Q37-Q128 92 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 Data ModelQ37-Q48

API & SDK Q37 of 200
What is the OpenTelemetry data model?
A) A database schema for storing telemetry data
B) A machine learning model that predicts application crashes
C) A language-agnostic specification defining the structure of telemetry data — how spans, metrics, logs, and resources are represented regardless of which SDK produced them
D) A JSON template all exporters must follow
✅ Correct Answer: C

The OTel data model defines that a Span has TraceId, SpanId, ParentSpanId, Name, Kind, timestamps, Attributes, Events, Links, Status — regardless of whether created by Python, Java, Go, or Node.js. A Metric has Name, Description, Unit, Instrument Kind, and Aggregation Temporality. This cross-language consistency is the foundation of OTel's interoperability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Data Model — Language-Agnostic, Cross-Signal
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SPANS:   TraceId, SpanId, ParentSpanId, Name, Kind, Start/End, │
  │           Attributes, Events, Links, Status                      │
  │  METRICS: Name, Description, Unit, Instrument Kind, Temporality  │
  │  LOGS:    Timestamp, Body, Severity, Attributes, TraceId, SpanId│
  │  RESOURCE: service.name, k8s.pod.name, host.name, cloud.region  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🐍 Python SDK ──► Data Model ◄── ☕ Java SDK                    │
  │  💡 ALL SDKs produce the SAME structure = TRUE portability       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Python Django and Go recommendation engine both produce spans conforming to the same model. Jaeger displays them identically regardless of source language.

API & SDK Q38 of 200
What fields make up the Span data model in OpenTelemetry?
A) Only TraceId and SpanId — the minimum required for tracing
B) TraceId, SpanId, ParentSpanId, Name, Kind (CLIENT/SERVER/INTERNAL/PRODUCER/CONSUMER), StartTime, EndTime, Attributes (key-value metadata), Events (timestamped annotations), Links (connections to other spans), and Status (OK/Error/Unset)
C) URL, HTTP method, status code, and duration — HTTP-specific fields
D) Service name, operation name, timestamp, and a free-form message string
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Span has 11 fields: TraceId (links all spans in a trace), SpanId (unique within trace), ParentSpanId (creates the span tree), Name (the operation), Kind (CLIENT/SERVER/INTERNAL/PRODUCER/CONSUMER), StartTime/EndTime (duration), Attributes (key-value metadata like http.method), Events (timestamped log-like annotations), Links (connections to other traces), and Status (OK/Error/Unset).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Data Model — All 11 Fields
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span {                                                          │
  │    TraceId:      "a1b2c3d4e5f6..."  ← Links ALL spans together  │
  │    SpanId:       "1234567890abcdef"  ← This span's ID           │
  │    ParentSpanId: "fedcba0987654321"  ← Parent (null for root)   │
  │    Name:         "POST /api/jobs"    ← What operation?          │
  │    Kind:         SERVER              ← CLIENT/SERVER/INTERNAL/  │
  │    StartTime:    14:32:01.000        ← When it started          │
  │    EndTime:      14:32:01.395        ← When it ended            │
  │    Attributes:   {http.method:"POST", http.route:"/api/jobs"}   │
  │    Events:       [{name:"cache.miss", timestamp:..., attrs:{}}]│
  │    Links:        [{traceId:"...", spanId:"...", attrs:{}}]     │
  │    Status:       {code: OK}          ← OK / Error / Unset       │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every Django request creates a SERVER span with all 11 fields populated automatically by the OTel Django instrumentation.

API & SDK Q39 of 200
What is a SpanContext and what fields does it contain?
A) SpanContext is the immutable subset of a Span that gets propagated across service boundaries — it contains TraceId, SpanId, TraceFlags (sampled flag), TraceState (vendor-specific data), and an IsRemote flag indicating whether the context was received from another service
B) SpanContext is the full span including all attributes, events, and links
C) SpanContext is a Java-specific class; other languages don't use this concept
D) SpanContext only contains the TraceId — nothing else is needed for context propagation
✅ Correct Answer: A

SpanContext is the serializable, immutable subset of a Span that gets propagated in HTTP headers (traceparent) or gRPC metadata. It's the "ID card" that travels with every request: TraceId (who am I part of?), SpanId (who am I?), TraceFlags (am I sampled?), TraceState (vendor-specific context). The IsRemote flag distinguishes spans created locally from those propagated from upstream services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SpanContext — What Gets Propagated Between Services
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SpanContext = IDENTITY that travels across the network          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  traceparent: 00-a1b2c3d4e5f6...-1234567890abcdef-01           │
  │               ││ │               │                ││             │
  │               ││ TraceId         SpanId           │Sampled      │
  │               ││                                  │              │
  │               Version                             01=sampled    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  tracestate: vendor1=value1,vendor2=value2  ← Vendor context    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when the Web tier calls the API tier, the SpanContext travels in the HTTP traceparent header. The API reads this context to create a child span linked to the same trace.

API & SDK Q40 of 200
What is a SpanLink and when should you use it?
A) SpanLink replaces the parent-child relationship — use it instead of ParentSpanId
B) SpanLink is a URL that points to the trace visualization in Jaeger
C) SpanLink is used to connect spans within the SAME trace for better visualization
D) SpanLink connects spans from DIFFERENT traces — use it for fan-in patterns (batch processing where multiple input requests feed one output), async workflows, and eventually-consistent systems where causal relationships span trace boundaries
✅ Correct Answer: D

SpanLink connects spans across trace boundaries. When a batch processor reads 100 messages and produces 1 output, each input message has its own trace — but the output span should link to ALL of them. SpanLink also handles "fire and forget" patterns, eventual consistency, and compensation transactions where the causal relationship isn't a simple parent-child.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SpanLink — Connecting Across Trace Boundaries
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Trace A: [Span: Send message 1]                                │
  │  Trace B: [Span: Send message 2]    ┐                            │
  │  Trace C: [Span: Send message 3]    │  SpanLinks connect         │
  │                                      ├──► these to...            │
  │  Trace D: [Span: Batch process] ◄───┘  the batch span           │
  │           links: [TraceA, TraceB, TraceC]                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when the Celery worker batch-processes 50 job application emails, each email message has its own trace. The batch span uses SpanLinks to reference all 50 input traces, so you can navigate from any failed email back to the batch that processed it.

API & SDK Q41 of 200
What is a SpanEvent in OpenTelemetry?
A) A SpanEvent is another name for a Span — they are the same thing
B) A SpanEvent tracks when a span is created or destroyed by the SDK
C) A SpanEvent is a timestamped annotation WITHIN a span — like a log entry tied to a specific moment in the span's lifecycle, used for recording exceptions, cache hits/misses, retry attempts, and other discrete occurrences during the span
D) A SpanEvent is how spans communicate with each other across different traces
✅ Correct Answer: C

A SpanEvent is a timestamped annotation on a span — it marks a specific moment during the span's execution. Think of it as a "log entry attached to a span": "cache miss at 150ms", "retry attempt #2 at 300ms", "exception thrown at 450ms". Events carry a name, timestamp, and optional attributes. The most common SpanEvent is the exception event, which records exception.type, exception.message, and exception.stacktrace.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SpanEvents — Annotations on the Span Timeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span: "POST /api/jobs/search" [0ms ────────────────── 500ms]   │
  │    │                                                             │
  │    ├── Event @ 50ms:  "cache.miss" {key: "jobs:search:..."}    │
  │    ├── Event @ 200ms: "db.query.start" {table: "jobs"}         │
  │    ├── Event @ 450ms: "db.query.end" {rows: 42}                │
  │    └── Event @ 480ms: "cache.set" {ttl: 300}                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the search span records events for cache miss, DB query start/end, and result count — giving granular visibility into what happened INSIDE the 500ms span.

API & SDK Q42 of 200
What are the three Span Status values and when should each be used?
A) Active, Complete, Abandoned — tracking span lifecycle states
B) OK (operation completed successfully), Error (operation failed — always set when recording an exception), Unset (default — the operation completed but no explicit status was set, treated as success by most backends)
C) 200, 400, 500 — HTTP-style status codes for spans
D) Started, Running, Ended — time-based status values
✅ Correct Answer: B

Span Status has three values: OK (explicit success — the operation was successful), Error (explicit failure — ALWAYS set when you call span.recordException()), and Unset (the default — most spans end with Unset, meaning no explicit status was set; backends treat this as success). Always call span.setStatus(ERROR, "description") after span.recordException(error).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Status Values
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UNSET:  Default. Span completed, no explicit status set.       │
  │          → Backends treat as success                             │
  │  OK:     Explicit success. span.setStatus({code: OK})           │
  │  ERROR:  Explicit failure. span.setStatus({code: ERROR,         │
  │          message: "DB connection refused"})                      │
  │          → ALWAYS set after span.recordException()              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when a DB query fails, the span records the exception AND sets Status to ERROR with the failure message. Jaeger highlights ERROR spans in red, making failures instantly visible.

API & SDK Q43 of 200
What are the five Span Kinds and what does each represent?
A) CLIENT (outgoing synchronous call), SERVER (incoming synchronous call), INTERNAL (operation with no remote call), PRODUCER (sending an async message), CONSUMER (receiving an async message) — each tells the backend how to display and interpret the span
B) HTTP, gRPC, TCP, UDP, WebSocket — protocol-specific span kinds
C) SYNC, ASYNC, BATCH, STREAM, ONESHOT — execution pattern kinds
D) ROOT, CHILD, LEAF, BRANCH, LINK — tree-structure kinds
✅ Correct Answer: A

The five Span Kinds tell backends how to render the span in a trace waterfall: CLIENT (I called someone — outgoing HTTP/gRPC), SERVER (someone called me — incoming request handler), INTERNAL (pure computation, no network), PRODUCER (I sent a message to a queue), CONSUMER (I received a message from a queue). Correct Kind assignment is critical for accurate service maps and latency analysis.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Kinds — How Backends Interpret Your Spans
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Service A                         Service B                     │
  │  ┌──────────┐  CLIENT ───────────► SERVER  ┌──────────┐        │
  │  │ anihpj-  │  "I called B"      "B called│ anihpj-  │        │
  │  │ web      │       me"          │ api     │        │
  │  └──────────┘                      └──────────┘        │
  │                                     │                            │
  │                              INTERNAL │                           │
  │                               "business logic"                   │
  │                                     │                            │
  │                              ┌──────▼──────┐                    │
  │                              │  PRODUCER   │ ──► Kafka          │
  │                              │  "I sent a  │                    │
  │                              │   message"  │                    │
  │                              └─────────────┘                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django views are SERVER spans, the API calling PostgreSQL creates CLIENT spans (the Django app is the client to the DB), business logic is INTERNAL, and sending emails via Celery is PRODUCER.

API & SDK Q44 of 200
What fields make up the Metric data model?
A) Only a name and a value — metrics are simple key-value pairs
B) Timestamp, value, and unit — the three required fields for any measurement
C) Metric name, type (gauge/counter/histogram), and a list of data points
D) Name, Description, Unit, Instrument Kind (Counter/Histogram/Gauge/UpDownCounter), Aggregation Temporality (Cumulative/Delta), and Data Points (each with Attributes, StartTime, Time, Value, and optional Exemplars)
✅ Correct Answer: D

A Metric has: Name (e.g., http.server.duration), Description (human-readable), Unit (ms, By, {call}), Instrument Kind (Counter/Histogram/Gauge/UpDownCounter), Aggregation Temporality (Cumulative=total since start, Delta=change since last report), and Data Points — each with Attributes (dimensions), timestamps, value, and optional Exemplars linking to traces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metric Data Model
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric {                                                        │
  │    Name:        "http.server.duration"                           │
  │    Description: "Measures the duration of inbound HTTP requests" │
  │    Unit:        "ms"                                             │
  │    Instrument:  Histogram                                        │
  │    Temporality: Cumulative                                       │
  │    DataPoints: [                                                 │
  │      {attributes: {method:"GET", route:"/api/jobs", status:200},│
  │       sum: 45000, count: 150, buckets: [...]}                    │
  │    ]                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the http.server.duration Histogram tracks API latency with Cumulative temporality — Prometheus can compute rate, p50, p95, p99 from the bucket distribution.

API & SDK Q45 of 200
What fields make up the LogRecord data model?
A) Just a timestamp and a message string — logs are simple
B) Log level, message, and file/line number — standard logging fields
C) Timestamp, ObservedTimestamp, SeverityText, SeverityNumber (TRACE=1..FATAL=21), Body, Attributes, TraceId, SpanId, and Resource — combining traditional logging with OTel trace correlation
D) Only Body and Timestamp — OTel does not add any structure to logs
✅ Correct Answer: C

A LogRecord combines traditional logging (Timestamp, Severity, Body) with OTel correlation (TraceId, SpanId). The SeverityNumber uses numeric levels: TRACE=1, DEBUG=5, INFO=9, WARN=13, ERROR=17, FATAL=21. Having TraceId and SpanId on every log record means you can navigate from any log line directly to the trace that produced it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LogRecord — Logging + Trace Correlation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LogRecord {                                                     │
  │    Timestamp:        14:32:01.500                                │
  │    ObservedTimestamp: 14:32:01.510  ← When Collector received   │
  │    SeverityText:     "ERROR"                                     │
  │    SeverityNumber:   17                                          │
  │    Body:             "DB connection refused"                     │
  │    Attributes:       {db.system: "postgresql", retry: 3}        │
  │    TraceId:          "a1b2c3..."  ← Links to the trace!         │
  │    SpanId:           "1234567..."  ← Links to the span!         │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django error logs automatically carry TraceId and SpanId. Clicking any log in Grafana navigates directly to the trace waterfall in Jaeger.

API & SDK Q46 of 200
What is the Resource data model and why is it important?
A) Resource defines CPU/memory limits for telemetry processing
B) Resource describes the ENTITY producing telemetry — it includes service.name, service.namespace, service.instance.id, k8s.pod.name, host.name, cloud.region, and telemetry.sdk.* — and is attached to EVERY span, metric, and log record
C) Resource is only used for billing — tracking which team "owns" each span
D) Resource is an optional field that most applications don't need to configure
✅ Correct Answer: B

Resource is the "who" behind every piece of telemetry. It answers: which service? which namespace? which pod? which node? which cloud region? Resource attributes are attached to ALL telemetry from a given entity — every span, metric, and log automatically carries them. The most critical Resource attribute is service.name — without it, telemetry has no identity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource — Identity for ALL Telemetry
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Resource {                                                      │
  │    service.name:        "anihpj-api"    ← CRITICAL: who am I?  │
  │    service.namespace:   "production"                             │
  │    service.instance.id: "anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde"                │
  │    service.version:     "2.3.1"                                  │
  │    k8s.pod.name:        "anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde"                │
  │    k8s.namespace.name:  "anihpj"                                │
  │    host.name:           "anih"                                   │
  │    telemetry.sdk.language: "python"                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  │  ↓ ATTACHED TO: Every Span, Metric, and Log from this pod       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Resource attributes are set via OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=anihpj-api and auto-detected by the OTel SDK. Every span from the API carries these attributes automatically.

API & SDK Q47 of 200
How does the OTel data model map to the OTLP wire format?
A) The data model is serialized into Protocol Buffers (protobuf) for efficient binary transmission over gRPC, or JSON over HTTP — the protobuf definitions are part of the OTLP specification and ensure type safety across all languages
B) The data model is always sent as raw JSON regardless of transport
C) Each language SDK defines its own wire format — there is no standardization
D) The data model is sent as CSV for maximum compatibility with legacy systems
✅ Correct Answer: A

The OTel data model is serialized to Protocol Buffers (protobuf) for gRPC transport (high-performance binary) or JSON for HTTP transport (firewall-friendly). The OTLP protobuf definitions are part of the spec — they define exact message structures for Traces, Metrics, and Logs. This ensures that the Python SDK and the Java Collector ALWAYS speak the same language at the wire level.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Model → OTLP Wire Format
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Abstract Data Model             Wire Format (OTLP)              │
  │  ───────────────────             ────────────────────             │
  │  Span {TraceId, SpanId, ...}  →  protobuf: ExportTraceServiceReq│
  │  Metric {Name, DataPoints...} →  protobuf: ExportMetricsService │
  │  LogRecord {Body, Sev...}     →  protobuf: ExportLogsServiceReq │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Transport: gRPC (protobuf binary, :4317) OR HTTP (JSON, :4318) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Python SDK serializes spans to protobuf and sends them via gRPC to the Collector on port 4317. The Collector can re-export them as JSON/HTTP to backends that prefer that format.

API & SDK Q48 of 200
What are the stability guarantees for the OTel data model?
A) All parts of the data model are experimental and may change at any time
B) The data model is frozen — no changes are ever allowed once a version is released
C) Only tracing is stable; metrics and logs data models are experimental
D) OTel uses a maturity model: Stable (production-ready, no breaking changes), Mixed (some signals stable, others experimental), and Experimental (may change) — Traces and Metrics are Stable in most SDKs, Logs reached Stable recently, and the OTLP protocol itself is Stable
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTel uses clear stability levels: Stable APIs are production-ready with backward-compatibility guarantees. The Tracing data model and API have been stable since 2021. Metrics reached stable in 2023. Logs reached stable in 2024. The OTLP protocol (v1.0+) is stable. Always check opentelemetry.io/status for the current maturity of each component.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Stability — What's Production-Ready
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT              STATUS      SINCE                       │
  │  ─────────              ──────      ─────                       │
  │  Tracing API & SDK     Stable      2021                        │
  │  Metrics API & SDK     Stable      2023                        │
  │  Logs API & SDK        Stable      2024                        │
  │  OTLP Protocol v1.0+    Stable      2021                        │
  │  Semantic Conventions   Mixed (HTTP/DB stable, others evolving) │
  │  Collector              Stable      Ongoing                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the team can confidently build on OTel Tracing, Metrics, and Logs APIs — all are stable and will not introduce breaking changes in minor versions.

2.2 Composability and ExtensionQ49-Q58

API & SDK Q49 of 200
What does "composability" mean in OpenTelemetry?
A) The ability to compose multiple traces into a single visualization
B) The ability to mix and match components via well-defined interfaces — you can swap exporters (OTLP → Jaeger → Prometheus), samplers (AlwaysOn → TraceIdRatio → custom), and processors without changing your instrumentation code
C) The ability to compose OpenTelemetry YAML configuration files from multiple sources
D) A design pattern for composing spans into parent-child hierarchies
✅ Correct Answer: B

Composability is OTel's plugin architecture. Every component — SpanExporter, SpanProcessor, Sampler, TextMapPropagator — implements a well-defined interface. You can swap a ConsoleExporter for an OTLPExporter by changing ONE line of config. You can chain multiple SpanProcessors (enrich → filter → export). This means you instrument ONCE and can change WHERE data goes without touching your application code.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Composability — Swap Components Without Rewriting Code
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  YOUR INSTRUMENTATION CODE (unchanged)                           │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────                               │
  │  tracer.startSpan("operation")                                   │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  SpanProcessor (configurable)                                    │
  │  ├── BatchSpanProcessor                                         │
  │  │     └── SpanExporter (SWAPPABLE!)                             │
  │  │           ├── OTLPSpanExporter  ← Today: Jaeger              │
  │  │           ├── ConsoleSpanExporter ← Debug                    │
  │  │           └── CustomExporter    ← Tomorrow: Grafana Tempo   │
  │  └── CustomEnrichProcessor (adds business attributes)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the team initially exports to Jaeger. When they decide to add Grafana Tempo for long-term trace storage, they change the exporter configuration in ONE place — the instrumentation code doesn't change at all.

API & SDK Q50 of 200
What are the main plugin interfaces in the OpenTelemetry SDK?
A) PluginLoader, PluginConfig, PluginRegistry — a generic plugin system
B) There are no plugins — OTel is monolithic and cannot be extended
C) SpanExporter (where spans go), SpanProcessor (how spans are processed), Sampler (which spans to keep), TextMapPropagator (how context travels between services), MetricExporter, MetricReader, LogRecordExporter, and LogRecordProcessor — each with a clean interface you implement
D) Only SpanExporter and SpanProcessor — other components are not extensible
✅ Correct Answer: C

OTel defines 8+ plugin interfaces across traces, metrics, and logs. Each is a clean interface you implement to customize behavior: SpanExporter (send spans to a backend), SpanProcessor (enrich/filter/batch spans), Sampler (decide which traces to record), TextMapPropagator (inject/extract context from carriers), MetricExporter/MetricReader (export/collect metrics), LogRecordExporter/LogRecordProcessor (export/process logs). This composable design is why OTel fits ANY observability stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Plugin Interfaces — The Extension Points
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACES:                         METRICS:                        │
  │  ───────                         ────────                        │
  │  ✅ SpanExporter                 ✅ MetricExporter               │
  │  ✅ SpanProcessor                ✅ MetricReader                 │
  │  ✅ Sampler                      ✅ View (custom aggregation)    │
  │  ✅ TextMapPropagator                                           │
  │                                   LOGS:                          │
  │  CROSS-CUTTING:                   ────                           │
  │  ──────────────                   ✅ LogRecordExporter           │
  │  ✅ Resource Detector             ✅ LogRecordProcessor          │
  │  ✅ IdGenerator                                                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the team implements a CustomSpanProcessor that adds business attributes (employer.subscription_tier, job.category) to every span before export. This processor plugs into the pipeline without modifying any OTel library code.

API & SDK Q51 of 200
How would you write a custom SpanExporter in OpenTelemetry?
A) Modify the OTel SDK source code directly to add your backend
B) Implement the SpanExporter interface with export() and shutdown() methods — then register it with your SpanProcessor (e.g., BatchSpanProcessor) — OTel handles the rest via its plugin architecture
C) Write a YAML configuration file that OTel automatically detects and loads
D) Custom exporters are not supported — you must use one of the built-in exporters
✅ Correct Answer: B

Writing a custom SpanExporter is straightforward: implement the SpanExporter interface with export(spans) (called by the SDK with completed spans) and shutdown() (graceful cleanup). Register it with a SpanProcessor. The SDK calls your exporter automatically. This is how vendors build OTel-compatible backends without forking the SDK.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom SpanExporter — Implementing the Interface
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  class MyCustomExporter implements SpanExporter {                │
  │    export(spans) {                                               │
  │      // Send spans to your proprietary backend                   │
  │      return ExportResult.SUCCESS;                                │
  │    }                                                             │
  │    shutdown() { /* cleanup connections */ }                      │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  exporter = MyCustomExporter()                                   │
  │  processor = BatchSpanProcessor(exporter)                         │
  │  tracerProvider.add_span_processor(processor)                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: if the team needed to send spans to an internal data warehouse, they'd implement a CustomSpanExporter — no SDK modification needed.

API & SDK Q52 of 200
How would you implement a custom Sampler that keeps all errors but only 10% of successful traces?
A) Configure two separate TracerProviders — one for errors, one for success
B) This is not possible — samplers must treat all spans the same way
C) Implement the Sampler interface with shouldSample() — check the span's attributes/status at decision time: if ERROR status or exception event present → return RECORD_AND_SAMPLE; otherwise → use a TraceIdRatioBased sampler at 0.1 (10%)
D) Use the Collector's tail sampling processor instead — the SDK cannot do this
✅ Correct Answer: C

Custom Samplers implement shouldSample(context, traceId, name, kind, attributes, links) and return a SamplingResult. Your sampler can inspect the span name, attributes, or parent context to decide: "If this looks like an error, ALWAYS sample it. Otherwise, sample 10%." Head-based sampling happens at span CREATION time in the SDK.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Sampler — Smart Head-Based Sampling
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  class ErrorAwareSampler implements Sampler {                    │
  │    shouldSample(context, traceId, name, kind, attrs, links) {   │
  │      // Check if any attribute indicates an error                │
  │      // In practice, check parent span status or use heuristics │
  │      return SamplingResult.RECORD_AND_SAMPLE;  // Always keep   │
  │      // OR: return ratioBased.shouldSample(...)  // 10%         │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a custom sampler ensures 100% of failed job applications are traced, while only 10% of successful ones are kept — balancing cost and debugging power.

API & SDK Q53 of 200
What would you use a Custom SpanProcessor for?
A) Enriching spans with business context (add attributes), filtering out sensitive spans, redirecting specific spans to different exporters, or adding custom logic before spans are exported — all without modifying instrumentation code
B) SpanProcessors can only batch spans — no custom logic is allowed
C) Replacing the entire OTel SDK with a custom implementation
D) SpanProcessors are deprecated — use the Collector for all processing
✅ Correct Answer: A

Custom SpanProcessors sit between span creation and export. Use them to: add business attributes (span.setAttribute("tenant.id", tenantId)), filter out health-check spans, route spans to different exporters based on attributes, or redact PII from span data. They're the SDK-level equivalent of Collector processors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SpanProcessor Chain — Enrich → Filter → Export
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span created → EnrichProcessor → FilterProcessor → Exporter   │
  │                 (adds tenant.id,  (drops /healthz  (OTLP)      │
  │                  env=production)   spans)                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a CustomSpanProcessor adds employer.subscription_tier and job.category to every span automatically.

API & SDK Q54 of 200
What is a Custom TextMapPropagator used for?
A) TextMapPropagators format text for display in dashboards
B) They're only used internally by the SDK — not customizable
C) Supporting legacy or proprietary context propagation formats (e.g., B3, Jaeger, AWS X-Ray) by implementing inject() and extract() methods to read/write context from HTTP headers or message metadata
D) Converting span data to plain text for logging purposes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Custom TextMapPropagators implement inject(context, carrier, setter) and extract(context, carrier, getter) to read/write trace context from carriers (HTTP headers, gRPC metadata, message properties). Use them to support legacy formats (B3, Jaeger) or proprietary propagation while migrating to W3C TraceContext.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Propagator — Inject & Extract Context
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  inject(carrier):  Add traceparent header to HTTP request       │
  │  extract(carrier): Read traceparent from incoming HTTP request  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Custom propagators bridge OTel with legacy tracing systems  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: if a legacy service uses B3 propagation, a custom propagator ensures traces remain connected across the migration.

API & SDK Q55 of 200
What are instrumentation libraries in OpenTelemetry?
A) Libraries that replace the OTel SDK entirely
B) Reusable packages that add OTel tracing/metrics to specific frameworks — e.g., opentelemetry-instrumentation-django automatically traces Django views, opentelemetry-instrumentation-psycopg2 traces PostgreSQL queries
C) GUI libraries for building observability dashboards
D) Libraries for instrumenting musical instruments in the context of OpenTelemetry
✅ Correct Answer: B

Instrumentation libraries are plug-and-play packages: install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django, and every Django view is automatically traced with HTTP semantic conventions. They use monkey-patching or hooks to inject OTel into framework internals — the application code doesn't change.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Django, Flask, FastAPI, Express, Spring Boot, Gin              │
  │  PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB, Redis, Elasticsearch               │
  │  gRPC, Kafka, RabbitMQ, AWS SDK, requests, urllib3              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: django + psycopg2 + redis instrumentation libraries cover the entire stack with zero manual code.

API & SDK Q57 of 200
How does OpenTelemetry handle version compatibility?
A) All versions are always backward-compatible — no breaking changes ever
B) Each language SDK is completely independent with its own versioning
C) OTel uses a single version number across all components
D) OTel follows semantic versioning with clear stability guarantees: Stable APIs (1.x) have no breaking changes in minor versions, the API and SDK are versioned separately, and instrumentation libraries declare their API/SDK dependency range
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTel uses strict semver: API and SDK are versioned separately. Instrumentation libraries declare compatible ranges (e.g., opentelemetry-api >=1.0, <2.0). Stable components never break in minor versions. Experimental components may change. This allows mixing SDK v1.28 with instrumentation v1.25 safely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Versioning
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  opentelemetry-api == 1.28.0  (Stable — no breaking changes)    │
  │  opentelemetry-sdk == 1.28.0  (Stable — matches API version)    │
  │  opentelemetry-instrumentation-django == 0.45b0 (Experimental)  │
  │  opentelemetry-exporter-otlp == 1.28.0 (Stable)                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the team pins API/SDK at 1.x and can safely update instrumentation libraries independently.

API & SDK Q58 of 200
How would you build a custom instrumentation library for anihpj/jobpost business logic?
A) Fork the OTel SDK and add anihpj-specific code to the core
B) Create a Python package that uses the OTel API to define custom spans ("matchCandidateToJob"), custom metrics ("job.match.score"), and custom attributes ("employer.tier") — then publish it so all anihpj services can import and use it consistently
C) Write configuration files that the Collector uses to auto-instrument the app
D) Custom instrumentation libraries are not supported — use manual instrumentation in each service
✅ Correct Answer: B

A custom instrumentation library exposes pre-built spans, metrics, and attributes for your domain. Instead of every developer writing tracer.startSpan("matchCandidate"), they call anihpj_otel.match_candidate(job, candidate) — which creates the span with correct semantic conventions, attributes, and metrics automatically. This ensures consistency across all services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Custom Instrumentation Library
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # anihpj_otel/instrumentation.py                                │
  │  def match_candidate(job_id, candidate_id):                      │
  │      span = tracer.start_span("matchCandidateToJob")             │
  │      span.set_attribute("job.id", job_id)                        │
  │      span.set_attribute("candidate.id", candidate_id)            │
  │      return span  # Caller uses as context manager               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the custom library ensures every service tags spans with job.id, employer.tier, and job.category consistently — no copy-paste, no drift.

API & SDK Q59 of 200
How do you configure the TracerProvider in OpenTelemetry?
A) Edit a global YAML file at /etc/otel/tracing.yaml
B) Create a TracerProvider with resource attributes, add SpanProcessors (BatchSpanProcessor with OTLP exporter), and set it as the global provider via trace.set_tracer_provider()
C) TracerProvider is auto-configured — no manual setup is needed
D) Configure it exclusively through environment variables
✅ Correct Answer: B

TracerProvider setup: create a Resource with service.name, create a TracerProvider with that resource, add SpanProcessors (typically BatchSpanProcessor wrapping an OTLPSpanExporter), and set it globally. This is typically done ONCE at application startup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TracerProvider Configuration
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  resource = Resource.create({                                    │
  │    "service.name": "anihpj-api"                                 │
  │  })                                                              │
  │  provider = TracerProvider(resource=resource)                    │
  │  provider.add_span_processor(                                    │
  │    BatchSpanProcessor(OTLPSpanExporter(                          │
  │      endpoint="http://collector:4317")))                         │
  │  trace.set_tracer_provider(provider)  ← Global for all tracers   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the TracerProvider is configured in otel_config.py at Django startup, setting service name and OTLP endpoint.

API & SDK Q60 of 200
How do you configure the MeterProvider for metrics collection?
A) MeterProvider uses the same configuration as TracerProvider automatically
B) Metrics don't need a provider — they work out of the box
C) MeterProvider only requires a service name — everything else is automatic
D) Create a MeterProvider, add MetricReaders (PeriodicExportingMetricReader with OTLP exporter for push, or Prometheus exporter for pull), set export intervals, and configure Views for custom aggregation
✅ Correct Answer: D

MeterProvider configuration: create the provider, add MetricReaders (one per backend), configure export intervals (export_interval_millis=30000 for 30s), and optionally add Views for custom aggregation. Each reader can export to a different backend simultaneously.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MeterProvider — Multiple Readers, Multiple Backends
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  provider = MeterProvider(resource=resource,                     │
  │    metric_readers=[                                              │
  │      PeriodicExportingMetricReader(OTLPMetricExporter(), 30_000),│
  │      PeriodicExportingMetricReader(PrometheusExporter(), 60_000) │
  │    ])                                                            │
  │  metrics.set_meter_provider(provider)                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the MeterProvider exports metrics every 30s via OTLP AND exposes a /metrics endpoint for Prometheus scraping — both from one provider.

API & SDK Q61 of 200
How do you configure the LoggerProvider?

B) LoggerProvider doesn't exist — OTel doesn't have a log signal yet
