For anihpj/jobpost: Django's built-in Python logging is bridged to OTel — every log line automatically gets TraceId and SpanId via the LoggerProvider.

API & SDK Q62 of 200
How does OpenTelemetry use environment variables for configuration?
A) Environment variables are only used for debugging — not for production configuration
B) OTel defines standard env vars: OTEL_SERVICE_NAME (service identity), OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT (Collector URL), OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES (key=value pairs), OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER (otlp/console/none), OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER, OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER, OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER, OTEL_PROPAGATORS — all SDKs respect these
C) OTel uses a single OTEL_CONFIG environment variable pointing to a YAML file
D) Environment variable configuration is deprecated — use code-only configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

OTel uses standardized OTEL_* environment variables across ALL languages. Key vars: OTEL_SERVICE_NAME (who am I?), OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT (where to send?), OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES (extra identity), OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER (sampling strategy). This means you can configure OTel without changing code — perfect for Kubernetes deployments where env vars are set in the pod spec.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Key OTel Environment Variables
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=anihpj-api                                   │
  │  OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4317         │
  │  OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES=deployment.environment=production     │
  │  OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=parentbased_traceidratio                   │
  │  OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG=0.1  (10%)                             │
  │  OTEL_PROPAGATORS=tracecontext,baggage                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: all OTel config is set via Kubernetes env vars in the Deployment YAML — no code changes needed to switch endpoints or sampling rates.

API & SDK Q64 of 200
How do you configure which exporter to use via environment variables?
A) Exporters cannot be configured via environment — only code
B) Set OTEL_EXPORTER=jaeger to choose Jaeger as the backend
C) OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp, OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp, OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp — each signal has its own env var; values: otlp (default), console (debug), none (disable)
D) All signals share OTEL_EXPORTER — you cannot choose per-signal
✅ Correct Answer: C

Each signal has its own exporter env var: OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER, OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER, OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER. Values: otlp (default, sends to OTLP endpoint), console (prints to stdout for debugging), none (disables). You can set traces=otlp and metrics=console independently.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp      → Traces to Collector           │

  │  OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp         → Logs to Collector            │

For anihpj/jobpost: traces go to OTLP (Collector), metrics are exposed for Prometheus, logs use OTLP — each configured independently.

API & SDK Q65 of 200
How do you configure the sampling strategy via environment?
A) OTEL_SAMPLE_RATE=0.1 — a single variable controls everything
B) Sampling cannot be configured via environment — only code
C) OTEL_SAMPLING_ENABLED=true/false — binary on/off only
D) OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=parentbased_traceidratio with OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG=0.1 — values include always_on, always_off, traceidratio, parentbased_always_on, parentbased_traceidratio, jaeger_remote, xray
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER sets the strategy: always_on/always_off (binary), traceidratio (e.g., 0.1 = 10%), parentbased_* variants (respect parent's sampling decision). OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG provides the parameter (ratio value). The best practice is parentbased_traceidratio — a child span is always sampled if its parent was.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sampler Environment Configuration
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=parentbased_traceidratio                   │
  │  OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG=0.1  ← 10% of root spans              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Effect: 10% of new traces sampled, ALL child spans of sampled  │
  │          traces are also sampled (no broken traces!)             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: 10% sampling in production with parentbased ensures complete traces, not fragmented ones.

API & SDK Q66 of 200
How do you configure context propagation formats?
A) OTel only supports W3C TraceContext — no other formats
B) OTEL_PROPAGATORS=tracecontext,baggage,b3 — comma-separated list of propagators; the SDK injects/extracts all listed formats, ensuring compatibility with services using different propagation standards
C) Each service must use exactly one propagation format
D) Propagation format is auto-detected from incoming headers
✅ Correct Answer: B

OTEL_PROPAGATORS accepts comma-separated propagator names: tracecontext (W3C, default), baggage (W3C Baggage), b3 (Zipkin), b3multi, jaeger, xray, ottrace. Multiple propagators can be active simultaneously — the SDK tries each for extraction and injects all for outgoing requests.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple Propagators — Extract Any, Inject All
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTEL_PROPAGATORS=tracecontext,baggage,b3                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Incoming: Can read B3 headers (legacy) AND W3C (modern)        │
  │  Outgoing: Injects ALL three formats for maximum compatibility  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: uses tracecontext+baggage for modern services, with b3 for backward compatibility during migration.

B) OTel SDK cannot be configured in code — only environment variables

API & SDK Q69 of 200
What is the configuration precedence in OpenTelemetry?
A) Environment variables always override code configuration
B) Code (programmatic) > File config > Environment variables > Defaults — code has highest priority, defaults are the fallback. This lets developers override in code while ops teams set defaults via env vars
C) All sources are merged equally with no precedence
D) File configuration always overrides everything else
✅ Correct Answer: B

Configuration precedence: Code > File > Env > Defaults. If you set OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=always_off on the pod but the code calls setSampler(AlwaysOn()), code wins. This prevents environment misconfiguration from breaking critical instrumentation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Configuration Precedence Pyramid
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔴 Code (Highest) — Developer intent                            │
  │  🟡 File Config — Declarative, version-controlled              │
  │  🟢 Environment — Ops-controlled, deployment-specific          │
  │  ⚪ Defaults (Lowest) — SDK built-in defaults                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the team keeps sampling logic in code (always sample errors) while the ops team adjusts the OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT via env vars per environment.

anihpj SDK Configuration — Hybrid Approach

2.4 Signals — TracingQ71-Q86

API & SDK Q71 of 200
What is a Trace in OpenTelemetry?
A) A log file that records every HTTP request to the server
B) A directed acyclic graph of Spans that represents the complete journey of a single request through a distributed system — all spans share one Trace ID
C) A metric that measures the total time taken by an operation
D) A configuration file that defines how tracing should work in the Collector
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Trace is a tree of Spans — one Trace ID links ALL spans from the moment a request enters the system until the final response. Think of it as a "movie" of your request: Web span (200ms) → API span (150ms) → DB span (45ms) → Redis span (5ms). Every frame (span) is part of the same movie (trace).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trace = A Tree of Spans with One Trace ID
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE ID: abc123 (Shared by ALL spans)                         │
  │  ├── Span A: Web (SERVER, 200ms)                                │
  │  │   └── Span B: API (SERVER, 150ms)                            │
  │  │       ├── Span C: DB Query (CLIENT, 45ms)                    │
  │  │       └── Span D: Redis (CLIENT, 5ms)                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a "Submit Job Application" click creates one trace spanning Web → API → PostgreSQL → Redis → Celery — all linked by one Trace ID.

API & SDK Q72 of 200
What is a Span in OpenTelemetry?
A) A named, timed operation representing a single unit of work — the building block of traces, with a name, start/end time, attributes, events, and status
B) The time between two HTTP requests
C) A configuration setting that controls how long tracing data is retained
D) A network packet that carries trace information between services
✅ Correct Answer: A

A Span is the fundamental unit of work in tracing — one named, timed operation. Each span captures: what happened (name), when (start/end time), context (attributes), and outcome (status). Spans form a tree: parent spans contain child spans, creating the trace waterfall.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span = One Unit of Work
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span { Name: "POST /api/jobs/search", Kind: SERVER,            │
  │         Start: 14:32:01.000, End: 14:32:01.500, Duration: 500ms│
  │         Attributes: {http.method:"POST", http.route:"/api/..."} │
  │         Status: OK }                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every Django view is a SERVER span, every DB query is a CLIENT span, every Redis command is a CLIENT span.

API & SDK Q73 of 200
What is a Trace ID and what are its characteristics?
A) A 4-byte integer that increments with each new trace
B) A human-readable string like "trace-2024-001"
C) A 16-byte (32 hex character) globally unique identifier that links ALL spans belonging to the same distributed trace — randomly generated, not sequential
D) The IP address of the server that originated the trace
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Trace ID is a 128-bit (16-byte) globally unique identifier, typically displayed as 32 hex characters (e.g., a1b2c3d4e5f6a7b8c9d0e1f2a3b4c5d6). It's randomly generated at the trace root and propagated to ALL child spans. The W3C TraceContext standard defines the Trace ID format.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trace ID — The Glue That Holds Spans Together
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Trace ID: a1b2c3d4e5f6a7b8c9d0e1f2a3b4c5d6                    │
  │  ├── Span ID: 1234567890abcdef  (Web, parent=null)              │
  │  │   └── Span ID: fedcba0987654321 (API, parent=1234...)       │
  │  │       ├── Span ID: aabbccddeeff0011 (DB, parent=fedc...)    │
  │  │       └── Span ID: 1122334455667788 (Redis, parent=fedc...) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every "Submit Application" request generates a unique Trace ID that follows the request through all 5 services.

API & SDK Q74 of 200
What is a Span ID and how does it differ from a Trace ID?
A) Span ID and Trace ID are the same — the terms are interchangeable
B) A Span ID is an 8-byte (16 hex char) identifier unique within a trace — it identifies ONE span, while the Trace ID identifies ALL spans in the trace
C) Span ID is shorter (4 bytes) and is only used internally within the SDK
D) Span ID is the human-readable name of the span, like "POST /api/jobs"
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Span ID is 64 bits (8 bytes, 16 hex chars), unique within a single trace. Multiple spans in one trace share the SAME Trace ID but each has a UNIQUE Span ID. The Span ID identifies "this specific operation" while the Trace ID identifies "this entire request journey."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trace ID vs Span ID
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Trace ID: a1b2...c5d6  ← SAME for ALL spans in this trace     │
  │  ├── Span ID: 1234...cdef  ← UNIQUE to this span (Web)        │
  │  │   └── Span ID: fedc...4321  ← UNIQUE to this span (API)    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Web span and the DB span share the same Trace ID but have different Span IDs — together they uniquely identify each operation in the trace tree.

API & SDK Q75 of 200
What is the Parent Span ID and how does it create the span hierarchy?
A) Parent Span ID is the Span ID of the span that created this span — it links child spans to their parent, creating the tree structure of a trace. A root span has no Parent Span ID (null/empty)
B) Parent Span ID is always the same as the Trace ID
C) Parent Span ID is a human-readable label like "api-service"
D) Parent Span ID is optional — spans don't need parent references
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Parent Span ID creates the span TREE: each span records the Span ID of its parent. The root span (first span in the trace) has no parent. When the API span creates a DB child span, the DB span's ParentSpanId = the API span's SpanId. This is how Jaeger/Grafana render the waterfall.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Parent Span ID Creates the Tree
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span A: SpanId=AAA, ParentSpanId=null  ← ROOT                 │
  │  Span B: SpanId=BBB, ParentSpanId=AAA   ← Child of A           │
  │  Span C: SpanId=CCC, ParentSpanId=BBB   ← Child of B           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django view span (root, no parent) creates the DB query span (child, parent=view's SpanId).

API & SDK Q76 of 200
What is a Root Span?
A) The span with the shortest duration in a trace
B) A span created by the system administrator for debugging
C) The span that contains the most child spans
D) The FIRST span in a trace — where the request entered the system. It has no parent (ParentSpanId is null) and is typically a SERVER span representing the initial incoming request
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Root Span is the trace's entry point — the first span created when a request enters your system. It has no ParentSpanId. All other spans in the trace are descendants of the root. The root span's start time is the trace start time; its duration plus all descendants equals the total trace duration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Root Span = Entry Point
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  External Request ──► Root Span (Django View)                   │
  │                        ├── Child Span (DB Query)                │
  │                        └── Child Span (Redis)                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django view span is the root — it's the first span created when an HTTP request arrives. All DB, Redis, and Celery spans descend from it.

API & SDK Q77 of 200
What are TraceFlags in OpenTelemetry?
A) A set of boolean flags indicating which programming language created the trace
B) Configuration flags that control how traces are displayed in Jaeger
C) An 8-bit field in the SpanContext — bit 0 (the SAMPLED flag) indicates whether this trace is being recorded and should be exported to backends
D) Flags that determine the trace retention period in storage
✅ Correct Answer: C

TraceFlags is an 8-bit field in the W3C traceparent header. The least significant bit (bit 0) is the SAMPLED flag: 1 = this trace is being recorded and should be exported, 0 = not sampled (do not export). The sampled flag is set by the Sampler at trace creation and propagated to all child spans.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TraceFlags in traceparent Header
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  traceparent: 00-a1b2...c5d6-1234...cdef-01                    │
  │                                              ││                 │
  │                                              TraceFlags=01      │
  │                                              Bit 0 = sampled ✅ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when the root span is sampled, TraceFlags=01 propagates to all child spans, ensuring the entire trace is exported together.

API & SDK Q78 of 200
What is TraceState and how is it used?
A) TraceState stores the current state of a trace (active, complete, error) for the SDK
B) TraceState is vendor-specific key-value data propagated alongside TraceContext — vendors (AWS, Google, Datadog) insert their own tenant IDs, sampling rates, or debug flags without affecting the standard traceparent
C) TraceState is a deprecated field replaced by SpanContext attributes
D) TraceState stores the geographic location where the trace originated
✅ Correct Answer: B

TraceState is carried in the W3C tracestate header. It contains vendor-specific key-value pairs: vendor1=opaqueValue1,vendor2=opaqueValue2. Example: aws=arn:aws:xray:us-east-1:123456:...;dd=s:2;o:rum. Each vendor can read/write their own entries without interfering with others.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TraceState — Vendor-Specific Context
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  tracestate: dd=s:2;o:rum,aws=xray:arn:...,gz=composer:1       │
  │              │            │                  │                   │
  │              Datadog      AWS X-Ray         Google Cloud        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: if using AWS X-Ray alongside OTel, TraceState carries X-Ray specific context without affecting the standard OTel trace.

API & SDK Q79 of 200
How do you get a Tracer in the OTel SDK?
A) tracer = trace.get_tracer("my-service", "1.0.0") — returns a Tracer instance scoped to your service name and version, obtained from the globally configured TracerProvider
B) tracer = new Tracer() — creates a tracer directly without a provider
C) Import tracer from opentelemetry.sdk.tracing — it's a global singleton
D) Tracers are created automatically — no code needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Getting a Tracer: tracer = trace.get_tracer("service-name", "version"). The name and version become part of the instrumentation scope. All spans created by this tracer share the same instrumentation scope. The tracer is obtained from the global TracerProvider set at startup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Getting a Tracer
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  from opentelemetry import trace                                 │
  │  tracer = trace.get_tracer("anihpj-api", "2.3.1")               │
  │  # All spans from this tracer are tagged with these values       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: each service (web, api, worker) gets its own tracer with its own name and version.

API & SDK Q80 of 200
How do you create and end a Span?
A) Spans are created automatically — you never call startSpan directly
B) span = Span(name); span.finish() — using the Span constructor
C) Create spans via the Collector configuration, not in code
D) span = tracer.start_span("operation-name"); try: ... finally: span.end() — always end spans in a finally block to prevent leaks. Use context managers for automatic cleanup
✅ Correct Answer: D

Create a span with tracer.start_span("name") and ALWAYS call span.end() — ideally in a finally block or using a context manager (with tracer.start_as_current_span("name"):). Unclosed spans leak memory and create broken traces. The context manager pattern auto-closes the span and manages the active context.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Lifecycle — Create → Use → End
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Manual (explicit end)                                         │
  │  span = tracer.start_span("process_application")                │
  │  try:                                                            │
  │      # ... do work ...                                           │
  │  finally:                                                        │
  │      span.end()  # ALWAYS end the span                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # Context manager (auto-end)                                    │
  │  with tracer.start_as_current_span("process_application"):       │
  │      # ... do work ...                                           │
  │  # Span auto-ended when exiting the 'with' block                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: business logic spans use context managers for automatic cleanup — no risk of unclosed spans.

API & SDK Q81 of 200
How do you set attributes on a Span?
A) span.attributes["key"] = "value" — dictionary-style assignment
B) span.set_attribute("http.method", "GET") — key-value pairs added AFTER span creation. Attributes must be set before span.end(); they become immutable after the span ends
C) Attributes are passed to start_span() and cannot be changed later
D) Set attributes via environment variables — not in code
✅ Correct Answer: B

Set attributes with span.set_attribute(key, value). Attributes are key-value metadata: strings, numbers, booleans, or arrays of these. They must be set BEFORE span.end() — after the span ends, attributes are immutable. Follow semantic conventions: http.method, db.system, user.id.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Setting Span Attributes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  span.set_attribute("http.method", "POST")                      │
  │  span.set_attribute("http.status_code", 201)                    │
  │  span.set_attribute("job.id", job_id)                           │
  │  span.set_attribute("user.role", "employer")                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every span gets business attributes like job.id, employer.tier, and user.role before ending.

API & SDK Q82 of 200
How do you add Span Events?
A) span.log("event name") — using the deprecated log method
B) Events are automatically added — no API call needed
C) span.add_event("cache.miss", attributes={"key": "job:123"}) — adds a timestamped annotation with optional attributes to the span's timeline
D) Events are created via the Collector, not the SDK
✅ Correct Answer: C

Span Events are timestamped annotations: span.add_event(name, attributes={...}). Use them for: cache hits/misses, retry attempts, queue messages received, milestone markers. Each event gets the current timestamp automatically. Events are displayed on the span timeline in Jaeger/Grafana.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Events on the Timeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  span.add_event("cache.miss", {"key": "jobs:search:tech"})     │
  │  span.add_event("db.query.start", {"table": "jobs"})           │
  │  span.add_event("db.query.end", {"rows": 42})                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the search span records cache miss, DB query start/end, and result count as events — giving granular timeline visibility.

API & SDK Q83 of 200
How do you record exceptions on a Span?
A) span.record_exception(error) — then span.set_status(Status(StatusCode.ERROR, str(error))). record_exception creates an event with exception.type, exception.message, and exception.stacktrace; set_status marks the span as failed
B) Just let the exception propagate — OTel catches it automatically
C) span.set_attribute("error", True) — a simple boolean flag
D) Write the exception to a log file — spans don't track exceptions
✅ Correct Answer: A

Recording exceptions is a two-step process: span.record_exception(error) creates a Span Event with exception.type, exception.message, and exception.stacktrace. Then span.set_status(StatusCode.ERROR, description) marks the span status as ERROR. Both are needed: the event captures details, the status enables filtering for all failed spans.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Recording Exceptions on Spans
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  try:                                                            │
  │      result = db.execute(query)                                  │
  │  except Exception as e:                                          │
  │      span.record_exception(e)  # Event with full stacktrace     │
  │      span.set_status(StatusCode.ERROR, "DB query failed")       │
  │      raise                                                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: failed DB queries record the exception AND set ERROR status — Jaeger highlights these spans in red for instant visual identification.

API & SDK Q84 of 200
How is Span timing calculated?
A) The SDK estimates timing based on CPU cycles
B) Each span records StartTime (when created) and EndTime (when span.end() is called) — Duration = EndTime - StartTime, measured with nanosecond precision using monotonic clocks
C) Timing is measured by the Collector, not the SDK
D) Duration is passed manually as a parameter to span.end()
✅ Correct Answer: B

Span timing is automatic: StartTime is captured when tracer.start_span() is called; EndTime is captured when span.end() is called. Duration = EndTime - StartTime, measured with nanosecond precision. The SDK uses monotonic clocks (not wall clock) to avoid clock skew issues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Timing — Automatic and Precise
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  StartTime = now()  ← Captured at span creation                │
  │  // ... work happens ...                                         │
  │  EndTime = now()    ← Captured at span.end()                   │
  │  Duration = 395ms   ← EndTime - StartTime                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every Django request span automatically captures timing with nanosecond precision — no manual timing code needed.

API & SDK Q85 of 200
How do nested spans work in anihpj/jobpost?
A) The API handler span is the parent; when it calls PostgreSQL, a child span is created with ParentSpanId = API span's SpanId; when it calls Redis, another child is created. The trace waterfall shows: API handler → DB query → Redis cache → Response — all nested
B) All spans are flat — there is no parent-child relationship
C) Nested spans require manual parent ID assignment for every child
D) Only two levels of nesting are supported by the OTel SDK
✅ Correct Answer: A

Nesting is automatic via context: when you create a span while another span is "active" (current), the new span automatically becomes a child. The API handler span is active → DB query span becomes child → Redis span becomes another child. The trace waterfall renders this as a tree: API (parent) → DB (child) + Redis (child).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Nested Spans in anihpj Trace
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  API Handler (SERVER, 150ms) ← Parent                           │
  │  ├── DB Query (CLIENT, 45ms) ← Child, ParentSpanId = API's ID  │
  │  └── Redis Cache (CLIENT, 5ms) ← Child, ParentSpanId = API's ID│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django view span is the parent; DB and Redis spans are children — the waterfall shows the full request hierarchy automatically.

API & SDK Q86 of 200
How does auto-instrumentation create spans for Django, DB, and Redis?
A) It uses a single span for all operations
B) It requires manual span creation for each operation
C) It only creates spans for HTTP — DB and Redis need manual instrumentation
D) Django auto-instrumentation wraps the WSGI handler to create a SERVER span for each request; psycopg2 instrumentation wraps the database driver to create CLIENT spans for each query; Redis instrumentation wraps redis-py to create CLIENT spans for each command — all with proper parent-child nesting via context
✅ Correct Answer: D

Each instrumentation library monkey-patches or hooks into its target: Django hooks into the WSGI request handler, psycopg2 wraps execute(), redis-py wraps execute_command(). Each creates the appropriate span Kind (SERVER for incoming HTTP, CLIENT for outgoing DB/Redis calls) with correct semantic conventions — all automatically nested via OTel context propagation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auto-Instrumentation Span Creation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Django WSGI ──► SERVER span (auto)                             │
  │    psycopg2.execute() ──► CLIENT span (auto, child of Django)   │
  │    redis.execute_command() ──► CLIENT span (auto, child of Django)│
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 All spans created automatically with zero manual code       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: install three packages (django, psycopg2, redis instrumentation) and every request is fully traced — no developer-written spans needed for the happy path.

2.5 Signals — MetricsQ87-Q98

API & SDK Q87 of 200
What is a Metric in OpenTelemetry?
A) A log entry that records how long an operation took
B) A measurement about a service captured at runtime — it has a name, instrument kind (Counter/Histogram/Gauge/UpDownCounter), optional attributes (dimensions), temporality (cumulative/delta), and data points with values
C) A configuration value that controls the sampling rate of traces
D) A trace span that has been converted to a numeric value for analysis
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Metric is a numeric measurement about your service: how many requests? how fast? how many errors? Each metric has a name (e.g., http.server.duration), an instrument kind (what TYPE of measurement), attributes (dimensions to slice by), and temporality (cumulative total or delta change). Metrics are aggregated and cost-effective — you can keep months of metric data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metric = Named Measurement + Type + Dimensions
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric: http.server.duration (Histogram, ms, Cumulative)       │
  │  ├── {method:"GET", route:"/api/jobs", status:"200"}            │
  │  │   count:150, sum:45000, min:20, max:3200                     │
  │  ├── {method:"POST", route:"/api/jobs", status:"201"}           │
  │  │   count:42, sum:16800, min:30, max:500                       │
  │  └── {method:"GET", route:"/api/jobs/{id}", status:"404"}       │
  │      count:3, sum:450, min:100, max:200                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: metrics track job applications per minute, API latency distribution, DB connection pool usage, and Redis cache hit ratios.

API & SDK Q88 of 200
What is a Counter in OpenTelemetry metrics?
A) A metric that can go up and down, tracking current values
B) A metric that measures distribution of values across buckets
C) A monotonic instrument that only increases — used for counting requests, errors, completed jobs, etc. Counter.Add(1) increments by 1; it can never decrease
D) A point-in-time snapshot of a value like CPU usage
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Counter is monotonic (only goes UP). Use it for: total HTTP requests, total errors, total job applications, total bytes sent. Default aggregation: Sum. In Prometheus, Counters appear as ever-increasing lines — use rate() to see per-second rates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Counter — Always Goes UP
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  counter = meter.createCounter("job.applications.total")        │
  │  counter.Add(1, {"status": "submitted", "job.category": "tech"})│
  │  counter.Add(1, {"status": "submitted", "job.category": "health"})│
  │  // Counter is now 2 (never 1, never -1)                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a Counter tracks total job applications, total user registrations, and total emails sent — all monotonically increasing.

API & SDK Q89 of 200
What is an UpDownCounter?
A) A Counter that increments by more than 1 at a time
B) A non-monotonic instrument that can go UP and DOWN — used for values that fluctuate: active connections, queue depth, in-flight requests, database pool usage
C) A Counter that counts in reverse (decrements by default)
D) A Counter combined with a Gauge — a hybrid instrument type
✅ Correct Answer: B

UpDownCounter is non-monotonic: counter.Add(1) increases, counter.Add(-1) decreases. Use for: active.connections, queue.depth, db.pool.usage, in-flight operations. Default aggregation: Sum. Unlike a Gauge (point-in-time), UpDownCounter reports accumulated changes between exports.

📝 Detailed Explanation
UpDownCounter — Goes UP and DOWN
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  pool_counter = meter.create_up_down_counter("db.pool.usage")   │
  │  pool_counter.Add(1)   # Connection opened → +1                 │
  │  pool_counter.Add(-1)  # Connection closed → -1                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: an UpDownCounter tracks the active PostgreSQL connection pool — going up when connections open, down when they close.

API & SDK Q90 of 200
What is a Histogram in OpenTelemetry?
A) A bar chart displayed in Grafana
B) A Counter that counts values in ranges
C) A Gauge that shows historical data points
D) An instrument that records values into predefined buckets (e.g., 0-5ms, 5-10ms, 10-25ms...) — enables computing p50, p95, p99, and average from the bucket distribution
✅ Correct Answer: D

A Histogram records values into buckets. Recording histogram.Record(145) increments the count in the bucket containing 145ms. From the bucket distribution, backends compute: count (total observations), sum, min, max, and percentiles (p50, p95, p99). This is THE most important metric instrument — use it for latency, payload size, and any distribution.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Histogram Buckets — Distribution Analysis
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Buckets: [0, 5, 10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000] ms          │
  │  Record(45)  → increments bucket [25-50]                        │
  │  Record(120) → increments bucket [100-250]                      │
  │  Record(8)   → increments bucket [5-10]                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  From distribution: p50=45ms, p95=120ms, p99=320ms              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the API latency histogram is the most critical metric — it enables SLO-based alerting on p99 latency.

API & SDK Q91 of 200
What is a Gauge in OpenTelemetry?
A) A point-in-time instrument capturing the CURRENT value — used for CPU %, memory bytes, temperature, queue size. Unlike Counters/Histograms, Gauges don't aggregate over time; they represent the value RIGHT NOW
B) A Counter that measures pressure in a system
C) A deprecated instrument replaced by UpDownCounter
D) A visual gauge displayed on monitoring dashboards
✅ Correct Answer: A

A Gauge represents a value at a point in time: CPU usage=73%, memory=8.2GB, active connections=42. Unlike Counters (which accumulate), a Gauge's value can go up, down, or stay the same — it's a snapshot. Default aggregation: LastValue (the most recent value). Common uses: resource utilization, queue size, temperature sensors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gauge — Point-in-Time Snapshot
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  cpu_gauge = meter.create_gauge("cpu.usage", unit="%")         │
  │  cpu_gauge.set(73.5)  # CPU is 73.5% RIGHT NOW                  │
  │  cpu_gauge.set(45.2)  # Now it's 45.2%                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a Gauge tracks the PostgreSQL connection pool size — a point-in-time value that fluctuates with traffic.

API & SDK Q92 of 200
What is the difference between synchronous and asynchronous metric instruments?
A) Synchronous instruments use HTTP; asynchronous use gRPC
B) There is no difference — the terms are interchangeable
C) Synchronous: value recorded at call time (counter.Add(1) when the event happens). Asynchronous: value collected via callback when the SDK polls (gauge observer reading current CPU from /proc/stat)
D) Synchronous instruments are deprecated — use only asynchronous
✅ Correct Answer: C

Synchronous instruments record values at call time: counter.Add(1) when a request arrives. Asynchronous instruments use callbacks: the SDK periodically calls your function to get the current value (e.g., reading /proc/stat for CPU). Async is ideal for values you can't push on every change — system metrics, sensor readings, queue depths.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sync vs Async Instruments
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SYNC: counter.Add(1)  ← You push when event happens            │
  │  ASYNC: callback() → returns 73.5  ← SDK pulls periodically     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: job applications use synchronous Counters (push on submit); CPU usage uses asynchronous Gauge (pull from OS).

API & SDK Q93 of 200
What is the difference between Cumulative and Delta metric temporality?
A) Cumulative is for traces; Delta is for metrics
B) Cumulative reports the TOTAL since process start (like an odometer); Delta reports the CHANGE since the last report (like a trip meter). Prometheus prefers Cumulative; many push-based systems prefer Delta
C) They are the same thing — different SDKs use different names
D) Delta is deprecated in favor of Cumulative
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cumulative: value = total since process start (Counter=15000 total requests). Delta: value = change since last report (Counter=+200 requests in last 30s). Prometheus uses Cumulative and computes rate on its own. OTLP supports both; the Collector can convert between them using the cumulativetodelta processor.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cumulative vs Delta Temporality
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Time:    0s      30s      60s      90s                         │
  │  Value:   100     250      400      600  ← Cumulative (odometer)│
  │  Delta:   +100    +150     +150     +200  ← Change each interval│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the SDK exports Cumulative counters; Prometheus scrapes and computes rate() for per-second rates.

API & SDK Q94 of 200
What is metric aggregation in OpenTelemetry?
A) Combining multiple metrics into a single value for storage
B) The process of deleting old metrics from the backend
C) How the SDK combines individual measurements into exportable data — defaults: Counter→Sum, Histogram→ExplicitBucketHistogram, Gauge→LastValue, UpDownCounter→Sum. Views can override these defaults
D) Aggregation is done by the Collector — the SDK does not aggregate
✅ Correct Answer: C

Aggregation converts individual measurements into exportable data points. Each instrument has a default: Counter → Sum (total accumulated), Histogram → Explicit Bucket Histogram (count per bucket + sum), Gauge → LastValue (most recent), UpDownCounter → Sum. Views let you customize aggregation (e.g., change Histogram buckets).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Default Aggregations Per Instrument
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Counter:       Sum                                             │
  │  Histogram:     ExplicitBucketHistogram (count+sum+buckets)     │
  │  Gauge:         LastValue (most recent observation)             │
  │  UpDownCounter: Sum                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the API latency Histogram uses default aggregation — Prometheus gets bucket counts, sum, and total count for computing percentiles.

API & SDK Q95 of 200
What are Exemplars in OpenTelemetry metrics?
A) Exemplars link a metric data point to a specific trace — when p99 latency spikes to 5s, the exemplar points to the Trace ID of the slow request, letting you jump from the metric spike directly to the trace waterfall showing WHY it was slow
B) Exemplars are example metric configurations for new users
C) Exemplars are deprecated in favor of Span Events
D) Exemplars store historical metric values for comparison
✅ Correct Answer: A

Exemplars are the bridge between metrics and traces. A Histogram data point can include an exemplar: a reference to the Trace ID and Span ID of a specific request that contributed to that bucket. When p99 latency spikes, click the spike in Grafana → the exemplar takes you to Jaeger showing the exact slow trace. This is the "WHY" behind every metric spike.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exemplar — Metric → Trace Navigation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  p99 Latency Spike (Grafana) → Click → Exemplar → Trace abc123  │
  │  Jaeger shows: DB query took 3.2s — missing index               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Exemplars = The "WHY" behind every metric spike             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the API latency Histogram includes exemplars — when p99 spikes, one click shows the exact slow trace with the root cause.

API & SDK Q96 of 200
What are Metric Views in OpenTelemetry?
A) Grafana dashboards that display metric data
B) SQL-like queries for filtering metric data points
C) Configuration files for the Prometheus exporter
D) A configuration mechanism to customize how instruments are output — rename metrics, drop unwanted attributes, change aggregation (e.g., override Histogram bucket boundaries), or filter which attribute combinations to keep
✅ Correct Answer: D

Views let you customize metric output without changing instrumentation code. You can: rename a metric (http.server.duration → api.latency), drop high-cardinality attributes, change Histogram bucket boundaries, or set custom aggregation. Views are registered on the MeterProvider at configuration time.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metric Views — Customize Output Without Code Changes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │    name: api.latency              ← Rename                      │

  │    aggregation: ExplicitBucketHistogram([0,10,50,100,500,1000])│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Views customize the API latency Histogram with custom buckets matching the SLO thresholds (100ms, 200ms, 500ms).

API & SDK Q97 of 200
What are the naming conventions for OpenTelemetry metric instruments?
A) Any descriptive name is fine — there are no conventions
B) Names use dot-separated hierarchical format: http.server.duration, db.client.connections.usage, messaging.publish.messages — following the pattern {namespace}.{optional_subnamespace}.{metric_name} with consistent units
C) Names use camelCase: httpServerDuration, dbClientConnections
D) Names are auto-generated by the SDK — you cannot choose them
✅ Correct Answer: B

OTel metric names use dot-separated, hierarchical format: http.server.duration, db.client.connections.usage, messaging.publish.messages. The pattern is: {namespace}.{subsystem}.{metric}. Units follow UCUM: ms (milliseconds), By (bytes), {call} (count). Consistent naming means you can write one PromQL query that works across all services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metric Naming Convention
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  http.server.duration          ms     HTTP request latency      │
  │  http.server.request_count     {call} Total HTTP requests       │
  │  db.client.connections.usage   {conn} DB connection pool       │
  │  messaging.publish.messages    {msg}   Published messages       │
  │  rpc.server.duration           ms     gRPC call latency        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: metrics named http.server.duration and db.client.operation.duration are immediately understood by any OTel-compatible backend.

What metrics would you instrument for anihpj/jobpost?
A) Counter: job.applications.total (applications submitted), Histogram: http.server.duration (API latency distribution), Histogram: db.client.operation.duration (query latency), Gauge: db.pool.connections (active DB connections), UpDownCounter: celery.tasks.pending (queue depth)
B) Only CPU and memory — application-specific metrics are unnecessary
C) A single Counter for total requests — that covers everything
D) Use only traces — metrics are redundant when you have full tracing
✅ Correct Answer: A

anihpj/jobpost instruments 5 key metrics: a Counter for business KPIs (applications submitted, enabling "how many today?" dashboards), a Histogram for API latency (enabling SLO-based alerting on p99), a Histogram for DB query latency (identifying slow queries), a Gauge for DB pool usage (preventing connection exhaustion), and an UpDownCounter for Celery queue depth (detecting backlog). Together, these cover the RED + USE methods for the entire application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost Metrics Portfolio
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Counter:   job.applications.total     Business KPI (RED: Rate) │

  │  Histogram: db.client.operation.duration DB latency (RED: Dur.) │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

With these 5 metrics and distributed tracing, the anihpj team has complete visibility: RED for services, USE for resources, and traces for deep-dive debugging — the complete observability trifecta.

2.6 Signals — LogsQ99-Q108

API & SDK Q99 of 200
What is a Log Record in OpenTelemetry?
A) A simple text string written to stdout by the application
B) A timestamped event with severity, body (message), attributes (structured metadata), and — uniquely in OTel — TraceId and SpanId fields that correlate the log to a specific trace and span
C) A file stored on disk that contains all application logs for a day
D) A metric that counts how many log entries were produced
✅ Correct Answer: B

An OTel LogRecord is a structured log entry that combines traditional logging (timestamp, severity, message) with OTel trace correlation (TraceId, SpanId). This is the key innovation: every log line carries the trace context, so you can navigate from a log directly to the trace that produced it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LogRecord = Log + Trace Context
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LogRecord {                                                     │
  │    Timestamp: 14:32:01.500, Severity: ERROR,                    │
  │    Body: "DB connection timeout after 3000ms",                  │
  │    TraceId: "abc...123",  ← Links to the trace!                 │
  │    SpanId: "def...456"    ← Links to the span!                  │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every Django log automatically carries TraceId and SpanId — click any ERROR log in Grafana and jump directly to the trace waterfall in Jaeger.

API & SDK Q100 of 200
What are the LogRecord severity levels in OpenTelemetry?
A) TRACE=1, DEBUG=5, INFO=9, WARN=13, ERROR=17, FATAL=21 — numeric SeverityNumber with corresponding SeverityText for human readability
B) LOW, MEDIUM, HIGH, CRITICAL — four simple severity levels
C) 1 through 10, with 10 being the most severe
D) OTel uses the same severity levels as syslog: 0-7
✅ Correct Answer: A

OTel defines 24 severity levels mapped to well-known numbers: TRACE=1, DEBUG=5, INFO=9, WARN=13, ERROR=17, FATAL=21. Each LogRecord has both SeverityNumber (numeric, for programmatic filtering) and SeverityText (human-readable). The gaps allow for custom levels between the standard ones.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Severity Levels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE=1   DEBUG=5   INFO=9   WARN=13   ERROR=17   FATAL=21    │
  │  ───────   ───────   ──────   ───────   ────────   ───────    │
  │  Verbose   Debug     Standard  Warning    Error      Critical   │
  │  tracing   info      operational issues   failures    crashes   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django's logging levels (DEBUG=10, INFO=20, WARNING=30, ERROR=40, CRITICAL=50) are mapped to OTel levels — ERROR becomes SeverityNumber=17.

API & SDK Q101 of 200
What are the numeric severity levels in OpenTelemetry?
A) 1-10 with 10 being most severe
B) Same as syslog: Emergency=0 through Debug=7
C) Only four levels: Info, Warning, Error, Critical
D) TRACE=1, DEBUG=5, INFO=9, WARN=13, ERROR=17, FATAL=21 — with gaps allowing custom levels between the standard ones
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTel severity levels use numeric values with gaps: TRACE=1, DEBUG2=2, DEBUG3=3, DEBUG4=4, DEBUG=5, INFO=9, WARN=13, ERROR=17, FATAL=21. The gaps allow custom levels between standard ones. These map cleanly to major logging frameworks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Severity Map
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE=1..4  DEBUG=5..8  INFO=9..12  WARN=13..16               │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django ERROR=40 maps to OTel ERROR=17 via the log bridge automatically.

API & SDK Q102 of 200
What is the OTel Log Bridge?
A) A network proxy between the Collector and log backends
B) A mechanism that connects existing logging frameworks (Python logging, log4j, winston) to the OTel pipeline — existing log calls automatically flow into OTel with TraceId/SpanId correlation without changing logging code
C) A Docker container that bridges logs between different clusters
D) A deprecated feature replaced by the LoggerProvider

The Log Bridge is the adapter between traditional logging and OTel. Instead of replacing your logging library, the bridge intercepts log records and routes them through the OTel LoggerProvider. Your existing logger.error("DB failed") calls continue to work but now automatically carry TraceId and SpanId.

📝 Detailed Explanation

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  log4j Logger                     → LogBridge → LoggerProvider   │
  │  winston logger                   → LogBridge → LoggerProvider   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 No logging code changes needed — the bridge handles it       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django's Python logging automatically flows through the OTel log bridge — every log line gets TraceId without changing any Django code.

API & SDK Q103 of 200
What is log correlation in OpenTelemetry?
A) Injecting TraceId and SpanId into every log line — so you can navigate from an ERROR log directly to the trace waterfall in Jaeger showing exactly what happened during that request
B) Comparing log files between different services to find patterns
C) Correlating log severity levels with CPU usage metrics
D) A machine learning algorithm that finds related log entries

Log correlation is OTel's killer feature for logs: every log line automatically carries the current TraceId and SpanId. In Grafana, click any ERROR log → "View Trace" → jumps to Jaeger showing the full request waterfall. This connects the "what happened" (log) to the "why it happened" (trace).

📝 Detailed Explanation

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Log: "DB timeout" {TraceId:abc, SpanId:def} → Click → Jaeger │
  │  Trace abc: Web(200ms)→API(150ms)→DB(3200ms)← THE BOTTLENECK! │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every Django ERROR log carries TraceId — one click from Grafana to the trace showing exactly which DB query timed out.

API & SDK Q104 of 200
What is structured logging in OpenTelemetry?
A) Logs formatted with colors and timestamps for better readability
B) Logs emitted as structured objects (JSON) with TraceId, SpanId, severity, and custom attributes — enabling programmatic querying and correlation across signals
C) Logs stored in a SQL database with indexes on every field
D) Logs that follow a specific file naming convention for parsing
✅ Correct Answer: B

Structured logs use JSON format with fields like {"severity":"ERROR","body":"DB timeout","TraceId":"abc","SpanId":"def","db.system":"postgresql"}. This enables filtering in Loki/Elasticsearch: "show all ERROR logs for TraceId=abc". Unstructured logs (plain text) can't be queried this way.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Structured vs Unstructured Logs
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ "Error: DB timeout at 14:32" — unqueryable text             │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django logs are emitted as structured JSON — queryable by TraceId, severity, and route.

API & SDK Q105 of 200
How do you emit a log record via the LoggerProvider?
A) print("log message") — standard output is automatically captured
B) Use the logging exporter in the Collector — the SDK doesn't emit logs
C) logger.emit(LogRecord(body="message", severity=Severity.ERROR, attributes={...})) — logs flow through LoggerProvider → LogRecordProcessor → Exporter
D) Write to a file — the Collector reads it with the filelog receiver

Direct log emission: logger.emit(LogRecord(...)). But most apps use the Log Bridge — existing logging calls (logger.error()) are intercepted and routed through the OTel LoggerProvider automatically. Direct emit is for when you need programmatic control over every LogRecord field.

API & SDK Q106 of 200
What is a LogRecordProcessor?

C) A database that stores processed log records for querying

2.7 SDK PipelinesQ109-Q118

API & SDK Q109 of 200
What is an SDK pipeline in OpenTelemetry?
A) A YAML file that defines the Collector configuration
B) The network path between the SDK and the Collector
C) The processing chain inside the SDK: Provider → Processor → Exporter — SpanProcessor processes spans, MetricReader collects metrics, LogRecordProcessor handles logs, each chaining to exporters that send data out
D) A CI/CD pipeline that deploys OpenTelemetry to production
✅ Correct Answer: C

SDK pipelines process telemetry BEFORE it leaves the application process. Each signal has its own pipeline chain: Traces flow through SpanProcessors, Metrics through MetricReaders, Logs through LogRecordProcessors. Multiple processors can be chained: enrich → filter → batch → export.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SDK Pipeline Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span → EnrichProcessor → FilterProcessor → BatchSpanProcessor  │
  │                                                     → Exporter  │
  │  Metric → MetricReader → Exporter                               │
  │  Log → LogRecordProcessor → Exporter                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the SDK pipeline enriches spans with business attributes, batches them for efficiency, and exports via OTLP.

API & SDK Q110 of 200
What is the difference between SimpleSpanProcessor and BatchSpanProcessor?
A) SimpleSpanProcessor exports each span synchronously as it ends (low latency, high overhead); BatchSpanProcessor queues spans and exports them in batches asynchronously (higher latency, much higher throughput) — Batch is the default for production
B) SimpleSpanProcessor is for Python; BatchSpanProcessor is for Java
C) There is no difference — they are aliases for the same processor
D) SimpleSpanProcessor is newer and replaces BatchSpanProcessor
✅ Correct Answer: A

SimpleSpanProcessor: exports each span immediately when span.end() is called. Good for debugging, terrible for production (blocks the application thread). BatchSpanProcessor: queues spans and flushes them in batches on a timer or when the queue is full. Production default — much higher throughput with minimal latency impact.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Simple vs Batch SpanProcessor
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Simple: span.end() → export immediately → blocks thread       │
  │  Batch:  span.end() → queue → flush every 5s or 512 spans      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 ALWAYS use BatchSpanProcessor in production                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: BatchSpanProcessor batches 512 spans or flushes every 5 seconds — efficient for 150 requests/second.

API & SDK Q111 of 200
How do you chain multiple SpanProcessors?
A) SpanProcessors cannot be chained — only one is allowed
B) Add processors in order: provider.add_span_processor(EnrichProcessor()); provider.add_span_processor(BatchSpanProcessor(exporter)) — each span flows through all processors in registration order
C) Chain them in the Collector — the SDK only supports one processor
D) Use a ChainSpanProcessor wrapper that combines them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Multiple processors are chained by registration order: add_span_processor() appends to the chain. Each span flows through ALL processors sequentially. Typical chain: enrich (add attributes) → filter (drop health checks) → batch (queue for export). Order matters — filtering before batching saves queue space.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SpanProcessor Chain
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Span → Enrich → Filter → BatchSpanProcessor → OTLP Exporter   │
  │         (add      (drop     (queue &       (send to             │
  │         tenant)   /healthz)  flush)         Collector)          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the chain adds business attributes → filters health-check spans → batches to OTLP exporter.

API & SDK Q112 of 200
What is a MetricReader and what types exist?

B) PeriodicExportingMetricReader exports metrics on a timer (push model); PullMetricReader exposes /metrics endpoint for Prometheus scraping (pull model). Both can coexist in one MeterProvider
C) A Collector component that reads metrics from backends
D) MetricReaders are only for debugging — not production use
✅ Correct Answer: B

PeriodicExportingMetricReader: exports metrics on a timer (e.g., every 30s) via push. PullMetricReader (Prometheus): exposes /metrics endpoint for scraping. Both can be added to the same MeterProvider simultaneously — push to OTLP AND expose for Prometheus.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dual MetricReaders
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MeterProvider                                                   │
  │  ├── PeriodicExportingMetricReader(OTLP, 30s) → Collector      │
  │  └── PrometheusMetricReader() → /metrics → Prometheus scrapes  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: metrics go to OTLP for central collection AND are exposed for Prometheus scraping — dual readers, one provider.

API & SDK Q113 of 200
Can you use multiple MetricReaders simultaneously?
A) Yes — add multiple MetricReaders to MeterProvider: one PeriodicExportingMetricReader for OTLP push AND one PrometheusMetricReader for Prometheus pull. Both receive the same metric data and export independently
B) No — only one MetricReader is allowed per MeterProvider
C) Multiple readers require separate MeterProviders for each
D) Only the Collector supports multiple readers, not the SDK

📝 Detailed Explanation
