API & SDK Q114 of 200
How does the LogRecordProcessor chain work?
A) LogRecordProcessors cannot be chained — only one is allowed
B) They chain in reverse order — last added runs first
C) Same as SpanProcessor: add multiple processors to LoggerProvider — each log flows through all processors in registration order: enrich → filter → batch → export
D) LogRecordProcessor chaining is only supported in the Collector
✅ Correct Answer: C

LogRecordProcessor chain pattern: enrich (add k8s metadata) → filter (drop DEBUG logs in production) → batch (queue for efficiency) → export (OTLP). Same composable pattern as SpanProcessor and MetricReader chains.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LogRecordProcessor Chain
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Log → Enrich → Filter(drop DEBUG) → Batch → OTLP Exporter     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: DEBUG logs are filtered out in production to reduce volume while ERROR logs pass through.

API & SDK Q115 of 200
What are the trade-offs of batching in SDK pipelines?
A) Batching has no trade-offs — it's always better than processing one at a time
B) Higher throughput and lower network overhead vs increased latency (data waits in queue) and potential data loss if the process crashes before flush — balance with batch size and flush interval
C) Batching only helps with logs; traces and metrics don't benefit
D) Batching reduces throughput but improves latency — the opposite of what most expect
✅ Correct Answer: B

Batching trades latency for throughput. A span waits up to scheduledDelayMillis (default 5000ms) or until the batch is full. Benefits: fewer network calls, higher throughput. Risk: if the process crashes, unflushed spans in the queue are lost. Mitigation: smaller batch sizes for critical data, persistent queues for the Collector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Batching Trade-Offs
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Small batch: low latency, high network overhead                 │
  │  Large batch: high latency, low network overhead                 │
  │  Default: 512 spans or 5000ms — good balance for most apps      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: 512 spans or 5 second flush — handles 150 req/s with minimal overhead and sub-5-second export latency.

API & SDK Q116 of 200
How does the SDK handle export timeouts and retries?
A) The OTLP exporter has configurable timeout (default 10s) and retry with exponential backoff — if the Collector is unreachable, exports are retried; if the timeout expires, the batch is dropped (in the SDK; the Collector can use persistent queues)
B) Failed exports are retried indefinitely until they succeed
C) The SDK has no retry mechanism — every failed export permanently loses data
D) Retries are only supported in the Java SDK
✅ Correct Answer: A

The OTLP exporter retries failed exports with exponential backoff (configurable via OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_TIMEOUT). After the timeout, the batch is dropped. For critical data, use the Collector's persistent queue (file storage or Kafka) which survives restarts — the SDK is not designed for durable storage.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Export Retry Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Export fails → retry with backoff → timeout → drop batch       │
  │  💡 SDK drops; Collector persists. Design your pipeline         │
  │     so the COLLECTOR handles durability, not the SDK.           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the SDK retries exports for 10s; the Collector Gateway uses a persistent file queue to survive restarts.

API & SDK Q117 of 200
What are ForceFlush and Shutdown and why are they critical?
A) ForceFlush clears all spans; Shutdown restarts the SDK — administrative operations
B) They are optional methods — telemetry is always exported before process exit
C) ForceFlush compresses spans; Shutdown archives old data — storage management
D) ForceFlush() immediately exports all queued spans/metrics/logs; Shutdown() flushes and cleanly closes exporters. ALWAYS call Shutdown() before process exit (SIGTERM handler) or you WILL lose unexported telemetry
✅ Correct Answer: D

ForceFlush: immediately exports everything in the batch queue. Shutdown: flushes then closes exporters. Always register a shutdown hook: on SIGTERM/SIGINT, call provider.shutdown() to prevent data loss. Kubernetes sends SIGTERM before killing pods — if you don't flush, queued spans are lost.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Graceful Shutdown — Prevent Data Loss
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  signal(SIGTERM) → provider.force_flush() → provider.shutdown() │
  │  💡 Without this: 512 queued spans = permanently lost           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django's shutdown hook calls force_flush() + shutdown() on the TracerProvider and MeterProvider — zero data loss on pod restarts.

API & SDK Q118 of 200
How does anihpj/jobpost configure its SDK pipeline end-to-end?
A) Traces: Django auto-instrumentation (opentelemetry-instrumentation-django) → BatchSpanProcessor (batch timeout 500ms, max 512 spans) → OTLPSpanExporter (localhost:4317). Metrics: PeriodicExportingMetricReader (30s interval) → OTLPMetricExporter (localhost:4317). Logs: OTLPHandler (bridge stdlib logging → OTel) → OTLPLogExporter. All configured in otel_config.py with env var overrides
B) Everything uses the SimpleSpanProcessor — batching is too complex for Django
C) anihpj doesn't use the SDK — it relies entirely on the OpenTelemetry Operator for auto-instrumentation
D) Each signal uses a completely different Collector endpoint (traces→:4317, metrics→:4318, logs→:55679)
✅ Correct Answer: A

The anihpj SDK pipeline uses BatchSpanProcessor (not SimpleSpanProcessor — batching is critical for production) with OTLP exporter for traces, PeriodicExportingMetricReader (30s export interval, temporality=CUMULATIVE) for metrics, and OTLPHandler to bridge Python's stdlib logging to OTel log pipeline. All three signals export to the Agent DaemonSet at localhost:4317 via gRPC. Configuration is centralized in otel_config.py with OTEL_* env var overrides for flexibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj SDK Pipeline — otel_config.py
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Traces                                                        │
  │  provider = TracerProvider()                                     │
  │  processor = BatchSpanProcessor(                                 │
  │      OTLPSpanExporter(endpoint="localhost:4317"))                │
  │  provider.add_span_processor(processor)                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # Metrics                                                       │
  │  reader = PeriodicExportingMetricReader(                         │
  │      OTLPMetricExporter(endpoint="localhost:4317"),              │
  │      export_interval_millis=30000)                               │
  │  provider = MeterProvider(metric_readers=[reader])               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # Logs (bridge)                                                 │
  │  handler = OTLPHandler(endpoint="localhost:4317")                │
  │  logging.getLogger().addHandler(handler)                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  # All signals → Agent DaemonSet :4317 → Gateway → Backends     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

All three signals share the same OTLP endpoint (Agent DaemonSet at localhost:4317). The Agent batches and forwards to the Gateway, which does heavy processing (tail sampling, OTTL transforms) and exports to Jaeger, Prometheus, and Loki. Env vars like OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=anihpj-api override configuration per deployment.

2.8 Context PropagationQ119-Q124

API & SDK Q119 of 200
What is context propagation in OpenTelemetry?
A) Copying trace data between different Collector instances
B) Passing TraceId, SpanId, and TraceFlags between services via HTTP headers, gRPC metadata, or message properties — so child spans in downstream services link to the same trace
C) Broadcasting configuration changes to all SDKs in a cluster
D) Synchronizing clock time between distributed services
✅ Correct Answer: B

Context propagation is how trace identity travels between services. When anihpj-web calls anihpj-api, it injects the current SpanContext into HTTP headers. The API extracts it and creates a child span with ParentSpanId = the web span's SpanId. Without propagation, each service starts its own independent trace — the waterfall is broken.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Context Propagation — The Trace Glue
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-web ──HTTP headers(traceparent)──► anihpj-api           │
  │  Span: abc             propagated               Span: def       │
  │  SpanId: 123                                   ParentSpanId: 123│
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Without propagation: Web trace=abc, API trace=xyz → BROKEN  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: context propagation ensures the Web→API→DB trace is one continuous waterfall, not three separate traces.

API & SDK Q120 of 200
What is the W3C TraceContext traceparent header?
A) A custom OpenTelemetry header format for internal SDK communication
B) The standard HTTP header: traceparent: 00-{traceId}-{spanId}-{traceFlags} — where 00=version, traceId=32 hex chars, spanId=16 hex chars, traceFlags=01 (sampled) or 00 (not sampled)
C) A JSON payload containing the full trace context for the current request
D) A deprecated header replaced by OpenTelemetry's proprietary format
✅ Correct Answer: B

The traceparent header is the W3C standard for trace context propagation. Format: traceparent: 00-a1b2c3d4e5f6a7b8c9d0e1f2a3b4c5d6-1234567890abcdef-01. All OTel SDKs, Jaeger, and major cloud providers support it. It's the universal trace identity format.

📝 Detailed Explanation
traceparent Header Format
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  traceparent: 00-a1b2c3d4e5f6a7b8c9d0e1f2a3b4c5d6-1234567890abcdef-01│
  │               ││ │──────────────────────────────│ │──────────────│ ││  │
  │               Version TraceId (32 hex)            SpanId (16 hex)  Flags│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every HTTP call between Web→API carries a traceparent header — the standard way traces stay connected across services.

API & SDK Q121 of 200
What is W3C Baggage in OpenTelemetry?
A) A set of HTTP headers that replace traceparent for context propagation
B) Key-value pairs propagated alongside trace context — carries application-level data (user-id, tenant-id, session-id, feature-flags) across service boundaries via the baggage HTTP header
C) Deprecated — span attributes have replaced Baggage entirely
D) A storage mechanism for span data that doesn't fit in the standard span fields
✅ Correct Answer: B

Baggage propagates business context alongside traces: baggage: userId=789,tenantId=tier3,featureFlags=beta-search. Unlike SpanContext (trace identity), Baggage carries application data. Downstream services can read baggage to make decisions. Caution: baggage values are visible in HTTP headers — never put secrets in baggage.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Baggage — Business Context Propagation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Web → API: baggage: userId=789,tenantId=tier3                  │
  │  API reads tenantId → routes to tier-3 DB, applies rate limits  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Baggage propagates userId and employerTier — the API uses this to apply tier-specific rate limits without querying the DB.

API & SDK Q122 of 200
What propagators are available in OpenTelemetry?
A) Only W3C TraceContext — no other formats are supported
B) Propagators are a Collector concept — the SDK doesn't use them
C) TraceContextPropagator (W3C standard), BaggagePropagator (W3C), B3Propagator (Zipkin legacy), JaegerPropagator, OTPropagator, and XRayPropagator — multiple can be active simultaneously for backward compatibility
D) There is only one propagator: CompositePropagator — which combines all formats
✅ Correct Answer: C

OTel supports multiple propagators. The default is tracecontext,baggage. Add B3 for Zipkin compatibility, Jaeger for Jaeger clients. Configure via OTEL_PROPAGATORS=tracecontext,baggage,b3. All listed propagators inject on outgoing requests and try extraction on incoming requests.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Propagator Setup
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTEL_PROPAGATORS=tracecontext,baggage,b3                        │
  │  Incoming: Can extract from W3C OR B3 headers                   │
  │  Outgoing: Injects ALL three formats                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: uses tracecontext+baggage for modern services, adds B3 for compatibility during migration.

API & SDK Q123 of 200
How does context propagation work across different carrier formats?
A) HTTP uses headers (traceparent), gRPC uses metadata, Kafka/RabbitMQ use message properties — the propagator interface abstracts the carrier type; the same inject/extract logic works regardless of transport
B) Context propagation only works over HTTP — other protocols are not supported
C) Each protocol requires a completely different propagation mechanism
D) All carriers use the same HTTP header format regardless of protocol
✅ Correct Answer: A

The propagator interface accepts any carrier: HTTP headers, gRPC metadata, Kafka message headers. inject(context, carrier, setter) and extract(context, carrier, getter) — the setter/getter adapts to the carrier type. Same code, any transport.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Carrier Propagation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HTTP:    traceparent header                                    │
  │  gRPC:    grpc-trace-bin metadata                               │
  │  Kafka:   message header property                               │
  │  💡 Same propagator, different carrier — OTel abstracts it      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: HTTP between Web→API, message headers in RabbitMQ for Celery tasks — same W3C context propagates across both.

API & SDK Q124 of 200
What causes the "broken trace" problem?
A) Too many spans in a single trace exceeding the maximum limit
B) The Collector crashing mid-trace causing partial data loss
C) Using different OTel SDK versions across services
D) Missing or incorrect context propagation — propagator not configured, headers stripped by proxy/load balancer, version mismatch (W3C vs B3), or manual HTTP clients that don't propagate headers
✅ Correct Answer: D

Broken traces happen when context propagation fails. Common causes: (1) propagator not configured (default is W3C, but B3 legacy clients send different headers), (2) proxy/load balancer strips traceparent header, (3) manual HTTP clients (requests library without OTel instrumentation), (4) version mismatch between services. Always verify the traceparent header is present across all hops.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Broken Trace Diagnosis
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Web (TraceId:abc) → API (TraceId:xyz) ← BROKEN!               │
  │  Check: Is traceparent in the HTTP request from Web to API?     │
  │  Debug: curl -H "traceparent: 00-..." to test manually         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a broken trace was caused by Nginx stripping traceparent headers — fixed by adding proxy_pass_request_headers on;.

2.9 AgentsQ125-Q128

API & SDK Q125 of 200
What are OpenTelemetry agents?
A) Human operators who manually instrument applications
B) Docker containers that run alongside the Collector
C) Runtime components that provide automatic instrumentation without code changes — Java uses a JVM agent JAR, Python uses a wrapper script, Node.js uses a require hook, and Kubernetes uses the OTel Operator with pod annotations
D) AI-powered tools that write instrumentation code automatically
✅ Correct Answer: C

OTel agents inject instrumentation at runtime: Java uses -javaagent:opentelemetry-javaagent.jar to intercept class loading, Python uses opentelemetry-instrument to monkey-patch libraries, Node.js uses --require flags, and Kubernetes Operator uses pod annotations to inject sidecars. Zero source code changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTel Agents — Zero-Code Instrumentation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☕ Java:   java -javaagent:opentelemetry-javaagent.jar -jar app│
  │  🐍 Python: opentelemetry-instrument python app.py              │
  │  🟢 Node:   node --require @opentelemetry/auto-instrumentations │
  │  ☸️  K8s:    Operator + annotation injection                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the K8s Operator injects the Python agent via pod annotation — Django is auto-instrumented with zero code changes.

API & SDK Q126 of 200
How does the Java OpenTelemetry agent work?
A) java -javaagent:opentelemetry-javaagent.jar -jar app.jar — the agent intercepts class loading via JVM instrumentation API, injects bytecode to wrap HTTP/database/gRPC/messaging calls with OTel spans automatically
B) The Java agent requires modifying the application source code to add @Trace annotations
C) It's a separate service that runs alongside the JVM and captures network traffic
D) Java agent only works with Spring Boot — not other frameworks
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Java agent uses JVM instrumentation to intercept class loading. It injects bytecode that wraps HTTP clients, JDBC drivers, gRPC stubs, and Kafka producers/consumers. Zero source code changes. Automatically traces Spring Boot, Tomcat, Jetty, OkHttp, Jedis, and 50+ libraries out of the box.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Java Agent — Bytecode Injection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  JVM loads opentelemetry-javaagent.jar → intercepts classes →  │
  │  wraps HTTP/DB/gRPC calls → exports spans via OTLP             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Java agent would auto-instrument any future Java microservices with zero code changes.

API & SDK Q127 of 200
How does the Python OpenTelemetry agent work?
A) It requires modifying Python's source code to add tracing hooks
B) opentelemetry-instrument python app.py — monkey-patches Django, Flask, psycopg2, redis, requests, and 30+ libraries at startup, injecting OTel spans without source code changes
C) It runs as a separate process that captures network traffic from the Python app
D) Python auto-instrumentation only works with Django — no other frameworks
✅ Correct Answer: B

opentelemetry-instrument uses monkey-patching to wrap framework functions at import time. For Django: wraps WSGI handler, database backends, cache backends, and template rendering. For psycopg2: wraps execute(). For redis: wraps execute_command(). All spans follow semantic conventions automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Python Monkey-Patching Agent
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  opentelemetry-instrument python manage.py runserver            │
  │  → Patches Django, psycopg2, redis, requests, urllib3...       │
  │  → Every HTTP/DB/Redis call auto-traced with zero code changes │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Python agent auto-instruments Django + PostgreSQL + Redis with a single wrapper command.

API & SDK Q128 of 200
How does the Kubernetes Operator auto-instrument pods?
A) Add annotation instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true" to the pod — the Operator's mutating webhook injects an init container with the OTel agent, sets environment variables, and the app starts auto-instrumented without any code or Dockerfile changes
B) The Operator SSH's into every pod and installs the agent manually
C) It deploys a sidecar Collector that intercepts all application traffic
D) The Operator only works with Java — Python and Node.js are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

The OTel Operator uses a Kubernetes mutating webhook. You annotate pods with instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python: "true" (or java, nodejs, dotnet). The Operator injects an init container that copies the agent to a shared volume, sets OTEL env vars, and the app starts fully instrumented. No Dockerfile changes, no code changes, no package installation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
K8s Operator — Annotation-Driven Injection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod annotation: instrumentation.opentelemetry.io/inject-python│
  │  → Operator mutates pod → injects init container → OTel active │
  │  💡 Zero code changes, zero Dockerfile changes, zero packages  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django Deployment gets one annotation — every pod is auto-instrumented by the Operator at creation time.

⚙️ Category 3: The OpenTelemetry Collector (26%)Q129-Q180

🟡 Intermediate: The Data Processing Engine of OpenTelemetry

The Collector is the programmable data pipeline that receives, transforms, and exports telemetry. It runs as an Agent (DaemonSet), Gateway (Deployment), or Sidecar. This category covers configuration, deployment patterns, scaling, pipeline design, and data transformation — everything you need to run the Collector in production.

Q129-Q180 52 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Collector ConfigurationQ129-Q140

Collector Q129 of 200
What is the structure of an OpenTelemetry Collector configuration?
A) A single JSON file listing all backend endpoints
B) A YAML file with four top-level sections: receivers (data inputs), processors (data transformations), exporters (data outputs), and service.pipelines (connecting receivers→processors→exporters into signal-specific pipelines)
C) Environment variables only — no config file needed
D) A Python script that programmatically builds the pipeline
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Collector YAML has 4 sections: receivers: (OTLP, Jaeger, Prometheus, Kafka — where data enters), processors: (batch, memory_limiter, attributes, filter, transform — what happens to data), exporters: (OTLP, Jaeger, Prometheus, logging — where data goes), and service.pipelines: (maps receivers→processors→exporters per signal: traces, metrics, logs).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Collector Config Structure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  receivers:                                                      │
  │    otlp:                    # Named receiver                     │
  │      protocols: {grpc: 4317, http: 4318}                        │
  │  processors:                                                     │
  │    batch:                   # Named processor                    │
  │      timeout: 5s                                                 │
  │  exporters:                                                      │
  │    otlp/jaeger:             # Named exporter                     │
  │      endpoint: jaeger:4317                                       │
  │  service:                                                        │
  │    pipelines:                                                    │
  │      traces:                                                     │
  │        receivers: [otlp]                                         │
  │        processors: [batch]                                       │
  │        exporters: [otlp/jaeger]                                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Collector config defines OTLP receiver → batch processor → Jaeger + Prometheus exporters in a clean YAML structure.

Collector Q130 of 200
What are service pipelines in the Collector?
A) Named pipelines (traces, metrics, logs) that define the data flow: which receivers feed data → which processors transform it → which exporters send it out — each signal has its own independent pipeline
B) Microservices that run alongside the Collector for additional processing
C) Kubernetes Services that expose Collector endpoints
D) CI/CD pipelines that deploy Collector configuration changes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Service pipelines define per-signal data flows. The traces pipeline might route OTLP→batch→Jaeger; the metrics pipeline might route OTLP→batch→Prometheus; the logs pipeline might route filelog→batch→Loki. Each is independent — you can send traces to one backend and metrics to another.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Per-Signal Pipelines
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  traces:  [otlp] → [batch, attributes] → [otlp/jaeger]         │
  │  metrics: [otlp] → [batch] → [prometheus]                       │
  │  logs:    [otlp] → [batch, filter] → [otlp/loki]               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: traces go to Jaeger, metrics to Prometheus, logs to Loki — three independent pipelines in one Collector.

Collector Q131 of 200
What does a minimal complete Collector configuration look like?
A) Just an endpoint URL — the Collector auto-configures everything else
B) A 500-line YAML file covering every possible option
C) receivers: {otlp:{protocols:{grpc:{}}}} — processors: {batch:{}} — exporters: {logging:{}} — service: {pipelines:{traces:{receivers:[otlp],processors:[batch],exporters:[logging]}}} — the simplest working config: OTLP in, batch, logging out
D) A single command-line flag: --endpoint=jaeger:14250
✅ Correct Answer: C

The minimal Collector config: one receiver (OTLP gRPC), one processor (batch), one exporter (logging for debug), and one service pipeline connecting them. This is the "hello world" that verifies your Collector is working — it prints every span to stdout.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Minimal Working Config
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  receivers: { otlp: { protocols: { grpc: {} } } }               │
  │  processors: { batch: {} }                                       │
  │  exporters: { logging: { loglevel: debug } }                    │
  │  service: { pipelines: { traces: { receivers:[otlp],            │
  │      processors:[batch], exporters:[logging] } } }              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the dev Collector starts with this minimal config, then adds Jaeger and Prometheus exporters for production.

Collector Q132 of 200
How do you configure multiple export pipelines to different backends?
A) Run separate Collector instances for each backend
B) Use a single exporter that routes to multiple backends automatically
C) Define multiple named exporters and reference them in service.pipelines — e.g., traces pipeline exports to [otlp/jaeger, logging], metrics pipeline exports to [prometheus] — one Collector, multiple destinations
D) Multiple pipelines require multiple Collector configuration files

One Collector, multiple backends. Define exporters otlp/jaeger and prometheus, then configure per-signal pipelines: traces export to Jaeger, metrics export to Prometheus. You can even export traces to MULTIPLE backends simultaneously (Jaeger + logging).

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

Collector Q133 of 200
How does environment variable substitution work in Collector config?
A) The Collector doesn't support environment variables — all values must be hardcoded
B) Use ${env:VARIABLE_NAME} or ${VARIABLE_NAME} syntax in the YAML config — e.g., endpoint: ${env:OTEL_EXPORTER_ENDPOINT} — the Collector resolves these at startup from the process environment
C) Environment variables are only supported for the OTLP receiver, not other components
D) Use a separate .env file that the Collector reads at startup
✅ Correct Answer: B

Collector config supports env var substitution: ${env:JAEGER_ENDPOINT} or ${JAEGER_ENDPOINT}. This enables one config file across environments — the same YAML, different env vars per deployment (staging vs production). Commonly used for endpoints, API keys, and TLS paths.

Env Var Substitution
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: one Collector ConfigMap with env var substitution works across dev/staging/prod.

Collector Q134 of 200
How do you validate a Collector configuration?

C) otelcol --validate-config --config=config.yaml — validates YAML syntax, component references, and pipeline connectivity before deployment

✅ Correct Answer: C

otelcol --validate-config checks: YAML syntax, referenced receivers/processors/exporters exist, pipeline references are valid. Use in CI/CD before deploying to prevent config errors from crashing the Collector. Dry-run mode tests with actual component initialization.

Collector Q135 of 200
How do you configure TLS/mTLS in the Collector?
A) TLS is enabled by default — no configuration needed
B) Add tls: block to receiver/exporter config with cert_file, key_file (for server), and ca_file (for client/mTLS). mTLS adds client_ca_file to require client certificates
C) TLS is configured via environment variables only
D) The Collector uses Kubernetes TLS secrets automatically
✅ Correct Answer: B

TLS config per receiver/exporter: tls: {cert_file, key_file, ca_file}. For mTLS, add client_ca_file to require client certs. The OTLP receiver on 4317 should always use TLS in production. Self-signed certs for internal Collector-to-Collector communication are common.

  │            cert_file: /certs/server.crt                         │
  │            key_file: /certs/server.key                          │
  │            client_ca_file: /certs/ca.crt  # mTLS                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Agent→Gateway uses mTLS; Gateway→Jaeger uses TLS with the Jaeger CA cert.

Collector Q136 of 200
How do you configure authentication for Collector components?

What Collector extensions are available?

B) Only pprof for profiling — other extensions are experimental

Collector Q138 of 200
What is the Collector component ID model?
A) Components use auto-generated UUIDs — you can't name them
B) Each component has a unique user-defined ID in format type[/name] — e.g., otlp/receiver, otlp/jaeger, batch/traces. The name after / lets you have multiple instances of the same type with different configs
C) Component IDs are sequential numbers assigned by the Collector
D) Only exporters have IDs — receivers and processors use default names
✅ Correct Answer: B

Component IDs: type[/name]. otlp is the default OTLP receiver. otlp/jaeger is a named OTLP exporter for Jaeger. batch/traces is a named batch processor. The name enables multiple instances: two OTLP exporters (one for Jaeger, one for another backend) with different endpoints.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Component ID Model
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  receivers:  { otlp: {...} }          ← type only (default name)│
  │  exporters:  { otlp/jaeger: {...},     ← named (multiple)       │
  │                otlp/tempo: {...} }                               │
  │  processors: { batch: {...} }          ← type only              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: uses otlp receiver, otlp/jaeger and prometheus exporters, batch processor.

Collector Q139 of 200
Where does the Collector look for its configuration file?
A) Only at /etc/otel/config.yaml — the path is hardcoded
B) The config is embedded in the Collector binary — no file needed
C) The Collector reads from a Kubernetes ConfigMap named otel-collector-config
D) Default: /etc/otelcol/config.yaml. Override with --config flag (single file or glob pattern). Can also use --config=/etc/otelcol/*.yaml to merge multiple config files
✅ Correct Answer: D

Default config location: /etc/otelcol/config.yaml. Override with --config=/path/to/config.yaml. Supports glob patterns: --config=/etc/otelcol/conf.d/*.yaml merges multiple files. This is useful for separating base config from environment-specific overrides.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Config Locations
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  otelcol --config=/etc/otelcol/config.yaml                      │
  │  otelcol --config=/etc/otelcol/base.yaml --config=/etc/otelcol/prod.yaml│
  │  otelcol --config=/etc/otelcol/conf.d/*.yaml (merge multiple)   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: base config in ConfigMap, mounted at /etc/otelcol/config.yaml. Dev/staging/prod use different ConfigMaps.

Collector Q140 of 200
What does the anihpj Collector configuration look like?
A) OTLP gRPC receiver (:4317) → batch processor → dual export: OTLP to Jaeger (traces) + Prometheus exporter (metrics) with health_check and file_storage extensions
B) A single line: endpoint=jaeger:14250
C) The anihpj Collector uses the default config — no customization
D) A 500-line YAML with every processor enabled

The anihpj Collector uses a clean production config: Receiver: OTLP gRPC on 4317. Processor: batch (5s timeout). Exporters: OTLP to Jaeger for traces, Prometheus for metrics. Extensions: health_check for K8s probes, file_storage for persistent queues. Pipelines: traces→[batch]→Jaeger, metrics→[batch]→Prometheus.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Collector Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Django SDK → OTLP(:4317) → batch → Jaeger(traces)             │

This config gives the anihpj team full distributed tracing + metrics collection with health monitoring and data durability.

3.2 Collector DeploymentQ141-Q152

Collector Q141 of 200
What is the Agent deployment pattern for the Collector?
A) A single centralized Collector instance handling all traffic
B) Collector runs as a DaemonSet — one per Kubernetes node, lightweight (only batch + OTLP forwarding), localhost latency for apps, scales horizontally with nodes
C) Collector injected as a sidecar into every application pod
D) A cloud-hosted SaaS Collector managed by a vendor
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Agent pattern runs a Collector on every node as a DaemonSet. Apps send telemetry to localhost:4317 (no network hop). The agent does minimal processing (batch + forward) and sends to a central Gateway. Benefits: low latency, node-local collection, automatic scaling with cluster size.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Agent (DaemonSet) Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Node 1: [App] → localhost:4317 → [Agent Pod] → Gateway        │
  │  Node 2: [App] → localhost:4317 → [Agent Pod] → Gateway        │
  │  Node 3: [App] → localhost:4317 → [Agent Pod] → Gateway        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Agent DaemonSet collects from Django pods on every node with sub-millisecond localhost latency.

Collector Q142 of 200
What is the Gateway deployment pattern?
A) A Kubernetes Ingress that routes traffic to Collectors
B) A load balancer distributing spans across backends
C) A single Collector that runs on the developer's machine
D) A centralized Collector running as a Deployment/StatefulSet — receives from Agents or directly from apps, does heavy processing (tail sampling, OTTL transforms, multi-export), and forwards to backends
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Gateway is the centralized processing tier. It receives telemetry from Agents (or directly from apps), performs heavy processing (tail sampling, complex OTTL transforms, routing to multiple backends), and exports to Jaeger/Prometheus/Loki/etc. Typically a Deployment with multiple replicas behind a load balancer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Agents (DaemonSet) ──► Gateway (Deployment) ──► Backends      │
  │  Lightweight             Heavy processing        Jaeger, Prom,  │
  │  Batch + Forward         Tail sampling, OTTL     Loki, Kafka    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Gateway runs as a Deployment with 3 replicas, handling tail sampling and multi-backend export.

Collector Q143 of 200
What is the Sidecar deployment pattern?
A) A Collector container injected into each application pod — dedicated per-service processing with guaranteed localhost access, no network hops, and resource isolation per service
B) A Collector that runs on a separate physical server next to the application
C) A Collector deployed as a Kubernetes sidecar to the API server
D) A deprecated pattern replaced by the Agent pattern
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Sidecar pattern injects a Collector container alongside each application pod. Benefits: dedicated per-service processing, guaranteed localhost access, no network hops, perfect for multi-tenant environments where each tenant needs custom processing. Downside: higher total resource usage (one Collector per pod vs one per node).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: [anihpj-api] [Collector sidecar] → Gateway               │
  │       localhost:4317                                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: sidecars could be used in multi-tenant deployments where each customer's telemetry needs different processing rules.

Collector Q144 of 200
What is the Agent + Gateway deployment pattern?
A) Two identical Collector instances for high availability
B) Agent (DaemonSet, lightweight, batch+forward) collects from apps on each node and forwards to Gateway (Deployment, heavy processing like tail sampling) which exports to backends — the production best practice
C) Agent runs on the developer's machine; Gateway runs in the cloud
D) Agent and Gateway are the same Collector with different configuration flags
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Agent+Gateway is the production best practice. Agents (DaemonSet) run on every node — lightweight, just batch and forward. They talk to apps via localhost:4317 (no network hop). The Gateway (Deployment) receives from all Agents, does heavy processing (tail sampling, OTTL transforms, multi-export), and sends to backends.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Agent + Gateway — Production Best Practice
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [App] → Agent(DaemonSet) ──→ Gateway(Deployment) ──→ Jaeger   │
  │  localhost:4317     batch+forward    tail sampling    Prometheus│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: 3 Agent pods (one per node) forward to 3 Gateway replicas that handle tail sampling and multi-backend export.

Collector Q145 of 200
When should you use each Collector deployment pattern?
A) Always use Agent+Gateway — other patterns are deprecated
B) Always use Gateway only — Agents add unnecessary complexity
C) Agent (DaemonSet): node-level collection, low latency. Gateway: centralized heavy processing. Sidecar: per-pod isolation, multi-tenant. Agent+Gateway: production best practice combining local collection + centralized processing. Choose based on scale, latency requirements, and operational complexity
D) The pattern is auto-selected by the Collector based on available resources
✅ Correct Answer: C

Decision matrix: Agent for small/medium clusters (simple). Gateway for processing-heavy setups (tail sampling). Sidecar for multi-tenant isolation. Agent+Gateway for production at scale (>10 nodes, tail sampling). No single pattern fits all — the right choice depends on scale, latency tolerance, and operational budget.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Deployment Decision Matrix
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pattern          Nodes  Latency  Complexity  Best For          │
  │  Agent only       <10    Low      Low         Small clusters    │
  │  Gateway only     Any    Medium   Medium      Central processing│
  │  Agent+Gateway    10+    Low      High        Production at scale│
  │  Sidecar          Any    Lowest   High        Multi-tenant     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: 6-cluster lab uses Agent-only for simplicity; production would use Agent+Gateway for tail sampling at scale.

Collector Q146 of 200
How do you deploy the Collector on Kubernetes?
A) Use kubectl run — the Collector auto-configures from the Kubernetes API
B) Deploy as DaemonSet (Agent) or Deployment (Gateway) with a ConfigMap for the config YAML, a Service for OTLP endpoint, and optionally a Headless Service for load-balanced Gateway access
C) Install via Helm chart only — manual YAML deployment is not supported
D) The Collector is a built-in Kubernetes component — no deployment needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Standard K8s deployment: ConfigMap holds the collector config YAML, DaemonSet/Deployment mounts it at /etc/otelcol/config.yaml, Service exposes ports 4317 (gRPC) and 4318 (HTTP). For Gateway: Headless Service + DNS-based load balancing across replicas.

📝 Detailed Explanation
K8s Collector Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ConfigMap(config.yaml) → DaemonSet/Deployment → Service(:4317)│
  │  Apps send to otel-collector.anihpj.svc:4317                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Collector Agent as DaemonSet on all 6 cluster nodes, Gateway as Deployment with 3 replicas.

Collector Q147 of 200
How do you size Collector resource limits?
A) The Collector auto-scales — manual sizing is not needed
B) Always use the default Kubernetes resource limits
C) Base on throughput: memory for batch queues (spans × avg size × queue depth), CPU for processors (OTTL transforms, tail sampling). Start with 256Mi/0.5 CPU, monitor otelcol_* metrics, and adjust based on actual usage
D) Set CPU and memory to maximum available on the node for best performance
✅ Correct Answer: C

Collector sizing formula: Memory = spans/sec × avg_span_bytes × batching_queue_size × pipeline_count. CPU = base + (spans/sec × processor_cost). Monitor otelcol_processor_batch_batch_size and otelcol_exporter_sent_spans. Start conservative, scale based on data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Sizing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dev:      128Mi / 0.25 CPU (1 pod)                             │
  │  Staging:  256Mi / 0.5 CPU  (2 pods)                            │
  │  Prod:     512Mi / 1.0 CPU  (3+ pods)                           │
  │  💡 Always set memory_limiter processor to prevent OOM          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: 256Mi/0.5 CPU for the Agent DaemonSet, 512Mi/1.0 CPU for the Gateway Deployment handling tail sampling.

Collector Q148 of 200
How do you run the Collector outside Kubernetes?
A) The Collector only runs on Kubernetes — bare metal/VM is unsupported
B) Use Docker Desktop — the only non-K8s option
C) Download binary from GitHub releases, run as systemd service or Docker container. Supports Linux, Windows, macOS. Use --config flag to point to your config file
D) Compile from source — no pre-built binaries are available
✅ Correct Answer: C

Download otelcol___.tar.gz from GitHub. Run: ./otelcol --config=config.yaml. For production: systemd service with restart=always. Docker: docker run otel/opentelemetry-collector with mounted config. Supports all major platforms.

Non-K8s Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Binary:  ./otelcol --config=/etc/otelcol/config.yaml           │
  │  Docker:  docker run -v ./config.yaml:/etc/otelcol/config.yaml  │
  │             otel/opentelemetry-collector                        │
  │  systemd: ExecStart=/usr/bin/otelcol --config=/etc/otelcol/... │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the lab cluster runs Collector as DaemonSet, but the team also runs a local Collector binary on dev machines for testing.

Collector Q149 of 200
What are the cloud deployment considerations for the Collector?
A) Cloud deployment is identical to on-premise — no differences
B) AWS: ECS/EC2 with EFS for persistence, EKS for K8s, ALB for load balancing. GCP: GKE with GCS for state. Azure: AKS. All: use IAM roles for auth instead of API keys, cloud-specific receivers (AWS CloudWatch, GCP metrics)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

Collector Q150 of 200
What is the complete anihpj Collector deployment architecture?
A) Agent DaemonSet (lightweight, batch+forward, one per node) → Gateway Deployment (3 replicas, tail sampling, OTTL transforms) → Jaeger (traces) + Prometheus (metrics) + Loki (logs) — with health_check probes, file_storage persistence, and mTLS between Agent and Gateway
B) A single Collector pod handling all traffic
C) Sidecar Collector in every Django pod with direct backend export
D) The SDK exports directly to Jaeger and Prometheus — no Collector needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The anihpj production architecture: Agent (DaemonSet, 1 per node, 128Mi/0.25 CPU) collects from Django pods via localhost:4317. Gateway (Deployment, 3 replicas, 512Mi/1.0 CPU) receives from Agents, performs tail sampling (keep errors + 10% of success), OTTL transforms for attribute normalization, and exports to Jaeger (traces), Prometheus (metrics), and Loki (logs). mTLS secures Agent→Gateway communication. health_check drives K8s probes. file_storage provides persistent queues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Production Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [Django Pods] → Agent(DaemonSet) → Gateway(Deployment x3)      │
  │  localhost:4317    batch+forward     tail sampling, OTTL        │
  │                                      ├→ Jaeger (traces)         │
  │                                      ├→ Prometheus (metrics)    │
  │                                      └→ Loki (logs)             │
  │  🔒 mTLS Agent↔Gateway  💾 file_storage  ❤️ health_check       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This architecture handles 150 req/s in the lab, and scales to 10,000+ req/s in production by adding Gateway replicas and tuning batch sizes.

Collector Q151 of 200
How do Collector health checks and readiness probes work?
A) The Collector has no built-in health endpoints — you must use external monitoring
B) Health checks only report if the Collector process is running (PID check)
C) The health_check extension exposes / (health, returns 200 when Collector is alive) and /ready (returns 200 when all pipelines are ready). These integrate with K8s livenessProbe and readinessProbe for automatic restarts and traffic routing
D) Health checks are only available in the enterprise edition of the Collector
✅ Correct Answer: C

The health_check extension provides two endpoints: / (returns 200 if the Collector process is alive — used by livenessProbe) and /ready (returns 200 only when ALL configured pipelines have successfully started — used by readinessProbe and StartupProbe). If /ready returns 503, Kubernetes won't route traffic to the pod, preventing data loss during startup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Collector Health Check Endpoints
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GET /          → 200 OK (process alive)   → livenessProbe      │
  │  GET /ready     → 200 OK (pipelines ready) → readinessProbe     │
  │                  → 503 (pipelines not ready)                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Config:                                                         │
  │  extensions:                                                     │
  │    health_check:                                                 │
  │      endpoint: 0.0.0.0:13133                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  K8s Probe:                                                      │
  │  livenessProbe:                                                  │
  │    httpGet: { path: "/", port: 13133 }                          │
  │  readinessProbe:                                                 │
  │    httpGet: { path: "/ready", port: 13133 }                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Agent DaemonSet uses livenessProbe on / and readinessProbe on /ready. If a Gateway replica's /ready returns 503, the Service removes it from the endpoint list until pipelines initialize.

Collector Q152 of 200
How does the Collector handle stateful data and persistent queues?
A) The Collector is entirely stateless — all data is lost on restart
B) The file_storage extension provides persistent local queues on disk, surviving Collector restarts. For larger scale, Kafka can be used as an intermediary buffer. The exporter queue with persistent storage ensures no data loss during backend outages
C) State is always stored in an external database (PostgreSQL required)
D) Persistent queues require Kubernetes StatefulSets — they don't work with Deployments
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Collector can be stateful via the file_storage extension. This writes queued telemetry to disk so data survives Collector restarts. For high-throughput pipelines (>10K spans/sec), Kafka acts as an intermediary: SDKs → Kafka → Collector, decoupling producers from the Collector and providing multi-day buffering during backend outages.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Stateful Collector — Persistent Queues
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [App SDK] → Collector → file_storage (disk queue) → Backend    │
  │                          ↓ (if backend down)                     │
  │                          queue persists on disk → retry later    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  At Scale:                                                       │
  │  [App SDK] → Kafka (buffer) → Collector → Backend               │
  │              ↑ days of retention                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  file_storage config:                                            │
  │  extensions:                                                     │
  │    file_storage:                                                 │
  │      directory: /var/lib/otelcol                                 │
  │      timeout: 10s                                                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Gateway Deployment mounts a PersistentVolume at /var/lib/otelcol for file_storage. During backend maintenance, queued spans survive Gateway restarts. At production scale, a Kafka cluster sits between Agents and Gateway for infinite buffering.

3.3 Collector ScalingQ153-Q160

Collector Q153 of 200
How do you horizontally scale the OpenTelemetry Collector?
A) The Collector cannot scale horizontally — it's single-instance by design
B) Use Kubernetes HPA based on CPU only — no other metrics matter
C) Run multiple Collector replicas behind a load balancer. For stateless pipelines: simple round-robin. For stateful pipelines (tail sampling): use trace ID-based consistent hashing so all spans from the same trace hit the same Collector instance
D) Scale by increasing CPU and memory on a single Collector (vertical scaling only)
✅ Correct Answer: C

Horizontal scaling runs multiple Collector replicas behind a load balancer. For stateless pipelines (batch → export), any replica can handle any span — round-robin works. For stateful pipelines (tail sampling), spans from the same trace must reach the same Collector, requiring trace ID hashing (e.g., loadbalancing exporter with consistent hashing).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Horizontal Scaling — Two Patterns
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Stateless (round-robin):                                        │
  │  [SDK] → LB → [Collector-1] → Backends                          │
  │            → [Collector-2] → Backends                            │
  │            → [Collector-3] → Backends                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Stateful (trace ID hashing):                                    │
  │  [SDK] → LB(hash:traceID) → [Collector-1] ← all of trace-abc    │
  │                           → [Collector-2] ← all of trace-xyz    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Gateway runs 3 replicas behind a headless Service. The Agent uses the loadbalancing exporter with trace ID hashing so tail sampling sees complete traces.

Collector Q154 of 200
How does the loadbalancing exporter distribute telemetry across backends?
A) It round-robins every individual span to different backends
B) It sends all data to all backends simultaneously (multicast)
C) It only works with the OTLP exporter and cannot load-balance other exporters
D) It distributes spans/logs/metrics across multiple backend instances using a routing key (typically trace ID for consistent hashing). This ensures all spans of a trace go to the same backend Collector, enabling tail sampling and complete trace assembly
✅ Correct Answer: D

The loadbalancing exporter wraps another exporter and distributes data across multiple backend endpoints. It uses a routing key (trace ID by default) hashed to a consistent backend. This ensures all spans from trace-abc always hit backend-1, while trace-xyz always hits backend-2 — critical for tail sampling and span assembly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Loadbalancing Exporter — Trace ID Hashing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  loadbalancing exporter:                                         │
  │    routing_key: "traceID"                                        │
  │    resolver:                                                      │
  │      dns:                                                         │
  │        hostname: otelcol-gateway.anihpj.svc                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  trace-abc (hash→1) → Gateway-1 (tail sampling on full trace)    │
  │  trace-xyz (hash→2) → Gateway-2 (tail sampling on full trace)   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Agent uses the loadbalancing exporter with trace ID routing to distribute spans across 3 Gateway replicas, ensuring each Gateway sees complete traces for tail sampling decisions.

Collector Q155 of 200
How does DNS-based load balancing work for the Collector?
A) The DNS resolver in the loadbalancing exporter periodically resolves a hostname to get all backend IPs. In Kubernetes, a headless Service returns Pod IPs directly. Changes are detected on DNS TTL refresh, enabling dynamic backend discovery
B) DNS load balancing uses round-robin DNS records configured manually
C) It requires an external load balancer like AWS ALB — DNS alone cannot work
D) DNS load balancing only works with IPv6 addresses
✅ Correct Answer: A

The DNS resolver in the loadbalancing exporter periodically resolves a hostname (e.g., otelcol-gateway.anihpj.svc.cluster.local). In Kubernetes, a headless Service (clusterIP: None) returns all Pod IPs from DNS. The resolver detects pod additions/removals on DNS TTL refresh, enabling dynamic scaling without reconfiguration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DNS Load Balancing — K8s Headless Service
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Headless Service (clusterIP: None):                             │
  │  otelcol-gateway → DNS → [10.1.1.1, 10.1.1.2, 10.1.1.3]        │
  │                           Gateway-1   Gateway-2   Gateway-3     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Agent periodically resolves, detects new Pod IPs on scale-up   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: A headless Service named otelcol-gateway exposes Gateway Pod IPs. The Agent's loadbalancing exporter resolves this hostname every 5 seconds, discovering new Gateway replicas automatically on scale-up.

Collector Q156 of 200
What is sharding by trace ID and why is it needed?
A) Sharding splits metrics by service name so different Collectors handle different services
B) Sharding routes all spans from the same trace (same trace ID) to the same Collector backend using consistent hashing. This is REQUIRED for tail sampling — the Collector must see the ENTIRE trace to decide if it's interesting enough to keep
C) Sharding is about distributing logs across multiple Loki instances
D) Sharding means running one Collector per Kubernetes namespace
✅ Correct Answer: B

Trace ID sharding uses consistent hashing to route all spans of a trace to the same Collector instance. This is required for tail sampling because the Collector must see the complete trace (all spans) to evaluate sampling policies like "keep traces with errors" or "keep traces slower than 500ms." Without sharding, spans of the same trace could land on different Collectors, each seeing only a partial trace.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Sharding Matters for Tail Sampling
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT Sharding:                                                │
  │  trace-abc: span-web→Col-1, span-api→Col-2, span-db→Col-3       │
  │  Result: NO Collector sees the full trace → tail sampling FAILS  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITH Sharding (trace ID hash):                                  │
  │  trace-abc: span-web→Col-1, span-api→Col-1, span-db→Col-1       │
  │  Result: Col-1 sees ALL 3 spans → can decide to KEEP or DROP    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The loadbalancing exporter with routing_key: "traceID" ensures all spans from a job application flow (Web→API→DB→Redis) reach the same Gateway replica for complete tail sampling evaluation.

Collector Q157 of 200
How do you manage Collector memory to prevent OOM (Out of Memory) crashes?
A) Set memory limits to unlimited — the Collector handles its own memory
B) Only use vertical scaling — memory management is automatic
C) Use the memory_limiter processor (soft limit triggers GC, hard limit rejects data), set GOMEMLIMIT for Go GC tuning, configure batch sizes appropriately, and monitor otelcol_processor_memory_limiter metrics. For K8s: set memory limits slightly above the hard limit
D) Memory management is only needed for the Gateway pattern — Agents are immune
✅ Correct Answer: C

The memory_limiter processor is your first line of defense. It has two thresholds: soft limit (triggers GC and reduces batch sizes) and hard limit (rejects new data). Set GOMEMLIMIT to 80% of your K8s memory limit. Monitor otelcol_processor_memory_limiter_data_refused to know when you're hitting limits.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Memory Management Layers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  memory_limiter:                                                 │
  │    check_interval: 1s                                            │
  │    limit_mib: 512       ← hard limit (reject data)              │
  │    spike_limit_mib: 256 ← soft limit (trigger GC, reduce batch) │
  │                                                                  │
  │  GOMEMLIMIT=400MiB  ← Go GC triggers at 400MiB                  │
  │  K8s limit: 512Mi   ← OOMKill if exceeded                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Safe zone: GOMEMLIMIT < soft < hard < K8s limit                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Gateway runs with memory_limiter (soft=256Mi, hard=512Mi), GOMEMLIMIT=400MiB. The Agent (lightweight, no heavy processing) uses 128Mi limits with milder memory_limiter settings.

Collector Q158 of 200
How does queue management work in the Collector?
A) The Collector has no queuing — data is sent immediately or dropped
B) All queues are in-memory only and lost on restart
C) Queues are managed by the operating system, not the Collector
D) Exporters have internal send queues with configurable size, retry, and backoff. With file_storage extension, queues persist to disk for durability. For infinite buffering, Kafka sits between the Collector and backends as an external queue
✅ Correct Answer: D

Each exporter has a sending queue with configurable queue_size (in-flight items), num_consumers (parallel senders), and retry_on_failure settings. Adding file_storage makes these queues persistent on disk. For the highest durability, Kafka sits as an external buffer: SDK → Kafka → Collector → Backend, providing days of retention and replay capability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Queue Management Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Exporter Queue Config:                                          │
  │  sending_queue:                                                  │
  │    queue_size: 5000     ← buffered items before backpressure     │
  │    num_consumers: 10    ← parallel exporter goroutines           │
  │    storage: file_storage/var/lib/otelcol ← persist to disk      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  retry_on_failure:                                               │
  │    enabled: true                                                 │
  │    initial_interval: 5s                                          │
  │    max_interval: 30s                                             │
  │    max_elapsed_time: 300s                                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Gateway exporters use queue_size=5000 with file_storage persistence at /var/lib/otelcol. During Jaeger maintenance, spans are queued on disk and replayed when the backend recovers — zero data loss.

Collector Q159 of 200
How does the Collector handle backpressure when backends are slow?
A) The Collector drops all data immediately when backends are slow
B) Backpressure cascades backwards: when the exporter queue fills up (backend too slow), the processor slows down, which causes the receiver to apply backpressure to SDKs via gRPC flow control. This natural backpressure prevents the Collector from running out of memory
C) The Collector crashes to alert operators — this is intentional design
D) Backpressure only works with HTTP — gRPC has no flow control
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Collector implements natural backpressure: When backends are slow, exporter queues fill → processors block waiting to export → receivers can't accept new data → gRPC flow control tells SDKs to slow down. This cascading mechanism prevents memory explosion. SDKs buffer locally and retry with exponential backoff.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backpressure Cascade
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Backend Slow → Exporter Queue FULL → Processor BLOCKED          │
  │             → Receiver BLOCKED → gRPC Flow Control → SDK SLOWS  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Cascade direction: ← ← ← ← (backwards from backend to SDK)     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SDK Response: buffer locally + retry with exponential backoff  │
  │  Result: No data loss, no OOM, graceful degradation            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When Jaeger is under maintenance, the Gateway's exporter queue fills up, backpressure cascades to the Agent, and the Django SDK buffers spans locally. Once Jaeger recovers, queued spans flush within seconds.

Collector Q160 of 200
How do you scale the anihpj Collector pipeline from dev (10 spans/sec) to production (10,000 spans/sec)?
A) Dev: single Collector, 128Mi/0.25 CPU. Prod: Agent DaemonSet (3+ nodes) → Gateway Deployment (3+ replicas, 512Mi/1.0 CPU) with loadbalancing exporter (trace ID hash), memory_limiter (soft 256Mi/hard 512Mi), file_storage persistence, Kafka buffer for peak traffic, HPA based on otelcol_exporter_queue_size
B) Just increase the CPU/memory of the single Collector — it scales linearly
C) Add more Django pods — the Collector auto-scales with the application
D) Switch to a proprietary observability vendor — OTel can't handle 10,000 spans/sec
✅ Correct Answer: A

Scaling anihpj from dev to production is a progressive scale-up: Start with a single Collector in dev (docker-compose). In staging, add Agent DaemonSet + 2 Gateway replicas with basic batching. In production (10,000 spans/sec), deploy the full Agent+Gateway architecture with load balancing, memory limits, persistent queues, and Kafka for peak buffering.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Scaling Journey
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEV (10 spans/s):    [Django] → single Collector → Jaeger     │
  │                       docker-compose, 128Mi/0.25 CPU            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STAGING (100/s):     Agent DS(×3) → Gateway(×2) → Jaeger+Prom  │
  │                       Basic batching, no tail sampling           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PROD (10,000/s):     Agent DS(×3+) → Kafka → Gateway(×3+)     │
  │                       Tail sampling, OTTL, mTLS, file_storage   │
  │                       HPA on queue depth, multi-backend export  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key scaling levers: add Gateway replicas (horizontal), tune batch sizes (timeout=200ms, max_size=8192), increase queue_size (10000+), add Kafka for decoupling, and monitor otelcol_exporter_queue_size to trigger HPA scaling.

3.4 PipelinesQ161-Q172

Collector Q161 of 200
What is the OTLP receiver and how is it used?
A) OTLP receiver is a legacy component — it's being replaced by Jaeger Thrift
B) It only works with gRPC — HTTP is not supported for ingestion
C) The OTLP receiver accepts telemetry via gRPC (port 4317) and HTTP (port 4318) in the OpenTelemetry Protocol format. It's the PRIMARY ingestion point for OTel SDKs and downstream Collectors — supports all three signals (traces, metrics, logs) on a single protocol
D) OTLP receiver only accepts traces — metrics and logs need separate receivers
✅ Correct Answer: C

The OTLP receiver is the standard ingestion endpoint for the OpenTelemetry ecosystem. It accepts traces, metrics, and logs via both gRPC (port 4317, default for most SDKs) and HTTP (port 4318, useful for web browsers and environments without gRPC). All OTel SDKs natively export via OTLP — it's the universal protocol of OpenTelemetry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTLP Receiver — The Universal Ingestion Point
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTLP Receiver:                                                  │
  │  gRPC: 0.0.0.0:4317  ← SDKs, downstream Collectors              │
  │  HTTP: 0.0.0.0:4318  ← Browsers, curl, environments w/o gRPC   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  All three signals on ONE protocol:                              │
  │  traces → otlp receiver → processors → otlp/jaeger exporter     │
  │  metrics→ otlp receiver → processors → prometheus exporter       │
  │  logs   → otlp receiver → processors → loki exporter            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django SDK exports to localhost:4317 (gRPC, Agent DaemonSet). Cross-cluster Collectors communicate via gRPC on 4317. Health check curl scripts use HTTP on 4318.

Collector Q162 of 200
Why would you use the Jaeger receiver in an OpenTelemetry Collector?
A) OTLP cannot export to Jaeger — the Jaeger receiver is the only way
B) The Jaeger receiver is faster than the OTLP receiver
C) It's required for Prometheus integration
D) For backwards compatibility — applications still using Jaeger clients (Thrift/gRPC) can send spans to the Collector, which converts them to OTel format. This enables gradual migration from Jaeger to OpenTelemetry without rewriting instrumentation
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Jaeger receiver accepts spans in Jaeger-native formats (Thrift compact, Thrift binary, gRPC) and converts them to OTel format internally. This is a migration bridge: teams with existing Jaeger instrumentation can send to the Collector without code changes, while new services use OTLP. The Collector normalizes everything to OTel.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Jaeger Receiver — Migration Bridge
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Legacy Service (Jaeger client) → Jaeger Receiver (Thrift/gRPC) │
  │                                    ↓                             │
  │                              OTel Internal Format                │
  │                                    ↓                             │
  │                              OTLP Exporter → Jaeger Backend      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  New Service (OTel SDK) → OTLP Receiver → Same Pipeline!       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If an older microservice still uses the Jaeger Python client (jaeger-client), the Collector's Jaeger receiver accepts those spans alongside OTLP from the Django OTel SDK — unified pipeline, gradual migration.

Collector Q163 of 200
How does the Prometheus receiver work in the Collector?
A) It replaces the Prometheus server entirely
B) It scrapes /metrics endpoints from your services (just like Prometheus does) and converts Prometheus metrics to OTel format. This unifies metric collection — the Collector scrapes, transforms, and exports to any backend
C) It only collects metrics from the Collector itself, not external services
D) It requires Prometheus to already be running — it proxies scrape requests
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Prometheus receiver acts as a Prometheus-compatible scraper. It periodically scrapes /metrics endpoints from your services (configurable scrape interval), converts Prometheus exposition format to OTel metrics, and feeds them into the Collector pipeline. This means you can collect Prometheus metrics (from your apps or third-party exporters) and forward them to any OTel-compatible backend.

Collector Q164 of 200
What is the Kafka receiver used for?
A) It consumes OTLP-encoded telemetry from Kafka topics, enabling decoupled buffered ingestion at massive scale. Kafka acts as a durable intermediary: SDKs → Kafka → Collector, allowing the Collector to consume at its own pace and survive restarts without data loss
B) It sends Kafka cluster metrics to Prometheus
C) It replaces the OTLP receiver — Kafka is the only supported protocol
D) It monitors Kafka consumer lag for alerting purposes
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Kafka receiver consumes telemetry from Kafka topics. This is the decoupled ingestion pattern: SDKs/Services produce to Kafka, and the Collector consumes at its own pace. Benefits: infinite buffering (Kafka retention), replay capability, decoupled scaling (Kafka consumers scale independently of producers), and survivability during Collector restarts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kafka Receiver — Decoupled Ingestion
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [SDK] → Kafka (otlp_spans topic) → Kafka Receiver → Pipeline  │
  │          ↑ 7-day retention        ↑ consumer group              │
  │          Producer                  Consumer                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Benefits:                                                       │
  │  • Collector can be down for hours — data waits in Kafka        │
  │  • Multiple Collector replicas share consumer group             │
  │  • Replay old data by resetting consumer offset                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: At production scale, Django SDKs export to Kafka topics (otlp_spans, otlp_metrics). The Gateway consumes from Kafka, providing multi-hour buffering during backend maintenance.

Collector Q166 of 200
Why is the Batch processor critical for Collector performance?
A) It's optional — the Collector works fine without it
B) It only helps with log processing, not traces or metrics
C) Batch processing is handled automatically by exporters — no processor needed
D) It groups individual spans/metrics/logs into batches, dramatically reducing the number of export calls, network overhead, and backend API load. Without batching, sending each span individually would overwhelm backends and increase latency 100x
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Batch processor is arguably the most important processor in any production pipeline. It accumulates spans/metrics/logs until either a timeout or max batch size is reached, then sends them as one bulk export. Without batching: 10,000 spans/sec = 10,000 export calls/sec (network and backend overwhelmed). With batching: 10,000 spans/sec → ~50 bulk exports/sec (5,000% efficiency gain).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Batch Processor — Before vs After
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT Batch:   10,000 spans → 10,000 export calls → 💥      │
  │  WITH Batch:      10,000 spans → ~50 batches → 50 export calls  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  batch config:                                                   │
  │    timeout: 200ms        ← flush if batch older than 200ms      │
  │    send_batch_size: 8192 ← or when 8192 items accumulated      │
  │    send_batch_max_size: 0 ← unlimited individual item size      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Always place batch processor AFTER memory_limiter           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Both Agent and Gateway use batch processors. Agent batches with timeout=500ms (lower urgency), Gateway with timeout=200ms (faster flush for tail sampling). This reduces Jaeger API calls from thousands/sec to dozens/sec.

Collector Q167 of 200
How does the Memory limiter processor prevent OOM?
A) It kills the Collector process when memory is high
B) It monitors Collector memory usage at check intervals. At the soft limit, it triggers GC and reduces data intake. At the hard limit, it refuses new data (returning errors to senders). This prevents the Go runtime from growing unbounded and hitting the K8s OOMKiller
C) It writes overflow data to disk to free memory
D) It only works with the Gateway pattern — Agents don't need it
✅ Correct Answer: B

The memory_limiter processor is your OOM guard. It periodically checks memory. Soft limit (e.g., 256MiB): triggers GC and signals upstream to slow down. Hard limit (e.g., 512MiB): refuses all new data until memory drops. This prevents the Go GC from delaying and the K8s OOMKiller from terminating the pod. Always put it FIRST in your processor pipeline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Memory Limiter — Two-Tier Defense
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Memory Usage:                                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  0% ──────────────────────────────────────────── 100%            │
  │       │                  │                    │                   │
  │       Normal        SOFT LIMIT           HARD LIMIT              │
  │       (process)     (reduce + GC)        (REFUSE data)           │
  │                      ↓ trigger GC        ↓ return errors         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Always: memory_limiter → batch → (other processors) → export│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Gateway config: memory_limiter(soft=256Mi, hard=512Mi) is the FIRST processor. During traffic spikes, soft limit reduces batch intake; if memory keeps climbing, hard limit returns RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED to Agents, triggering their retry logic.

D) It is deprecated in favor of the Transform processor with OTTL

Collector Q169 of 200

Collector Q171 of 200
How does the Prometheus exporter work?
