3.5 Transforming DataQ173-Q180

Collector Q173 of 200
What is OTTL (OpenTelemetry Transformation Language)?
A) A general-purpose programming language for writing Collector plugins
B) A domain-specific language for transforming telemetry data IN-FLIGHT within the Collector pipeline. It lets you read, modify, and filter spans/metrics/logs using simple expressions — no code compilation needed
C) A query language for searching traces in Jaeger (like SQL for traces)
D) A replacement for YAML — Collector configs are written in OTTL instead
✅ Correct Answer: B

OTTL (OpenTelemetry Transformation Language) is a pipeline-native DSL for transforming telemetry data as it flows through the Collector. Unlike writing Go plugins (which requires compilation), OTTL expressions are written directly in the Collector YAML config. You can access span attributes, resource attributes, metric values, and log body — and transform, filter, or route them dynamically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTTL — Transform Telemetry In-Flight
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTTL Expression Example:                                        │
  │  set(attributes["deployment.environment"], "production")        │
  │  where attributes["http.method"] == "GET"                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Benefits:                                                       │
  │  • No code changes — edit YAML config only                      │
  │  • No recompilation — Collector reloads config                  │
  │  • Safe — read-only by default, explicit mutations              │
  │  • Works on all signals — spans, metrics, logs                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: OTTL transforms normalize attribute naming (http.method → http.request.method), add deployment.environment=production, and hash user.id — all without touching Django code.

Collector Q174 of 200
What are the key OTTL functions for data transformation?
A) SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE (SQL-like operations)
B) map, filter, reduce, flatMap (functional programming operations)
C) Only set and get — OTTL has minimal transformation capabilities
D) set (assign value), delete_key (remove attribute), replace_match (regex replace), keep_keys (whitelist specific keys), truncate_all (limit string length), limit (cap numeric values), convert_case (lower/upper), and SubString (slice strings)
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTTL provides a rich set of transformation functions: set() for assigning values, delete_key() for removing attributes, replace_match() for regex-based replacement, keep_keys() for whitelisting (drop everything EXCEPT specified keys), truncate_all() for capping string lengths, limit() for capping numeric values, convert_case() for case normalization, and SubString() for string slicing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OTTL Function Reference
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  set(attributes["env"], "prod")              — create/update    │
  │  delete_key(attributes, "user.email")        — remove PII       │
  │  replace_match(attributes["url"], "http://", "https://")        │
  │  keep_keys(attributes, ["service", "env"])   — drop everything else│
  │  truncate_all(attributes, 256)               — cap string lengths│
  │  limit(attributes["count"], 0, 100)          — clamp to range   │
  │  ConvertCase(attributes["name"], "lower")    — normalize case   │
  │  SubString(attributes["id"], 0, 8)           — first 8 chars    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: OTTL delete_key removes user.email and user.phone from all spans; keep_keys on resource attributes keeps only service.name, deployment.environment, and k8s.* — dropping hundreds of auto-detected resource attributes.

What does the Resource Detection processor do?
A) It automatically detects and populates resource attributes from the environment — host name, OS type, cloud provider (AWS/GCP/Azure region, account ID), Kubernetes (namespace, pod name, node name, UID), and container runtime — without any manual configuration
B) It detects resource usage (CPU, memory) of the Collector itself
C) It discovers new services in the cluster for automatic instrumentation
D) It scans for available backend storage resources (disk space, DB connections)
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Resource Detection processor automatically enriches telemetry with infrastructure context. It uses detectors for: system (host.name, os.type, os.description), cloud (aws/gcp/azure — region, account_id, instance_id), k8s (k8s.namespace.name, k8s.pod.name, k8s.node.name, k8s.pod.uid), and container (container.id). This enriches every span/metric with WHERE it came from automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Detection — Auto-Enrichment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │             os.type = "linux"                                    │

  │             k8s.node.name = "worker-3"                           │
  │  cloud:     cloud.provider = "aws"                               │
  │             cloud.region = "us-east-1"                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Without this, every span is anonymous — no idea where it    │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Agent's resource detection processor adds k8s.namespace.name=anihpj, k8s.pod.name (the specific Django pod), and host.name (the node). This lets the team filter traces by pod or node during incident response.

Collector Q176 of 200
What does the Resource processor do?
A) It detects available system resources (CPU, memory, disk)

D) It allocates CPU and memory to different Collector pipelines

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Processor — Manual Control
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  resource:                                                       │
  │    attributes:                                                   │

  │        value: anihpj-api                                        │

Collector Q177 of 200
How does the Transform processor with OTTL enable powerful data manipulation?
A) It converts OTel format to proprietary vendor formats
B) It applies OTTL expressions to transform telemetry data in-flight: rename attributes (http.method → http.request.method), filter out PII (hash user.email), normalize values (lowercase service names), drop debug spans conditionally, enrich with computed values (duration_ms = end - start), and route data based on attribute conditions
C) It transforms the Collector configuration from YAML to JSON
D) It only transforms log records — spans and metrics need separate processors
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Transform processor is the most flexible transformation tool. It applies OTTL expressions to any telemetry data. You can: rename attributes for semantic convention compliance, hash PII for privacy, normalize values for consistency, conditionally drop debug data, compute new attributes from existing ones, and route data to different pipelines based on conditions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Transform Processor — OTTL in Action
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  transform:                                                      │
  │    span_statements:                                              │
  │      - set(attributes["http.request.method"],                    │
  │             attributes["http.method"])                           │
  │        where attributes["http.method"] != nil  ← rename         │
  │      - set(attributes["user.id"],                                │
  │             SHA256(attributes["user.id"]))     ← hash PII       │
  │      - delete_key(attributes, "user.phone")    ← remove PII     │
  │    log_statements:                                               │
  │      - set(body, "REDACTED")                                    │
  │        where IsMatch(body, "password|secret|token")  ← scrub   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Gateway transform processor renames http.method→http.request.method for semantic convention v1.26 compliance, hashes user.id for GDPR, and redacts body fields containing "password" or "token" — all without touching Django code.

Collector Q179 of 200
What is the Routing connector and how does it differ from processors?
A) A routing connector splits telemetry across DIFFERENT pipelines based on OTTL conditions — e.g., traces with errors → "critical" pipeline (no sampling), normal traces → "standard" pipeline (10% sampling). Unlike processors (which transform within one pipeline), connectors route BETWEEN pipelines
B) It routes network traffic between Collector instances (load balancer)
C) It's the same as the Filter processor — routing and filtering are identical
D) Routing only works for metrics — traces and logs cannot be routed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Routing connector directs telemetry to different pipelines based on conditions. Key distinction: Processors transform data WITHIN a single pipeline (renaming attributes, filtering). Connectors route data BETWEEN multiple pipelines (error traces → critical pipeline, normal traces → standard pipeline). Each pipeline can have different sampling, processing, and export destinations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Routing Connector — Multi-Pipeline Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OTLP Receiver → Routing Connector                               │
  │    ├─ condition: status.code == ERROR                            │
  │    │  → Pipeline "critical": NO sampling → Jaeger + AlertManager│
  │    ├─ condition: attributes["http.target"] == "/api/payments"    │
  │    │  → Pipeline "pci": encrypt attrs → dedicated PCI backend  │
  │    └─ default:                                                   │
  │       → Pipeline "standard": 10% sampling → Jaeger              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The routing connector sends error traces to a "critical" pipeline (100% sampling → immediate alerting), payment-related traces to a "pci" pipeline (PII scrubbing → PCI-compliant backend), and all other traces to a "standard" pipeline (10% sampling → Jaeger).

Collector Q180 of 200
How does anihpj use data transformation in production?
A) anihpj doesn't transform data — raw telemetry is exported directly
B) Only the Django SDK performs transformations — the Collector just forwards data
C) The Gateway transforms all telemetry: add deployment.environment=production (resource processor), rename http.method→http.request.method for semantic convention v1.26 (transform/OTTL), hash user.id and delete user.email for GDPR (attributes/OTTL), drop /healthz spans (filter), and enrich with k8s.pod.name (K8s attributes). All transformations happen in the Collector — zero Django code changes
D) Transformation is done by an external ETL pipeline — not the Collector
✅ Correct Answer: C

The anihpj Gateway runs a comprehensive transformation pipeline: Resource processor adds deployment.environment=production and normalizes service.name. Transform processor (OTTL) renames attributes to match semantic convention v1.26 and hashes PII. Filter processor drops /healthz spans. K8s attributes processor enriches with pod/namespace/node metadata. All without modifying Django code — the power of Collector-based transformation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Full Transformation Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Gateway Processor Chain:                                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  1. memory_limiter (OOM guard — ALWAYS first)                   │
  │  2. K8s attributes (add k8s.pod.name, k8s.namespace.name)       │
  │  3. resource (add deployment.environment=production)            │
  │  4. transform/OTTL:                                              │
  │     • rename http.method → http.request.method                  │
  │     • hash user.id → SHA256(user.id)                            │
  │     • delete user.email, user.phone                              │
  │  5. filter (drop /healthz, /ready, /metrics spans)              │
  │  6. batch (8192 items or 200ms)                                  │
  │  7. routing:                                                     │
  │     • errors → critical pipeline (no sampling, alert)           │
  │     • default → standard pipeline (tail sampling 10%)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Result: Clean, enriched, privacy-compliant telemetry with zero Django code changes. The ops team can update transformation rules by editing the Collector ConfigMap and triggering a rollout restart — no application redeployment needed.

4. Maintaining & Debugging Observability PipelinesQ181-Q200 (10%)

🔧 Domain 4: Maintaining & Debugging Observability Pipelines (10% — Q181-Q200)

The observability pipeline ITSELF needs observability. When traces go missing, metrics show zeros, or the Collector crashes silently — you need systematic approaches to debug, handle errors, and manage schema changes across versions.

4.1 Context Propagation DebuggingQ181-Q185

Debugging Q181 of 200
How do you diagnose broken traces where spans from different services don't link together?
A) Broken traces are normal — OpenTelemetry doesn't guarantee trace links
B) Check W3C traceparent header at each service boundary (incoming and outgoing), verify propagators are configured in each SDK (TraceContextPropagator + BaggagePropagator), inspect middleware/proxy for header stripping, and validate that trace IDs are 32 hex characters (not truncated or malformed)
C) Restart all services — the trace links will reconnect automatically
D) Broken traces are always caused by network latency — increase timeouts
✅ Correct Answer: B

Broken traces usually mean context propagation failure. The traceparent header (trace ID + parent span ID + trace flags) must pass from caller to callee. Diagnostic checklist: 1) Is the SDK configured with the right propagator? 2) Is the HTTP client/library propagating headers? 3) Is a proxy/gateway stripping unknown headers? 4) Is the traceparent value valid (32 hex chars for trace ID, 16 for span ID)?

📝 Detailed Explanation
Broken Trace Diagnosis
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Web Service                     API Service                    │
  │  traceparent: 00-abc123...-def456...-01                          │
  │       │                              │                           │
  │       ├──── HTTP GET /api/jobs ──────►│  ← CHECK: traceparent   │
  │       │   (with traceparent)          │     present in request?  │
  │       │                               │                           │
  │  Debug Steps:                                                    │
  │  1. curl -H "traceparent: 00-...-...-01" http://api/jobs        │
  │  2. Check API logs: trace_id = ?                                 │
  │  3. If trace_id differs → propagation broken                    │
  │  4. Inspect middleware for header stripping                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team found broken traces between Web and API — Nginx ingress was stripping unknown headers including traceparent. Fix: add proxy_pass_request_headers on; and explicitly allow the traceparent and tracestate headers.

Debugging Q182 of 200
How do you verify W3C TraceContext headers are being propagated correctly?
A) Check the Jaeger UI — if traces appear, propagation is correct
B) TraceContext headers are internal and cannot be inspected externally
C) Only the Collector can verify propagation — SDKs have no visibility
D) Inspect HTTP headers with curl -v or Wireshark: traceparent header format is "version-traceId-spanId-traceFlags" (e.g., "00-0af7651916cd43dd8448eb211c80319c-b7ad6b7169203331-01"). Verify traceId (32 hex), spanId (16 hex), and traceFlags (01=sample, 00=don't). Also check tracestate for vendor-specific data
✅ Correct Answer: D

The traceparent header is structured as: version-traceId-spanId-traceFlags. Version is always "00". TraceId is 32 lowercase hex chars (16 bytes). SpanId is 16 lowercase hex chars (8 bytes). TraceFlags: 01 means "sampled" (this trace will be exported), 00 means "not sampled". Use curl or browser DevTools to capture and verify these values at each service hop.

📝 Detailed Explanation
W3C Traceparent Header Structure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  traceparent: 00-0af7651916cd43dd8448eb211c80319c-b7ad6b7169203331-01│
  │               │  │                               │               │ │
  │               │  └─ traceId (32 hex)             │               │ │
  │               │                                  │               │ │
  │               └─ version (00)                    │               │ │
  │                                                  │               │ │
  │               └─ spanId (16 hex) ────────────────┘               │ │
  │                                                                  │ │
  │               └─ traceFlags (01=sampled) ────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  tracestate: vendor1=opaqueValue,vendor2=opaqueValue             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use curl -v -H "traceparent: 00-$(openssl rand -hex 16)-$(openssl rand -hex 8)-01" http://anihpj-api/jobs/ to inject a test traceparent and verify it's preserved through the API→DB chain in Jaeger.

Debugging Q183 of 200
What are the most common context propagation failures?
A) Wrong propagator configured (e.g., B3 instead of W3C TraceContext), proxy/load balancer stripping unknown HTTP headers, library version mismatch (SDK and auto-instrumentation agent versions incompatible), middleware ordering issues (trace middleware after auth middleware intercepts), and missing instrumentation on HTTP client libraries (requests/urllib not instrumented)
B) Propagation only fails when the network is down — it's a connectivity issue
C) Context propagation always works if the Collector is running
D) Failures only occur with gRPC — HTTP propagation is always reliable
✅ Correct Answer: A

The top propagation failures: 1) Wrong propagator — using B3 format while downstream expects W3C. 2) Proxy strips headers — Nginx/Envoy/Istio dropping traceparent as an unknown header. 3) Version mismatch — different OTel SDK versions with incompatible propagation. 4) Middleware ordering — auth middleware creates its own trace before OTel middleware runs. 5) Uninstrumented HTTP client — using raw urllib without OTel instrumentation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Top 5 Propagation Failure Causes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Wrong Propagator: SDK=A(W3C), Service B expects(B3) → ❌    │
  │  2. Proxy Strips: Nginx removes traceparent as unknown → ❌     │
  │  3. Version Mismatch: SDK 1.10 (new format) vs SDK 1.5 (old) →❌│
  │  4. Middleware Order: Auth MW creates span BEFORE OTel MW → ❌  │
  │  5. Uninstrumented Client: raw urllib.get() → no headers → ❌   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The most common failure was #2 — Nginx ingress stripping traceparent. Fixed by adding nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/configuration-snippet: | more_set_headers "traceparent";.

Debugging Q184 of 200
How do you test context propagation end-to-end?
A) Run all services and check if Jaeger shows anything — if yes, propagation works
B) Only the OpenTelemetry Operator can test propagation automatically
C) Inject a known traceparent header with curl (trace ID you generate), call the full service chain, and verify in Jaeger that ALL spans share the same trace ID. This is a deterministic test — you control the trace ID and can trace it through every service
D) Propagation testing requires a commercial observability platform
✅ Correct Answer: C

End-to-end propagation testing: 1) Generate a known trace ID (e.g., openssl rand -hex 16). 2) Construct a traceparent header with this ID. 3) Send a curl request with this header to the entry point. 4) Search for this trace ID in Jaeger. 5) Verify ALL expected service spans appear under the same trace. This is deterministic — you KNOW the trace ID and can prove complete propagation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
E2E Propagation Test
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ TRACE_ID=$(openssl rand -hex 16)                              │
  │  $ SPAN_ID=$(openssl rand -hex 8)                                │
  │  $ curl -v -H "traceparent: 00-${TRACE_ID}-${SPAN_ID}-01" \     │
  │         -H "tracestate: test=true" \                             │
  │         http://anihpj-web/jobs/                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Then in Jaeger: search trace_id=${TRACE_ID}                     │
  │  Expected:                                                       │
  │  └─ Web Service (our injected parent)                           │
  │      └─ API Service (child span — propagated correctly? ✅/❌)  │
  │          └─ DB Query (child of API — propagated? ✅/❌)         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team runs this e2e test as part of CI/CD. A post-deploy smoke test injects a test traceparent, calls /api/jobs/, and asserts in Jaeger that exactly 3 spans (Web→API→DB) appear with the expected trace ID.

Debugging Q185 of 200
How was the anihpj Web→API broken trace diagnosed and fixed?
A) The team gave up on tracing and switched to logs-only debugging
B) Jaeger showed Web and API as separate traces (not linked). Diagnosis: curl with manual traceparent proved Web wasn't forwarding the header → found Nginx ingress stripping unknown headers → fix: allowed traceparent/tracestate in Nginx config → verified in Jaeger: single trace with Web→API→DB spans
C) The database was missing — restoring PostgreSQL fixed the trace links
D) The OpenTelemetry Operator auto-fixed the propagation issue without human intervention
✅ Correct Answer: B

The anihpj team noticed Web and API traces appeared as separate, unlinked traces in Jaeger. Diagnostic flow: 1) Manual curl test proved the API SDK was instrumented correctly. 2) curl from Web Pod showed traceparent was generated but NOT reaching the API. 3) Network inspection revealed Nginx ingress was stripping the traceparent header. 4) Fix: Nginx config updated to preserve traceparent and tracestate. 5) Verification: Jaeger now shows a single trace waterfall: Web → API → DB.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Broken Trace — Before & After
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BEFORE (Broken):                                                │
  │  Trace 1: [Web Service] ← 2 spans, all from Web                 │
  │  Trace 2: [API Service] ← 2 spans, all from API                 │
  │  ❌ No link — Nginx stripped traceparent                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  AFTER (Fixed):                                                  │
  │  Trace 1: [Web Service] → [API Service] → [DB Query]            │
  │           parent              child             grandchild       │
  │  ✅ Full waterfall — traceparent passes through Nginx           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Lesson: When traces are broken, the #1 suspect is the infrastructure BETWEEN services — proxies, load balancers, and ingress controllers — not the SDKs themselves.

4.2 Debugging PipelinesQ186-Q190

Debugging Q186 of 200
What is the systematic pipeline debugging methodology?
A) Start from the application and work forward — check SDK, then Collector, then backend
B) Restart all components and hope the issue resolves itself
C) Start from the BACKEND and work backwards: 1) Is data reaching the backend? 2) Is the Collector exporting? (check exporter metrics) 3) Is the Collector receiving? (check receiver metrics) 4) Is the SDK exporting? (check SDK debug logs). This is faster because you eliminate the biggest unknowns first
D) Check the Collector logs first — all problems are visible there
✅ Correct Answer: C

The back-to-front debugging methodology: Start at the backend (Jaeger/Prometheus/Loki). If data IS there, the pipeline works and the issue is upstream (query/dashboard). If data is NOT there, check the Collector exporter metrics (is it sending?). Then check Collector receiver metrics (is it receiving?). Finally check the SDK (is it exporting?). This eliminates the biggest unknowns first — you don't waste time debugging the SDK if the backend is simply down.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Back-to-Front Debugging Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Step 1: BACKEND — Is data in Jaeger/Prometheus/Loki?          │
  │  ├─ YES → pipeline works, check dashboards/queries              │
  │  └─ NO  → Step 2                                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 2: EXPORTER — otelcol_exporter_sent_spans > 0?            │
  │  ├─ YES → issue is backend connectivity/auth                    │
  │  └─ NO  → Step 3                                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 3: RECEIVER — otelcol_receiver_accepted_spans > 0?        │
  │  ├─ YES → issue is internal pipeline (processors dropping data)  │
  │  └─ NO  → Step 4                                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 4: SDK — Is the SDK exporting? Check OTEL_LOG_LEVEL=debug│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When traces disappeared, checking Jaeger first (Step 1: empty) → Gateway exporter metrics showed 0 sent spans (Step 2: issue is internal) → Gateway receiver metrics showed spans arriving (Step 3: processor dropping) → filter processor config had a typo dropping ALL spans. Fixed in 5 minutes with the back-to-front method.

Debugging Q187 of 200
How do you debug the SDK side of the pipeline?
A) SDKs are black boxes — you can't debug them
B) Only the Collector has debugging capabilities — SDKs are too minimal
C) SDK debugging requires attaching a debugger to the application process
D) Enable OTEL_LOG_LEVEL=debug for verbose SDK logging, add a ConsoleExporter to see spans printed locally, use OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=console to verify export, and check SDK self-metrics (otel.exporter.exported_spans) to confirm data is leaving the process
✅ Correct Answer: D

SDK debugging toolkit: OTEL_LOG_LEVEL=debug prints internal SDK operations (span creation, export attempts, errors). ConsoleExporter prints spans to stdout — immediately visible. OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=console configures export to console without code changes. SDK self-metrics: the SDK emits its own metrics about export success/failure — check for otel.exporter.exported_spans counter.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SDK Debugging Toolkit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Quick SDK Debug:                                                │
  │  $ OTEL_LOG_LEVEL=debug \                                       │
  │    OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=console \                                │
  │    OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=console \                               │
  │    python manage.py runserver                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Output:                                                         │
  │  [DEBUG] SpanProcessor: exporting span "HTTP GET /api/jobs/"    │
  │  [DEBUG] BatchSpanProcessor: batch of 5 spans exported          │
  │  [DEBUG] OTLPSpanExporter: sending to localhost:4317            │
  │  [ERROR] OTLPSpanExporter: connection refused — is Collector up? │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django container's startup command includes OTEL_LOG_LEVEL=info in staging (debug is too verbose for everyday). When debugging, the team temporarily sets it to debug for 5 minutes to trace span export flow.

Debugging Q188 of 200
How do you debug the Collector itself?
A) The Collector has no debugging capabilities — use external network monitoring
B) Add the logging exporter (detailed verbosity) to see exact telemetry flowing through, enable the health_check extension for operational status, access zpages (in-flight span/metric/log counts at :55679), check Collector self-metrics (otelcol_receiver_accepted_spans, otelcol_exporter_sent_spans), and set Collector log level to debug
C) Only the Collector's stdout logs are useful — other tools are deprecated
D) Use a commercial APM tool — the Collector's built-in tools are insufficient
✅ Correct Answer: B

Collector debugging toolkit: Logging exporter (print every span passing through a pipeline). health_check extension (confirm Collector is alive and pipelines are ready). zpages (web UI at :55679 showing in-flight spans, metrics, logs by pipeline — real-time operational visibility). Self-metrics (the Collector emits OpenTelemetry metrics about itself — receiver accepted, processor dropped, exporter sent). Set log level to debug for verbose output.

Collector Debugging Toolkit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  zpages (:55679/debug/tracez):                                   │
  │  Pipeline "traces": 150 spans in-flight, 3,421 processed total  │
  │  Pipeline "metrics": 892 data points in-flight                  │

  │  Self-Metrics (:8888/metrics):                                   │

  │  otelcol_exporter_sent_spans{pipeline="traces"} = 4980          │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team enables zpages in staging (port 55679) for real-time pipeline visibility. In production, they rely on self-metrics scraped by Prometheus for pipeline health monitoring.

Debugging Q189 of 200
What Collector self-metrics should you monitor for pipeline health?
A) otelcol_receiver_accepted_spans (data in), otelcol_receiver_refused_spans (rejected data), otelcol_exporter_sent_spans (data out), otelcol_exporter_send_failed_spans (export failures), otelcol_processor_batch_batch_size (batch efficiency), otelcol_exporter_queue_size (backpressure), and otelcol_processor_memory_limiter_data_refused (OOM risk)
B) Only CPU and memory — Collector self-metrics are unreliable
C) Self-metrics are only available in the enterprise Collector
D) Just check if the Collector process is running — that's sufficient
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Collector exposes comprehensive self-metrics at :8888/metrics. Critical dashboard: accepted vs sent (difference = data being dropped/queued), refused (rate-limited data → scaling needed), queue size (backpressure — rising queue = backend too slow), send failures (exporter errors needing investigation), batch size (tuning efficiency), and memory_limiter refused (OOM risk — add replicas).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Essential Collector Self-Metrics Dashboard
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric                                What It Tells You          │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  otelcol_receiver_accepted_spans      Data arriving ✅          │
  │  otelcol_receiver_refused_spans       Rate limit hit ⚠️         │
  │  otelcol_exporter_sent_spans          Data leaving ✅            │
  │  otelcol_exporter_send_failed_spans   Backend down 🚨            │
  │  otelcol_exporter_queue_size           Backpressure ↑ ↑         │
  │  otelcol_processor_batch_batch_size    Batch efficiency          │
  │  otelcol_processor_memory_limiter_...  OOM risk 🔴              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: A Grafana dashboard displays these 7 metrics for both Agent and Gateway. Alert rule: if otelcol_exporter_send_failed_spans > 0 for 5 minutes → PagerDuty alert.

Debugging Q190 of 200
What are the most common pipeline failures?
A) OpenTelemetry pipelines rarely fail — this is an edge case
B) Only the SDK side fails — the Collector is always reliable
C) Pipeline failures are always caused by the backend (Jaeger/Prometheus) being down
D) Wrong endpoint URL (typo in exporter config), TLS certificate mismatch (mTLS misconfiguration), authentication failure (wrong API key/token), network partition (Collector can't reach backend), and configuration error (processor ordering, invalid OTTL syntax, missing pipeline definition)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Top pipeline failures: 1) Wrong endpoint — typo in exporter URL (e.g., htttp:// instead of http://). 2) TLS mismatch — mTLS configured but certificates don't match between Agent and Gateway. 3) Auth failure — wrong API token or expired credentials. 4) Network partition — Collector can't reach backend due to NetworkPolicy/firewall. 5) Config error — processors in wrong order, OTTL syntax error, or pipeline section missing entirely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Top 5 Pipeline Failure Patterns
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Endpoint:  exporter → endpoint: "htttp://backend:4317" → ❌│
  │  2. TLS:       Agent cert CN != Gateway cert CN → ❌           │
  │  3. Auth:       Authorization: Bearer EXPIRED_TOKEN → ❌        │
  │  4. Network:   NetworkPolicy denies Collector→Jaeger:14250 → ❌│
  │  5. Config:    processors: [batch, memory_limiter] ← WRONG ORDER│
  │                memory_limiter must be FIRST!                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: A recent pipeline failure was caused by a ConfigMap update that accidentally removed the "traces" pipeline section. Result: traces received but never exported. Detected when otelcol_receiver_accepted_spans was normal but otelcol_exporter_sent_spans dropped to zero.

4.3 Error HandlingQ191-Q195

Debugging Q191 of 200
How does the Collector's retry mechanism work?
A) The Collector retries indefinitely until data is sent — there's no timeout
B) The exporter retries failed sends with exponential backoff (start at 5s, double each retry, cap at max_interval like 30s), up to max_elapsed_time (e.g., 300s). Retryable errors include network timeouts and 429/503 HTTP responses. Non-retryable errors (400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized) are dropped immediately
C) Retry is handled by gRPC automatically — the Collector has no retry logic
D) Every error is retried — including 400 Bad Request (malformed data)
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Collector's retry_on_failure mechanism uses exponential backoff: initial_interval: 5s (first retry after 5s), max_interval: 30s (cap max wait), max_elapsed_time: 300s (give up after 5 minutes). Retryable: network errors, timeouts, 429 (rate limit), 503 (service unavailable). Non-retryable: 400 (bad data — retrying won't help), 401 (bad auth — needs token fix).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exponential Backoff Retry Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Retry Timeline:                                                 │
  │  Fail → wait 5s → retry → fail → wait 10s → retry → fail        │
  │       → wait 20s → retry → fail → wait 30s → retry → fail       │
  │       → wait 30s → retry → fail (300s elapsed → DROP to DLQ)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Config:                                                         │
  │  retry_on_failure:                                               │
  │    enabled: true                                                 │
  │    initial_interval: 5s                                          │
  │    max_interval: 30s                                             │
  │    max_elapsed_time: 300s                                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Gateway exporter configured with max_elapsed_time=300s. During Jaeger rolling restart (30s downtime), the Collector retries and successfully delivers all queued spans. If Jaeger is down for >5 minutes, spans are dropped (trade-off: memory vs data persistence).

Debugging Q192 of 200
What is a Dead Letter Queue (DLQ) and how does it work in OpenTelemetry?
A) DLQ is a built-in Collector feature that requires no configuration
B) The DLQ sends failed telemetry to the trash — it's just a delete mechanism
C) A DLQ stores telemetry that failed ALL retry attempts for later analysis/replay. In OTel, this is typically implemented by exporting failed data to a file (file_storage), a Kafka topic (dead_letter_queue), or a cloud storage bucket — allowing operators to investigate WHY data failed and replay it after fixing the issue
D) DLQs are only available in commercial observability platforms
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Dead Letter Queue (DLQ) captures telemetry that failed all retry attempts — data that would otherwise be silently dropped. Implementations: file_storage extension (write failed data to disk), Kafka DLQ topic (dead_letter_queue topic for Ops to consume), or cloud storage (S3/GCS bucket). The DLQ enables operators to investigate failure patterns and replay data after fixing the root cause.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dead Letter Queue Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Normal path: SDK → Collector → Backend ✅                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Failure path: SDK → Collector → Retry(X times) → Still fails  │
  │                                       ↓                          │
  │                              Dead Letter Queue                   │
  │                              (file/Kafka/S3)                     │
  │                                       ↓                          │
  │                              Ops investigates → Fix → Replay    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Failed spans (after 300s of retries) are written to /var/lib/otelcol/dql/ via file_storage. A cron job compresses and archives these daily. Ops can replay DLQ'd spans by copying them to a "replay" directory the Collector monitors.

Debugging Q193 of 200
How does backpressure and flow control work in the Collector?
A) When exporters slow down (slow backend), their send queue fills → processors block waiting for queue space → receivers can't push new data → gRPC flow control signals SDKs to slow down → SDKs buffer locally. This cascading mechanism prevents the Collector from running out of memory and ensures graceful degradation
B) The Collector drops data immediately when backends are slow — there's no flow control
C) Flow control is handled by Kubernetes resource limits, not the Collector
D) Backpressure only works with the memory_limiter processor disabled
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backpressure flows backwards through the pipeline. Slow backend → exporter queue fills → processor blocks → receiver can't accept → gRPC channel backpressure → SDK buffers locally. Each layer has its own buffer and retry logic. This is natural backpressure — no explicit coordination needed. The memory_limiter adds a safety net by refusing data if buffers grow too large.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backpressure Flow — Cascade Direction
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Slow Backend ← Exporter Queue FULL ← Processor BLOCKED         │
  │       ↑              ↑                    ↑                      │
  │       └──────────────┴────────────────────┘                      │
  │            Backpressure flows ← ← ← ←                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Result:                                                         │
  │  • Collector memory stays stable (no explosion)                  │
  │  • SDKs buffer and retry (no data loss in short outages)        │
  │  • System gracefully degrades instead of crashing               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: During a 2-minute Jaeger outage, the Gateway's exporter queue climbed to 80% capacity. Backpressure slowed the Agent's forwarding rate, which triggered gRPC flow control to the Django SDK. The SDK buffered spans in memory. When Jaeger recovered, all queued spans flushed within 30 seconds — zero data loss.

Debugging Q194 of 200
What are the most common data loss scenarios in an OTel pipeline?
A) Data loss is impossible — OpenTelemetry guarantees at-least-once delivery
B) Data loss only happens at the SDK level — the Collector never loses data
C) Data loss is always caused by network packet loss — it's unavoidable
D) Batch processor crash before export (in-memory batch lost), exporter queue overflow without persistent storage, exporter timeout after max_elapsed_time with no DLQ, memory_limiter hard limit rejecting data, and Collector pod OOMKilled by Kubernetes before flushing buffers
✅ Correct Answer: D

Top data loss scenarios: 1) Batch processor crash — spans accumulated in-memory are lost if the Collector crashes before the batch is exported. 2) Queue overflow — exporter queue full with no persistent storage → new data dropped. 3) Retry exhaustion — max_elapsed_time reached, no DLQ configured → data permanently lost. 4) Memory limiter — hard limit rejecting data during traffic spikes. 5) K8s OOMKill — Pod killed before graceful shutdown flush.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Loss Prevention Checklist
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Scenario                Prevention                              │
  │  ──────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  Batch crash → loss      file_storage persistence              │
  │  Queue overflow → drop   Larger queue + memory_limiter          │
  │  Retry exhaustion → drop DLQ (file/Kafka/S3)                   │
  │  Memory limiter reject   Scale out to more Collector replicas   │
  │  K8s OOMKill → loss      Graceful shutdown + file_storage      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team prevents data loss with: file_storage persistence on Gateway (survives pod restarts), DLQ to /var/lib/otelcol/dql (captures retry-exhausted spans), graceful shutdown (30s terminationGracePeriodSeconds), and Prometheus alerts on dropped span counters.

Debugging Q195 of 200
How do you set up alerting on pipeline health?
A) Alerting is handled by the backend — the Collector doesn't need monitoring
B) Monitor the Collector's own /metrics endpoint with Prometheus and create alerts: otelcol_exporter_send_failed_spans > 0 (export failure), otelcol_exporter_queue_size > 80% (backpressure), otelcol_receiver_refused_spans > 0 (rate limiting), otelcol_processor_memory_limiter_data_refused > 0 (OOM risk), and rate(otelcol_exporter_sent_spans[5m]) == 0 (silent data stop)
C) Only alert on CPU and memory — Collector-specific metrics are unnecessary
D) Alerting requires a proprietary observability platform — Prometheus can't do it
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pipeline alerting based on Collector self-metrics. Prometheus scrapes :8888/metrics from every Collector instance. Key alerts: Export failure (send_failed > 0 for 5m → P1), Backpressure (queue_size > 80% for 10m → P2), Rate limiting (receiver_refused > 0 → P2), OOM risk (memory_limiter refused > 0 → P2), Silent stop (sent_spans rate drops to 0 while accepted_spans > 0 → P1 — data's arriving but not leaving).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pipeline Health Alerts
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Alert                         Condition            Severity    │
  │  ────────────────────────────  ───────────────────  ─────────── │
  │  Exporter Failures             send_failed > 0/5m    🔴 P1      │
  │  Queue Backpressure            queue_size > 80%      🟡 P2      │
  │  Rate Limiting Active          receiver_refused > 0  🟡 P2      │
  │  Memory Limit Hit              memory_limiter > 0    🟡 P2      │
  │  Silent Data Stop              sent=0, accepted>0    🔴 P1      │
  │  Collector Down                up == 0               🔴 P1      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: These alerts are configured in Prometheus with AlertManager routing to PagerDuty. The "Silent Data Stop" alert once caught a misconfigured filter processor that was dropping ALL spans — detected within 5 minutes instead of the next business day.

4.4 Schema ManagementQ196-Q200

Debugging Q196 of 200
What is a schema URL in OpenTelemetry?
A) A URL pointing to the OpenTelemetry documentation website
B) The endpoint where the Collector's configuration schema is hosted
C) A versioned identifier (e.g., https://opentelemetry.io/schemas/1.26.0) sent with telemetry that declares which semantic convention version the producer uses. This enables consumers (Collector, backend) to know how to interpret attributes and perform schema transformations between versions
D) The URL of the OpenTelemetry GitHub repository
✅ Correct Answer: C

A schema URL is a versioned identifier (e.g., https://opentelemetry.io/schemas/1.26.0) that accompanies telemetry data. It tells consumers: "This data was produced using semantic conventions version X." This is critical because OTel evolves — attribute names change (http.status_code → http.response.status_code), new attributes are added, old ones are deprecated. The schema URL allows the Collector or backend to apply version-specific transformations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Schema URL — Version Declaration
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Producer (SDK v1.26):                                          │
  │  Schema URL: https://opentelemetry.io/schemas/1.26.0           │
  │  Attributes: http.request.method, http.response.status_code    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Consumer (Collector):                                           │
  │  "I see schema 1.26 — I know how to interpret these attributes" │
  │  Can transform to 1.25 for legacy backends if needed            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django SDK is configured with the schema URL matching its OTel SDK version. When the backend (Jaeger) only supports schema 1.20, the Collector applies schema transformations to convert 1.26 attributes to 1.20 format.

Debugging Q197 of 200
How does OpenTelemetry handle schema versioning and backwards compatibility?
A) Schema changes are breaking — each version requires rewriting all instrumentation
B) There's no versioning — semantic conventions never change
C) Old SDKs automatically get new conventions without any changes
D) OTel uses schema URLs for version declaration. The Collector's schema processor can TRANSFORM between versions (e.g., 1.20→1.26). Deprecated attributes are supported alongside new ones for a transition period (usually 2 major versions), giving teams time to migrate instrumentation
✅ Correct Answer: D

OTel manages schema evolution through: 1) Schema URLs — declare which version produced the data. 2) Schema processor — the Collector can transform data from one version to another (e.g., rename http.status_code→http.response.status_code). 3) Deprecation window — deprecated attributes remain supported for ~2 major versions, allowing gradual migration. 4) Backwards compatibility — new SDKs can still emit old attributes if needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Schema Evolution — Migration Path
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Version 1.20  →  Version 1.23  →  Version 1.26               │
  │  http.status_code  http.status_code   http.response.status_code │
  │  (supported)       (deprecated ⚠️)    (current ✅)              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Transition period:                                              │
  │  • 1.20: http.status_code (primary)                             │
  │  • 1.23: http.status_code → http.response.status_code (BOTH)   │
  │  • 1.26: http.response.status_code (primary)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Schema processor transforms 1.20→1.26 automatically           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When upgrading the Django OTel SDK from 1.20 to 1.26, the Collector's schema processor auto-transforms old http.status_code to http.response.status_code for Jaeger — zero Django code changes needed during the transition.

Debugging Q198 of 200
How do you handle deprecated attributes in your pipeline?
A) Identify deprecated attributes via audit (compare against the current schema), add a Transform processor to convert old→new names (e.g., http.status_code → http.response.status_code), set up monitoring to track deprecated attribute usage rate, and plan SDK upgrades to emit new conventions natively. Deprecation is a GRADUAL process — not a flag day
B) Deprecated attributes must be removed immediately — break everything and fix later
C) Keep using deprecated attributes indefinitely — they never get removed
D) Only the SDK team handles deprecation — Collector operators don't need to worry
✅ Correct Answer: A

Handling deprecated attributes is a gradual migration: 1) Audit — identify which deprecated attributes are still in use (check your Collector or backend). 2) Transform — use the Transform processor to map deprecated→current names in the Collector (no app changes needed). 3) Monitor — track the ratio of deprecated vs current attributes. 4) Upgrade SDKs — when most services are on new SDKs, the deprecated ratio drops. 5) Remove transforms — when the deprecated ratio is near 0%.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Deprecation Migration Workflow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Phase 1: 80% deprecated → Add Collector transform mappings    │
  │  Phase 2: 50% deprecated → Upgrade SDKs gradually              │
  │  Phase 3: 20% deprecated → Alert on remaining deprecated attrs │
  │  Phase 4: <5% deprecated → Remove Collector transforms         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Example transform:                                              │
  │  - set(attributes["http.response.status_code"],                  │
  │         attributes["http.status_code"])                          │
  │    where attributes["http.status_code"] != nil                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team tracks deprecated attribute usage via a Collector metric. When upgrading SDK versions, the Collector transform maps old→new until all Django pods are on the new SDK. Then the transform is removed.

Debugging Q199 of 200
How does the Collector handle schema transformations between versions?
A) Schema transformations are manual — you must write custom Go plugins
B) The schema processor (or Transform processor) can auto-convert telemetry between schema versions. You specify a target schema URL, and the processor renaming/deprecating attributes according to the OTel schema evolution file. Non-standard transformations use OTTL in the Transform processor
C) Schema transformation requires completely reprocessing historical data
D) Only the backend (Jaeger/Prometheus) can perform schema transformations
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Collector can perform schema transformations using the built-in schema processor or the Transform processor with OTTL. The schema processor reads OTel's official schema evolution file and applies version-specific renames/deprecations automatically. For custom transformations or when the schema processor doesn't cover a specific change, the Transform processor with OTTL provides fine-grained control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Schema Transformation in the Collector
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Producer (schema 1.20) → Collector                              │
  │    http.status_code: 200                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Schema Processor (target: 1.26):                               │
  │    → rename http.status_code → http.response.status_code        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Transform Processor (custom OTTL):                              │
  │    → add deployment.environment (not in schema)                 │
  │    → hash user.id (PII handling)                                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Gateway schema processor auto-converts older SDK attributes (schema 1.20) to the current standard (1.26). Additional custom transformations (PII hashing, environment tagging) are handled by the Transform processor.

Debugging Q200 of 200
How did anihpj handle the schema evolution from http.status_code to http.response.status_code?
A) The team rewrote all 50 Django views to emit the new attribute name
B) They ignored the deprecation — old attribute names still work
C) Phase 1 (immediate): Collector Transform processor mapping http.status_code→http.response.status_code (zero code changes). Phase 2 (1 month): Upgraded Django OTel SDK to 1.26 (now emits new convention natively). Phase 3 (2 months): Monitored deprecated attribute ratio dropping to <1%. Phase 4 (3 months): Removed the Collector mapping. Result: zero backend queries broke during the 3-month transition
D) They switched to a different observability framework to avoid the schema change
✅ Correct Answer: C

The anihpj team executed a 4-phase schema migration. Phase 1: Immediate Collector transform (http.status_code→http.response.status_code) — zero Django changes, zero downtime. Phase 2: Gradual SDK upgrade (rolling update, one pod at a time). Phase 3: Monitoring deprecated attribute usage. Phase 4: Remove transforms when deprecated ratio <1%. This is the OTel-recommended gradual migration pattern.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Schema Migration — 4 Phases
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Phase 1 (Day 0):  Collector transform adds both attributes     │
  │  Phase 2 (Week 4): SDK upgrade → 80% emit new, 20% old         │
  │  Phase 3 (Week 8): Monitor — deprecated <1%, only legacy pods  │
  │  Phase 4 (Week 12): Remove transform — all pods on new SDK     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Grafana query continued working throughout — no breaking change│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key lesson: Never make schema changes a "flag day." Use the Collector as a compatibility layer. Transform in the pipeline, upgrade SDKs gradually, monitor the transition, and remove transforms only when deprecated usage approaches zero.
